xxv
Foreword
Through years of steady effort, the Philippines has made significant strides in 
expanding access to education. Landmark reforms such as the Enhanced Basic 
Education Act of 2013 and the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education 
Act have reshaped the education landscape. Strategic investments in teacher 
development, school infrastructure, and social support programs have contributed 
to gains in enrollment, retention, and participation rates. Yet, despite these 
achievements, deeply rooted challenges persist. Learning poverty remains 
widespread, infrastructure gaps endure, regional disparities affect equity, and a 
disconnect between education outcomes and labor market demands hinders 
long-term progress.
These challenges are taking place in the context of a rapidly changing world. 
Technological disruption, shifting labor markets, and widening social inequalities 
are redefining the demands placed on education systems. The task ahead is no 
longer just about expanding access but also about ensuring that education is 
high-quality, inclusive, and aligned with the diverse needs of Filipino learners.                           
Navigating these complex and evolving demands requires evidence-based reform 
and sustained collaboration among government, educators, communities, and 
the private sector.
This volume brings together a collection of rigorous studies conducted by 
researchers at the Philippine Institute for Development Studies (PIDS), offering 
data-driven insights and policy recommendations to make the country’s education 
system more inclusive, equitable, and transformative. These studies examine 
long-standing issues across basic, higher, and technical-vocational education, 
including the low performance of Filipino students in international assessments, 
persistent regional and gender disparities in educational attainment, and the 
challenges surrounding teaching quality.
They also assess the adequacy and impact of public investments and 
programs such as the K–12 curriculum, Mother Tongue-Based Multilingual 
Education, and the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act. Further 
attention is given to student financial aid, the education effect of the Pantawid 
Pamilyang Pilipino Program, school feeding programs, and infrastructure 
provision. Together, these studies provide a nuanced and interconnected view 
of the Philippine education system—its progress, its pain points, and the critical 
reforms required to move forward.
At PIDS, we remain committed to supporting education reform through 
evidence-based research and informed dialogue. We sincerely hope that the analyses 
and findings in this volume will serve as a valuable resource for shaping policies 
and practices that are responsive to the needs of today’s learners and tomorrow’s 
economy. We invite policymakers, educators, parents, and the private sector to 
work together toward a shared vision: a Philippine education system that is not 
only accessible but also empowering and transformative for every learner.
ANICETO C. ORBETA JR.
President 
Philippine Institute for Development Studies
=== PAGE 27 ===

=== PAGE 28 ===
xxvii
Preface
This volume brings together a collection of studies examining critical 
dimensions of the Philippine education system—ranging from student learning 
outcomes and teacher quality to school infrastructure, financing mechanisms, 
and the alignment of education with labor market needs. Developed as part 
of an ongoing research initiative to support evidence-informed education 
policy, the studies reflect a shared commitment to better understanding the 
developmental milestones and systemic gaps that influence the performance 
of Philippine basic and tertiary education.
The motivation for this collection stems from the increasing 
demand for rigorous, policy-relevant research that can support national                     
and local efforts to improve the quality, equity, and relevance of education. 
By offering empirical insights into persistent challenges as well as areas 
of progress, this book aims to contribute to a more strategic and inclusive 
dialogue on education reform in the country. Each chapter provides 
analytical perspectives that can inform both short-term interventions and 
long-term transformation.
The volume is organized thematically, with chapters addressing key 
areas such as foundational learning, teaching capacity, financing trends, 
assessment and accountability, and education-employment linkages. 
While diverse in focus, the studies collectively underscore the need for a 
coordinated and forward-looking approach to improving the education 
system’s performance and responsiveness.
We hope that the findings and recommendations presented in this 
volume will serve not only as a guide for reform-minded policymakers 
and program designers, but also as a resource for educators, researchers, 
civil society actors, and private sector partners working toward education 
transformation. It is our belief that through collaborative effort and 
evidence-based action, we can help shape a more inclusive, resilient, and 
future-ready education system for all Filipinos.
ANICETO C. ORBETA JR. 
MICHAEL R.M. ABRIGO
VICENTE B. PAQUEO

=== PAGE 33 ===
2
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
This book compiles recent studies by researchers from the Philippine 
Institute for Development Studies (PIDS) addressing critical aspects of 
the Philippine education system. This integrative chapter aims to provide 
a comprehensive overview and synthesis of the key themes, findings,                      
and recommendations from the contributed chapters. By weaving together 
these diverse perspectives, this chapter highlights the interconnectedness of 
the issues and suggests a unified path forward for improving education in 
the Philippines.
The book explores the challenges and opportunities within the 
Philippine education system, offering data-driven recommendations for 
policy reforms to enhance educational outcomes at all levels. It examines 
the impact of educational policies, the factors influencing student 
performance, and the role of various stakeholders in shaping the education 
landscape. The book’s evidence-based approach is key, using data and 
analysis to inform policy recommendations for improving the education 
system. The chapters draw on diverse data sources, including national 
and international assessments, enrollment figures, policy documents, and 
stakeholder interviews.
The Philippine education system has undergone significant 
transformations, influenced by various policies and socioeconomic factors. 
Key legislative measures have shaped the educational landscape, such 
as the Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2013 and the Universal Access                            
to Quality Tertiary Education Act. Despite these efforts, quality, equity,                
and access challenges persist, requiring thorough examination and                 
targeted interventions.
The book emphasizes the need for quality improvement, equitable 
access, data-driven decision-making, and stakeholder collaboration. The 
overall message calls for comprehensive and context-sensitive reforms to 
address the Philippine education system’s multifaceted challenges and to 
enhance educational outcomes for all students.
=== PAGE 34 ===
3
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Summary of contributed chapters
Chapter-by-chapter overview
Orbeta and Paqueo (Chapter 1) provide a comprehensive overview of 
the Philippine education system, focusing on basic education, higher 
education, and technical and vocational education and training (TVET). 
They examine the impacts of key legislative acts and identify critical 
issues such as the quality of basic education, higher education, and TVET 
programs. Through literature review, data analysis, and research synthesis, 
the chapter explores issues like the K-12 [from kindergarten to Grade 12] 
curriculum, Mother Tongue-Based Multilingual Education (MTB-MLE) 
implementation, student performance in international assessments, 
and challenges in higher education and TVET. It emphasizes the need 
for data-driven, context-sensitive reforms to address learning gaps and 
improve educational outcomes. The chapter reveals significant learning 
gaps in basic education, uneven quality in higher education, and the 
need for stronger industry linkages in TVET. Recommendations include 
prioritizing quality improvement, enhancing transparency, and utilizing 
technology for personalized learning.
Orbeta and coauthors (Chapter 2) investigate the factors influencing 
Filipino students’ test performance in the Program for International Student 
Assessment 2018, which showed low rankings in reading, mathematics, 
and science. The analysis considers individual, household, and school 
characteristics using the “education production function” framework. Data 
from a sample of 7,233 15-year-old students, analyzed using ordinary least 
squares and quantile regressions, reveal significant correlations between 
student performance and factors such as grade repetition, bullying, parental 
education, and school resources. Recommendations include addressing 
grade repetition, promoting parental support, and fostering a positive 
school environment.
Dacuycuy and Bayudan-Dacuycuy (Chapter 3) examine social mobility 
and educational inequality in the Philippines from an intergenerational 
perspective. Using data from the 2010 Census of Population and Housing, 
the study focuses on five-year cohorts born between 1960 and 1984. It 
measures intergenerational education mobility with linear regression and 
=== PAGE 35 ===
4
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
assesses educational inequality using the Gini index. The findings show 
high mobility in regions like the National Capital Region and Cordillera 
Administrative Region but low mobility and persistent educational 
challenges in regions like the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region in Muslim 
Mindanao (BARMM). The study calls for targeted policy interventions 
to address regional and gender disparities and the promotion of equitable 
access to education.
Albert (Chapter 4) examines the role of public school teachers in 
educational quality, focusing on how incentives affect teaching quality in 
the Philippines. A mixed-methods approach, including literature review, 
data analysis, focus groups, and interviews, reveals an increase in the 
teaching workforce, improved salaries, and more professional development 
opportunities. However, challenges remain, such as heavy workloads, 
non-teaching responsibilities, and ineffective incentive mechanisms. 
Recommendations include providing administrative support, refining 
incentive systems, and enhancing professional development.
Navarro (Chapter 5) assesses the status of school infrastructure in the 
Philippines, focusing on issues such as classroom congestion, electrification, 
access to digital technology, and water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) 
facilities. Benchmarking against other East and Southeast Asian countries 
using data from the Department of Education, Philippine Statistics Authority, 
and United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization, 
the chapter reveals significant infrastructure gaps and regional disparities. 
Recommendations include increased investment, policy improvements, 
public-private partnerships, and better monitoring.
Abrigo (Chapter 6) analyzes education spending patterns in the 
Philippines, examining the drivers and impacts on schooling quality using the 
National Transfer Accounts (NTA) framework. It compares the Philippines 
with regional peers and uses regression and stochastic frontier models to 
assess spending efficiency. While education expenditures have grown, the 
Philippines lags behind regional peers in per-student public spending. The 
study finds that higher spending correlates with better student performance, 
but the efficiency of converting spending into outcomes is lower than in 
other countries. Recommendations include increasing spending, improving 
efficiency, and promoting equity in resource allocation.
Orbeta and coauthors (Chapter 7) explore the impact of the K-12 
reform, specifically the Senior High School (SHS) program, on student 
=== PAGE 36 ===
5
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
employability in the Philippines. The chapter presents insights from    
three studies: a process evaluation of the K-12 program, a qualitative study 
on the alignment between the SHS curriculum and labor market needs, 
and an analysis of labor force survey data on SHS graduates’ employment 
outcomes. Findings highlight challenges in curriculum alignment with 
industry needs, limited employer confidence in SHS graduates, and mixed 
labor market outcomes. The chapter recommends ongoing curriculum 
reviews, strengthening work immersion programs, and further research on 
factors influencing SHS graduates’ employment outcomes.
Monje and coauthors (Chapter 8) evaluate the implementation 
of the MTB-MLE program in the Philippines using a process evaluation 
methodology. The study finds that while the program has a high 
implementation rate, the extent of its application varies, with resource 
and training gaps hindering effectiveness. It positively impacts student 
engagement, but challenges exist in aligning the medium of instruction 
with students’ actual mother tongues. Recommendations include improving 
information dissemination, refining program design to address linguistic 
diversity, and enhancing teacher training and resources.
Ortiz and coauthors (Chapter 9) evaluate the initial implementation 
of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act or Republic 
Act (RA) 10931 in the Philippines. The study identifies challenges related 
to policy clarity, resource adequacy, and administrative processes using a 
process evaluation methodology, including a theory of change framework, 
desk review, online surveys, interviews, and administrative data analysis. 
Recommendations include strengthening monitoring mechanisms, managing 
enrollment capacity, leveraging subsidies for quality improvement, and 
reassessing program design for equity.
Silfverberg and Orbeta (Chapter 10) focus on the Student Grants-in-Aid 
for Poverty Alleviation (SGP-PA) and its expanded version (ESGP-PA), 
examining their impact on access to higher education and the academic 
performance of grantees from poor households. Using data from eight state 
universities and colleges, the study compares grantees with non-beneficiary 
peers, employing regression analysis to control for confounding factors. 
Findings indicate that grantees often start with academic disadvantages but 
show improvement over time. Challenges include admission flexibility, 
limited administrative capacity, and the need for academic support, 
particularly in the initial stages. Recommendations emphasize the importance                
=== PAGE 37 ===
6
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
of rigorous admission processes, data management, tailored support 
services, interagency collaboration, and adequate resource allocation.
Orbeta, Melad, and Araos (Chapter 11) evaluate the impact of the 
Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino  Program (4Ps) on the educational outcomes 
of children from beneficiary households. Using a regression discontinuity 
design, the study estimates program effects on school enrollment, attendance, 
child labor, and household educational investments. The program has 
significantly improved school enrollment for older children (12–17 years old)                                                                  
but has had minimal impact on younger children and no statistically 
significant effect on child labor. Recommendations include refocusing 
educational grants, monitoring learning outcomes, addressing child labor 
concerns, and improving education access in rural areas.
Albert (Chapter 12) evaluates the School-Based Feeding Program 
(SBFP) and its effectiveness in improving pupils’ nutritional status, 
attendance, and health behaviors. A mixed-methods approach, including 
surveys, interviews, focus group discussions, and analysis of nutritional 
data, finds that the SBFP positively impacts the nutritional status of 
severely wasted pupils. However, the sustainability of the gains remains a 
concern. The program has effectively maintained high attendance rates. 
Recommendations include standardizing equipment, reviewing nutrition 
goals, increasing the food budget, extending the feeding cycle, and integrating 
the program with other poverty reduction initiatives.
Common themes, trends, and patterns
Several common themes, trends, and patterns emerge across the chapters:
• Education quality improvement. Many chapters emphasize the 
need to enhance the quality of education in the Philippines. 
This includes improving teacher quality through professional 
development and reduced workload, aligning the curriculum 
with labor market needs, and addressing regional disparities in 
educational infrastructure and resources.
• Equitable access to education. Ensuring equal access to quality 
education for all Filipino students, regardless of socioeconomic 
background or location, is a recurring theme. Chapters 
highlight disparities in educational outcomes across regions 
and socioeconomic groups, stressing the need for targeted            
interventions to bridge these gaps. Initiatives like the SGP-PA,                                                                                                                     
ESGP-PA, and the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary 
=== PAGE 38 ===
7
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Education (UAQTE) Act reflect the government’s commitment to 
improving educational equity.
• Imperative for increased and efficient investment. Several chapters 
stress the crucial need for amplified education investments, 
encompassing financial resources and strategic allocation. This 
involves not only augmenting overall spending on education 
but also ensuring the efficient use of these resources to yield 
tangible improvements in educational outcomes. Comparisons 
with regional counterparts reveal that the Philippines lags in 
per-student public spending, particularly at the primary and 
secondary levels.
• Data-driven policymaking. Several chapters underscore the 
importance of using data and evidence to inform policy decisions 
and evaluate the effectiveness of educational interventions. 
Rigorous methodologies, such as impact evaluations and 
national-level surveys, highlight the commitment to evidence-based 
policymaking in the education sector.
• Crucial role of teachers. Teachers’ pivotal role in shaping 
educational outcomes is consistently highlighted in several 
chapters. Factors such as teacher quality, incentives, professional 
development opportunities, and workload significantly impact 
the overall effectiveness of the education system. Addressing 
these factors is crucial to improving instruction quality and 
student learning experiences.
• Role of stakeholders. The importance of collaboration among 
various stakeholders, including the government, educational                  
institutions, teachers, parents, and the private sector, is evident 
throughout the book. The chapters emphasize the need for shared 
responsibility to improve the education system and achieve 
desired outcomes. For example, the success of initiatives like the 
SHS program relies heavily on partnerships between schools, 
local governments, and private companies.
• Impact of specific policies and programs. The book examines the 
impact of specific educational policies and programs, such as the 
K-12 program, the 4Ps, SGP-PA, and the School-Based Feeding 
Program. These analyses provide valuable insights into the 
effectiveness of these initiatives and offer recommendations               
for improvement.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Synthesis and analysis
Integration of ideas
The ideas presented across the different chapters interrelate and 
complement each other by highlighting the multifaceted challenges and 
opportunities within the Philippine education system. Several overarching 
themes connect the findings and recommendations of these chapters.
• Education quality and inequality. Chapters 2, 3, 4, and 6 emphasize 
the persistent issue of education quality in the Philippines, despite 
efforts to expand access. Chapter 2 links student performance 
with individual, household, and school characteristics, revealing 
the influence of factors like grade repetition, parental support, 
and school climate. Chapter 3 extends this by exploring social 
mobility through an intergenerational lens, uncovering regional 
disparities in educational mobility, and highlighting the persistent 
influence of socioeconomic background. Chapter 4 focuses on 
teacher quality, examining incentives, professional development, 
and workload, suggesting that improving teacher effectiveness 
is crucial for better learning outcomes. Chapter 6 examines the 
connection between education spending and quality, revealing 
that despite increased spending, the Philippines lags in per-student 
public spending compared to regional peers, which impacts 
overall student performance.
• Impact of K-12 reform. Chapters 6 and 7 focus on specific aspects 
of the K-12 educational reform. Chapter 6’s analysis of education 
spending trends using NTA data provides a broader context for 
understanding the financial resources allocated to education. 
Chapter 7 explicitly examines the effectiveness of the Senior High 
School (SHS) program in preparing students for the workforce. 
Findings from process evaluations, qualitative studies, and labor 
force surveys reveal challenges in aligning the SHS curriculum 
with labor market needs and highlight the need for stronger 
partnerships with industries.
• Addressing access and equity. Chapters 8, 9, and 10 concentrate 
on programs to improve access to education for disadvantaged 
groups. Chapter 8 evaluates the UAQTE (RA 10931), revealing 
implementation challenges and suggesting refinements to ensure 
=== PAGE 40 ===
9
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
equitable access and program sustainability. Chapter 9 examines                   
the SGP-PA, highlighting its successes in increasing higher 
education access for low-income students while identifying 
areas for improvement in selection, support, and program 
administration. Chapter 10 evaluates the 4Ps, demonstrating 
its positive impact on school enrollment, particularly for older 
children, and suggesting adjustments to address child labor.
• Importance of school infrastructure and support programs. Chapters 
5, 10, and 11 underscore the importance of adequate school 
infrastructure and targeted support programs in improving 
educational outcomes. Chapter 5 emphasizes the need for 
increased investment in school infrastructure, particularly 
to address spatial inequalities in classroom congestion, 
electrification, and access to digital technology. Chapter 10, 
while focusing on the 4Ps, suggests enhancing the availability 
and quality of educational facilities, especially in rural areas, 
to maximize the program’s effectiveness. Chapter 11 evaluates 
the SBFP, demonstrating its positive impact on the nutritional 
status of pupils while also highlighting the need for program 
adjustments to ensure the sustainability of nutritional gains and 
address implementation challenges.
By highlighting these interconnected themes, the chapters reveal 
the complexities of the Philippine education system. Furthermore, they 
collectively call for evidence-based policymaking, increased investment, and 
a focus on equity to enhance educational outcomes for all Filipino students.
Cross-chapter insights
The chapters collectively reveal a complex web of interconnected challenges 
within the Philippine education system. These challenges span various 
levels of education, from basic education to higher education and TVET,                 
and are influenced by factors such as socioeconomic background, geographic 
location, and government policies.
• Importance of early intervention. Chapters 2, 3, and 11 highlight                                                        
the significance of early intervention in a child’s educational 
journey. Chapter 2 emphasizes that factors such as the age at 
which a child starts schooling and grade repetition correlate 
=== PAGE 41 ===
10
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
significantly with test scores, suggesting that early intervention                       
is crucial for improving educational outcomes. Chapter 3 
underscores that socioeconomic status and family background 
significantly influence educational outcomes across generations, 
emphasizing the need for interventions that address these 
disparities from an early age. Chapter 10, focusing on the 
Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program, finds that the program 
successfully improved school enrollment among older children 
but had minimal impact on younger children, suggesting a need 
to refocus educational interventions for younger age groups.
• Teacher quality and support. Chapters 4 and 6 underscore the 
critical role of teachers in ensuring quality education. Chapter 4 
highlights that while the Philippines has seen an increase in 
its teaching workforce, challenges remain regarding teacher 
workload, non-teaching responsibilities, and the effectiveness 
of professional development programs. Chapter 6 echoes these 
concerns, emphasizing that simply increasing education spending 
is insufficient without addressing the efficiency of resource 
allocation and the need for quality improvement in teaching.
• Policy interventions that account for contextual realities. Several 
chapters emphasize the need for policy interventions sensitive 
to the unique context and challenges of the Philippines. For 
instance, the implementation of the Mother Tongue-Based 
Multilingual Education (MTB-MLE) program, while grounded 
in sound educational principles, faces challenges due to the 
country’s exceptional linguistic diversity (Chapter 8). Similarly,  
the push for increased digital technology access in education must 
contend with the realities of unequal electrification rates and 
infrastructure limitations across different regions (Chapter 5).
• Ensuring equitable access while addressing disparities. Chapters 3, 
5, 7, and 9 emphasize the persistent challenge of ensuring 
equitable access to quality education across different regions and 
socioeconomic groups. Chapter 3 reveals significant regional 
disparities in educational outcomes, with regions like BARMM 
lagging behind more developed areas like NCR. Chapter 5 
highlights spatial inequality in school infrastructure, noting that 
some regions face classroom overcrowding and a lack of essential 
=== PAGE 42 ===
11
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
facilities like electricity and WASH. Chapters 7 and 8, which 
focus on SHS and the UAQTE Act, respectively, indicate that 
while these initiatives aim to improve access, challenges remain 
in ensuring that all students, regardless of background, have equal 
opportunities to benefit from these programs.
• Aligning education with labor market needs. Chapters 6 and 7 
underscore the need to better align the Philippine education 
system with labor market demands. Chapter 6 emphasizes the 
importance of increasing per capita education spending and 
improving the efficiency of education spending to enhance 
educational outcomes that translate into better employment 
prospects. Chapter 7, focusing on SHS, reveals a disconnect 
between the curriculum and labor market needs, with employers 
expressing reluctance to hire SHS graduates and students feeling 
unprepared for the workforce.
Several chapters propose complementary recommendations, suggesting  
a holistic approach to education reform:
• Data-driven decision-making. Chapters 2, 6, 9, and 10 highlight the 
significance of data collection, analysis, and monitoring to inform 
policy decisions and track program effectiveness. These chapters 
collectively emphasize that evidence-based decision-making is 
crucial for enhancing the education system.
• Investing in infrastructure and resources. Chapters 5 and 9 
underscore the necessity for increased investment in school 
infrastructure and resources. They propose that addressing 
infrastructural gaps, such as providing adequate classrooms, 
electricity, and WASH facilities, is essential for creating a 
conducive learning environment.
• Targeted interventions and support systems.  Chapters 2, 8, 9, 
and 10 stress the importance of targeted interventions and 
support systems to address the diverse needs of students. These 
recommendations include providing bridging programs, academic 
support, improved health services, and financial assistance.
These interconnected challenges and shared recommendations 
highlight that improving the Philippine education system requires a 
=== PAGE 43 ===
12
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
multifaceted approach involving policymakers, educators, parents, 
and the private sector. By understanding the interconnected nature of                                  
these issues and implementing comprehensive reforms, the Philippines 
can move toward a more equitable and high-quality education system.
Gaps, contradictions, and areas of debate
While the chapters provide comprehensive analyses, certain gaps and 
contradictions emerge. These are summarized below:
• Contradiction between increased spending and efficiency.  While 
Chapters 5 and 6 highlight the need for increased education 
spending in the Philippines, Chapter 6 also emphasizes the 
importance of spending efficiency. This focus on efficiency 
arises from the observation that simply increasing expenditures 
does not guarantee improved educational outcomes. There’s a 
clear distinction between “how much” is spent and “how well” it 
is spent.
• Contradiction on access, quality, and equity. Several chapters reveal 
a nuanced contradiction between access to education and the 
quality and equity of education in the Philippines. While the 
country has made significant strides in expanding access to 
education across all levels, achieving equitable and high-quality 
education for all remains a challenge. Quality and equity issues 
persist, particularly in less developed regions.
• Contradictions in teacher salary competitiveness. One chapter claims 
that public school teacher salaries have surpassed those of their 
private-sector counterparts. However, the same chapter suggests 
that low teacher salaries contribute to challenges in attracting and 
retaining qualified teachers. This discrepancy raises questions 
about the accuracy of salary comparisons and the broader debate 
on teacher compensation.
• Debate on the effectiveness of teacher incentives.  While increased 
teacher salaries are acknowledged, the effectiveness of incentive 
mechanisms, such as the Performance-Based Bonus (PBB), is 
debated. Some argue that the PBB incentivizes performance, 
while others find its implementation problematic due to metric 
manipulation and increased administrative burden. This debate 
=== PAGE 44 ===
13
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
highlights the need for a nuanced understanding of teacher 
motivation and the design of effective incentive systems.
• Gaps in addressing learning outcomes.  Several chapters focus on 
improving access to education, but there’s a noticeable gap in 
addressing the quality of education and learning outcomes. 
While Chapter 6 suggests that higher education spending 
correlates with better student performance, other chapters 
emphasize on inputs (e.g., infrastructure, teacher numbers) without 
directly measuring their impact on learning outcomes. This gap 
emphasizes the need for research and policies that prioritize 
improving the quality of education alongside expanding access.
• Gaps in addressing the digital divide. Although Chapter 5 highlights 
inadequate access to digital technology in Philippine schools, 
and Chapter 1 highlights the need to harness technology                                         
for personalized learning, subsequent chapters do not offer 
specific recommendations to address this digital divide. Given 
the increasing importance of technology in education, the 
lack of targeted interventions to bridge the digital gap raises 
concerns about the Philippines’ preparedness for 21st-century 
learning environments.
These gaps, contradictions, and areas of debate underline the 
complexity of educational reform in the Philippines. They highlight 
the need for comprehensive data collection, rigorous analysis, and                     
evidence-based policymaking to address the multifaceted challenges facing 
the education system.
Implications and applications
The findings have significant implications for practitioners (teachers and 
school administrators), policymakers (legislators, Department of Education, 
government agencies), and other stakeholders (parents, communities, and 
the private sector). Implementing the recommended reforms can enhance 
the quality and equity of education, thereby contributing to national 
development goals.
=== PAGE 45 ===
14
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
For practitioners (teachers and school administrators)
The findings emphasize the need for teachers to focus on their primary 
teaching role, given the substantial workload from nonteaching 
responsibilities. Administrative support should be made available in schools 
to manage nonteaching duties, allowing teachers more time for instruction                
and student development. Additionally, teachers can leverage the insights 
from the PBB scheme to advocate for a more effective incentive system                    
that genuinely rewards quality teaching. School administrators can use the 
research on school infrastructure to advocate for increased investment, 
particularly in areas with high classroom congestion or inadequate   
facilities. They can also apply findings on the SBFP to implement nutrition 
monitoring and support systems within their schools.
For policymakers (legislators, DepEd, 
government agencies)
Policymakers should prioritize increasing per capita education spending to 
match regional standards, including strategies to boost both government 
allocations and private sector contributions. The findings on teacher                
salaries and incentives call for a review and potential revision of existing 
compensation structures and incentive mechanisms. Policymakers should 
consider refining the PBB or exploring alternative approaches that effectively 
reward quality teaching without creating undue administrative burdens. 
Addressing the digital divide in education by developing and funding 
initiatives that expand access to computers, internet connectivity, and digital 
learning resources in schools is crucial. Insights from the K-12 program, 
particularly the Senior High School (SHS) curriculum, highlight the need 
for continuous review and adjustment to ensure alignment with labor 
market demands, including strengthening partnerships with industry to 
provide relevant work immersion experiences for students. 
Policymakers can use insights from the UAQTE Act’s implementation              
to improve its effectiveness, including refining the targeting of 
beneficiaries for the Tertiary Education Subsidy (TES), streamlining  
program guidelines, and addressing technical issues with the TES portal                                                            
and Listahanan database. The findings on the SGP-PA and ESGP-PA 
=== PAGE 46 ===
15
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
programs can inform the design and implementation of future grants-in-aid 
initiatives. Strengthening selection criteria to identify motivated students, 
providing comprehensive academic and non-academic support, and ensuring 
adequate resources to support grantees effectively should be prioritized. 
The impact evaluation of the 4Ps offers valuable insights for refining 
the program’s design and implementation. Policymakers should consider 
increasing educational grants, particularly for older children at higher 
risk of dropping out, and linking these grants to educational milestones. 
Additionally, efforts should be made to address child labor through increased 
grant amounts or targeted interventions. Using insights from the SBFP 
impact evaluation can improve the program’s effectiveness in addressing 
child malnutrition. Recommendations include standardizing equipment 
and training for accurate nutrition monitoring, reviewing and potentially 
extending the feeding cycle, and adjusting the budget to account for inflation                                       
and rising food costs.
For other stakeholders (parents, communities, 
private sector)
Parents can advocate for increased government investment in education and 
hold policymakers accountable for improving educational quality. They can 
also actively support their children’s education by engaging in school activities 
and advocating for their children’s needs. Communities can collaborate 
with local government units and schools to address infrastructure gaps and 
support programs like the SBFP to improve the educational environment 
for children. Understanding the benefits of mother tongue education can 
empower parents to support its implementation and advocate for necessary 
resources. The private sector can contribute to educational development 
through corporate social responsibility initiatives, particularly in enhancing 
digital technology access and infrastructure development. Businesses can 
partner with schools to provide work immersion opportunities and align                 
the SHS curriculum with industry needs.
By understanding and acting upon these practical implications,  
various stakeholders can contribute to building a more robust and equitable 
education system in the Philippines.

Future directions
Research agenda
A research agenda can be drawn from the chapters, covering areas such as 
understanding the learning crisis; determinants of student performance; 
impact of the language of instruction; effectiveness of K-12 reforms; 
access, quality, and equity; teacher quality; improving spending efficiency; 
and comprehensive data for effective monitoring and impact evaluation of 
education programs.
• Understanding the learning crisis.  More research is needed to 
identify the specific factors driving the learning crisis in basic 
education. While Chapter 1 recognizes significant learning                     
gaps, further investigation into the roles of curriculum design,                 
teacher training, pedagogical approaches, and socioeconomic 
factors is essential.
• Determinants of student performance.  A deeper understanding of 
the relationship between specific education interventions and 
student performance is required. Chapters 1, 2, 6, and 8 call                 
for identifying and scaling cost-effective interventions that 
translate into tangible improvements in learning. This involves 
moving beyond simply measuring inputs like spending to 
assessing the actual impact of different teaching methods,                                 
curriculum designs, language of instruction, and school-based 
programs on student achievement.
• Impact of K-12 reforms. More in-depth research is needed 
to evaluate the long-term impacts of the K-12 program, 
particularly regarding its effectiveness in preparing students 
for the workforce. While Chapter 7 acknowledges the initial 
implementation successes of the K-12 program, it also highlights 
the need for continuous evaluation, especially in aligning the 
curriculum with labor market demands. This research includes 
tracking the employment outcomes of SHS graduates over 
time, comparing them to their peers with different educational 
backgrounds, and analyzing the skills mismatch between what 
students learn and what employers seek.
=== PAGE 48 ===
17
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
• Access, quality, and equity. Several chapters highlight the need to 
balance expanded access with quality improvement. This raises 
a key question about effectively ensuring increased enrollment 
translates into better learning outcomes. More research is needed             
to explore alternative tuition schemes and policies that might 
better support lower socioeconomic groups and ensure equitable 
access to education.
• Teacher quality. More research is needed on effectively measuring             
and improving teacher quality. Chapters 2 and 5 indicate that 
simply increasing the number of teachers does not necessarily 
lead to better learning outcomes. This result underscores the need 
for more robust research on defining, measuring, and enhancing                              
teacher quality, including examining the impact of teacher 
qualifications, pedagogical practices, professional development 
programs, incentives, and school leadership on student learning.
• Assessment of the long-term impact of the MTB-MLE program                                   
on students’ linguistic proficiency and academic performance.                          
Chapter 8 highlights the challenges and successes of implementing 
the MTB-MLE program in a linguistically diverse country like 
the Philippines. Future research could focus on the program’s 
long-term effects, examining its impact on students’ proficiency                              
in both their mother tongue and other languages, such as Filipino 
and English. This research could also investigate the program’s 
effects on students’ academic performance across different 
subjects, considering variables such as regional variations in 
implementation, teacher training, and the availability of localized 
learning materials. 
• Improving spending efficiency. How can the effectiveness of education 
spending be maximized to improve learning outcomes, and 
are there specific interventions that offer a better return on 
investment? Chapter 6 notes that despite increased education 
expenditures, the Philippines still lags regional peers in per-student 
public spending, particularly at the primary and secondary levels. 
Coupled with the finding that the Philippines has lower technical 
efficiency in converting education spending into student 
=== PAGE 49 ===
18
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
performance, this stresses the need to evaluate the impact of 
education spending and prioritize interventions that demonstrate    
a high return on investment.
• Comprehensive data for effective monitoring and impact evaluation 
of education programs. More comprehensive data collection and 
analysis are necessary to effectively monitor the progress and impact 
of various education programs. Several chapters emphasize the 
importance of robust data management systems for tracking 
student progress, evaluating program effectiveness, and 
informing policy adjustments. This includes collecting data not 
only on enrollment and attendance but also on learning outcomes, 
nutritional status, and long-term impacts on students’ lives.
Policy and practice
Policymakers should prioritize data-driven and context-sensitive reforms, 
focusing on quality improvement, equitable access, and efficient resource 
allocation. Collaborative efforts between the government, private sector, and 
communities are essential to address the multifaceted educational challenges.
Listed below are areas for further development in policy and practice                
in the Philippine education system:
• Aligning SHS curriculum with labor market needs. Implementing 
the K-12 program, particularly the SHS track, requires further 
development to align with labor market needs. While the program 
aims to enhance employability, there is a disconnect between the 
curriculum and employers’ expectations. Firms often prefer 
college graduates and lack awareness of the SHS program’s 
competency-based approach. Recommendations to address 
this misalignment include continuous curriculum review, 
incorporating teacher and student feedback, and strengthening 
partnerships with industry stakeholders to ensure graduates 
possess relevant skills.
• Improving the effectiveness and sustainability of the SBFP. While                                                                                                                                     
the SBFP has effectively increased school attendance and 
improved the nutritional status of severely wasted pupils, the 
program faces challenges in sustaining these gains. A significant 
proportion of children regress to a wasted or severely wasted 
=== PAGE 50 ===
19
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
status after the feeding cycle ends. The study suggests extending  
the feeding cycle beyond the current 100–120 days and  
complementing the program with interventions that address the 
root causes of malnutrition, such as poverty and food insecurity.
• Refining the implementation of the MTB-MLE program. Chapter 8 
recognizes the program’s potential but emphasizes the need for 
adjustments to address implementation challenges and linguistic 
diversity. Research can focus on (a) reviewing and refining the 
program to better accommodate the realities of simultaneous 
bilingualism in Filipino households, (b) improving teacher 
training by providing more targeted and ongoing professional 
development focused on multilingual education strategies,                        
(c) allocating dedicated funds to create and distribute high-quality, 
localized learning materials, and (d) encouraging schools to 
develop localized strategies that reflect the linguistic diversity of 
their student populations.
• Addressing equity issues in the UAQTE Act. Despite its goal of 
promoting equitable access to higher education, the UAQTE Act’s 
implementation has encountered challenges related to equity. 
Economically disadvantaged students face barriers to accessing         
the program due to factors such as admission test performance                                                            
and administrative hurdles. The study suggests exploring 
alternative tuition schemes and refining the targeting of          
beneficiaries under the TES to ensure the program effectively 
benefits those most in need.
• Learning from SGP-PA and ESGP-PA for interventions aiming to 
increase access to higher education for students from poor households.  
The SGP-PA and its expanded version ESGP-PA have significantly 
improved access to higher education for disadvantaged students. 
However, challenges remain in ensuring the academic success 
of grantees, who often come from underprivileged backgrounds 
and require additional support. Recommendations include 
strengthening academic support services, such as bridging 
programs and counseling, to address knowledge gaps and provide 
holistic student support.
=== PAGE 51 ===
20
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
• Addressing gender disparities in education. The chapter summaries 
reveal gender disparities in education, particularly regarding                                                                                                                 
boys’ underperformance compared to girls. This finding 
highlights the need for gender-sensitive policies and 
interventions that cater to the specific learning needs of boys, 
ensuring they are not left behind in educational attainment. 
Addressing this imbalance requires a nuanced understanding 
of the factors contributing to boys’ lagging performance, which 
may include sociocultural norms, teaching methodologies, and 
curriculum design.
Conclusion
This integrative chapter synthesizes insights from the contributed chapters, 
offering a comprehensive overview of the challenges, innovations, and 
future directions of the Philippine education.
The book thoroughly analyzes the Philippine education system, 
highlighting its complexities, achievements, and persistent challenges. 
A recurring theme throughout the book is the need to balance increased 
access to education with improvements in quality and equity. While the 
Philippines has made commendable strides in expanding educational                                            
access, significant learning gaps persist, particularly in basic education. This 
disparity underscores the fact that simply increasing enrollment does not 
automatically translate to improved learning outcomes. Several chapters 
emphasize the crucial role of teachers in enhancing educational quality. 
However, challenges remain regarding teacher workload, the effectiveness                                             
of professional development programs, and the debate surrounding teacher 
incentives like the PBB. Adequate school infrastructure is also deemed 
essential for creating a conducive learning environment, with regional 
disparities in classroom congestion, electrification, and access to digital 
technology highlighted. 
The book acknowledges the government’s commitment to improving 
educational equity through initiatives like the SGP-PA, ESGP-PA, and                   
the UAQTE Act. However, persistent disparities in educational outcomes 
across different regions and socioeconomic groups indicate the need for 
=== PAGE 52 ===
21
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
targeted interventions and support systems to address the diverse needs 
of students, ensuring that equitable access translates into meaningful 
opportunities for all. 
The book advocates for a multifaceted approach to educational                 
reform in the Philippines, involving policymakers, educators, parents,                       
and the private sector. It stresses the importance of continuous evaluation,               
data-driven decision-making, and increased investment in education to 
address the system’s complexities and work toward a more equitable 
and high-quality education system. The chapter also proposes a research                  
agenda to deepen the understanding of the learning crisis, the determinants 
of student performance, teacher quality, and the impact of MTB-MLE on 
academic performance, as well as to evaluate K-12 reforms and spending 
efficiency. The book calls for comprehensive and context-sensitive reforms 
to enhance the Philippine education system.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
The Philippine education system is currently undergoing intensive changes 
while also facing significant challenges. The passage of Republic Act (RA) 10533, 
or the Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2013, which introduced the senior 
high school, or the K-12 (from Kindergarten to Grade 12) curriculum, has 
required substantial restructuring and an overhaul of the basic education 
curriculum. Additionally, mother tongue-based multilingual education 
(MTB-MLE) was formally introduced in the primary grades. Amid these 
transitions, the dismal results of the country’s first participation in the 
Program for International Student Assessment (PISA) in 2019 and the                 
2019 Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS)                            
have led the public to question the state of the Philippine education system. 
The country maintains high attendance rates at all levels of the 
education system compared to countries with similar development 
statuses. However, it faces the challenge of low overall quality, despite 
producing high-quality graduates who have successfully entered global 
labor markets for decades. Moreover, educational outcomes appear to                                                                           
reflect the socioeconomic status of students rather than serve as an 
e q u a l i z e r .  C o m p o u n d i n g  t h e s e  i s s u e s  i s  t h e  n e w  s e t  o f  c h a l l e n g e s  p o s e d                                                                                                                   
by the forced transition to remote learning due to the COVID-19 pandemic.                  
The country must learn from these experiences, rely more on data, and 
build rigorously validated evidence on what works for the educational 
system, as educational outcomes are highly context sensitive.
Drawing mainly from research conducted by the authors at the 
Philippine Institute for Development Studies (PIDS) and occasionally 
from other authors, this integrative chapter addresses the question,                                          
“What has happened to the Philippine education system?” It describes 
the three education subsectors―basic education, higher education, and 
technical and vocational education and training (TVET)―as to where they 
are in terms of their primary outcomes, the primary reasons for being in                 
their current state, and the recommended ways to move forward.

Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
Basic education
Where are we now?
The country is on par with even richer countries in school attendance rates.                   
Studies show that the Philippines has high school attendance rates. 1 In 
terms of expected years of education completed by age 18, 2 the country is 
comparable to other middle-income countries with a similar gross domestic 
product (GDP) per capita. It ranks even higher than some more progressive 
countries like Malaysia (Figure 1). 
1 Conditional cash transfer data attest to a near-universal attendance rate for the elementary level, even 
among the poor (Orbeta et al. 2021b).
2 Expected years of schooling measures the number of years of school a child progressing through the 
current pattern of enrollment rates can expect to obtain by the age of 18, up to a maximum of 14 years. 
It uses the total net enrollment rate published by the United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural 
Organization Institute for Statistics.

=== PAGE 57 ===
26
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The country is way below its potential in measures of learning. However, 
when the expected number of years of schooling is adjusted for the                          
quality of education received during the years students spend in school,                   
the Philippines has an estimated learning gap of about 5.5 years, 3 which is 
larger than that of its neighbors. This finding suggests that the average 
Filipino student spends more time in school but less productively than 
their counterparts in other countries. This result also highlights that 
schooling does not always translate into learning (World Bank 2018). 4 
The country’s performance in international large-scale assessments (ILSAs) 
confirms it has been experiencing a learning crisis for some time. The crisis is 
further confirmed by the country’s performance in ILSAs, especially 
considering the Philippines’ income level. Filipino 15-year-old students 
performed below expectations in the 2018 PISA, achieving low scores 
in mathematics, science, and reading (Figures 2a, 2b, and 2c). Similarly, 
Filipino Grade 4 students did not fare any better in the 2019 TIMSS. The                
only redeeming factor is that Philippine private schools performed better 
than expected in both assessments, given the country’s income level, 
compared to public schools.
A vast proportion of Filipino students are below minimum proficiency levels. The 
results of the ILSAs reveal that a considerable proportion of Filipino students 
fall below these minimum proficiency levels. The low average scores are 
not due to all students performing poorly; rather, the average test scores 
are dragged down by the overwhelming proportion of students who do not                                                                                                                                          
meet the minimum proficiency levels. In PISA, as shown in Figure 2, the 
country’s average achievement scores in both reading (340) and science (357)                                      
fall at proficiency Level 1a, which is one level lower than the minimum

Education continues to be a worthwhile investment. Despite the challenges 
the Philippines faces in this area, education remains a good investment,

the returns are estimated to be as high as 30 percent for private sectarian

Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
The response to the COVID-19 pandemic reveals the unique challenges 
of remote learning. In 2020, the pandemic posed significant obstacles to 
remote learning, which the Department of Education (DepEd) addressed 
commendably by issuing the Basic Education-Learning Continuity Program 
(DepEd 2020a). DepEd streamlined the K-12 curricula, reducing the original 
14,171 competencies to 5,689, now labeled as the Most Essential Learning 
Competencies. The department also identified several learning delivery 
modes, including (1) face-to-face learning; (2) distance learning, which 
encompasses three types: (a) modular distance learning, (b) online distance 
learning, and (c) TV/radio-based instruction; (3) blended learning; and                   
(4) homeschooling. Additionally, the learning assessments were adjusted to 
“meaningfully support learner development and respond to varied contexts” 
(DepEd 2020b, item 3).
Enrollment data from the DepEd Planning Service indicate that 
most public basic education students are using printed modules (90% for 
elementary, 83% for junior high school, and 80% for senior high school),

while a considerable proportion of private school students are engaged 
in online learning (46% for elementary, 36% for junior high school, and                     
44% for senior high school) (Figure 4). This pattern of selected learning                       
modes reflects (a) the connectivity issues the country is facing and (b) the 
differences in the nature of the clientele in public and private schools. 
These findings, supported by survey data, highlight issues that hinder 
remote learning, especially within low-income households. Key challenges 
include a lack of connectivity and access to computers and parents’ limited 
ability to provide quality educational support, as most heads of poor 
households have only completed secondary schooling. Unfortunately, there 
appears to be no systematic measurement of learning during the pandemic 
that could have shed light on the impact of this large-scale experiment in 
remote education delivery. While some learning is always better than none, 
it is important to recognize the likely differential impacts of remote learning 
delivery. Additionally, these issues compound the problems that existed 
before the pandemic.

=== PAGE 62 ===
31
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
Why are we here?
The Philippine education system has focused on access but has neglected quality.  
While the emphasis on access has led to attendance rates on par with those of 
even wealthier countries, the quality of education has not received adequate 
attention. When the ILSA results were released, many Filipino students 
scored below minimum proficiency levels. This outcome should serve as a 
wake-up call that something needs to be done to improve learning quality.
Test scores are not only influenced by school characteristics but 
are also strongly related to families, peers, and other nonschool inputs 
(Hanushek 2021). They are reliable measures of progress and serve 
as important indicators of a country’s education system performance.                                       
Although assessment tests, such as the national achievement tests (NAT), 
are conducted, the analysis has never been made available for public 
discussion. Education administrators and researchers could have been 
alerted and acted sooner had the NAT results, administered almost every 
year, been released, analyzed, and publicly discussed. 
Expenditure on education is not as high as in our neighboring countries. 
Figure 5 shows the Philippines is not investing as much in education as its 
neighboring countries (as a percentage of GDP). While it is recognized that 
simply increasing resources will not solve education problems, additional 
resources used wisely will ease the constraints on critical inputs that can 
improve quality.
Education policies have undermined better-performing private schools.          
Both PISA 2018 and TIMSS 2019 showed that private schools posted higher 
test scores than public schools. However, instead of harnessing the strengths               
of these better-performing private schools, the education system largely 
ignores the constitutionally mandated public-private sector complementarity. 
This neglect has led to the marginalization of private schools over the 
years, as evidenced by the decline in their share of secondary education. In                                               
School Year (SY) 1970–1971, the share of private schools was 56 percent,                                                                                                            
but it has steadily decreased to 17 percent in SY 2018–2019―a consistent 
decline of almost 1 percent per year. While there are public-private 
partnership programs, namely, the Education Service Contracting for 
Junior High School, the Senior High School Voucher Program, and the                             
Joint-Delivery Program for Senior High School Technical-Vocational-Livelihood 
Specializations, they do not have a well-defined long-term plan needed to 
guide private sector investments (ADB forthcoming).
=== PAGE 63 ===
32
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The reasons for our dismal performance in ILSAs still need to be discovered and 
understood. Determinants of quality and test scores are highly context-sensitive 
(Hanushek 2021). The country’s dismal performance in ILSAs indicates 
that the Philippines needs to do more to improve learning outcomes.                        
There is a need for in-depth analyses of the results of ILSAs. The analysis 
by the World Bank of the three ILSAs—PISA, TIMSS, and Southeast Asia 
Primary Learning Metrics—has highlighted four issues, namely: (a) a safe 
and inclusive learning environment; (b) investment in early childhood 
education and development and foundational skills; (c) strengthening the 
MTB-MLE policy and its implementation; and (d) increasing government 
education spending and equitable allocation (World Bank 2021b). 
The importance of the MTB-MLE policy and its implementation in student 
learning is not clearly understood. The study done by the institute researchers 
highlights the conceptual confusion among implementers and stakeholders 
and the implementation challenges of the MTB-MLE policy (Monje et                                                                                                                                               
al. 2021). Educating a child should harness the critical resource a child

brings into his/her education, in addition to innate ability—his/her 
language of thinking and expression. Evidence has shown that starting a 
child’s education using the language a child understands well is the best 
way to learn second and third languages in the long run (Metilla et al. 2017).
Ways forward
General recommendations
1. Prioritize quality improvement.  Improving education quality is 
imperative, considering the high attendance rates in the country. 
The Philippine Department of Education’s Edukalidad 7 initiative 
aligns well with this goal. However, identifying effective strategies 
to enhance quality remains a challenge. Research suggests the 
importance of school, individual, household, and community 
factors in education (Hanushek 1978; Todd and Wolpin 2003). 
However, local context-specific research is needed to understand 
their impact in the Philippines. Hanushek (2021) cautions against 
generalizing international findings due to unique country-level 
institutional characteristics.
2. Ensure transparency in school performance data.  Public access to 
school quality data, such as test scores and school characteristics, 
is essential. This transparency aids in informed public policy and 
household decisions about education. Similar to labor market 
and national income statistics, regular reporting of educational 
performance is crucial for a robust education system.
3. Establish an independent education statistics body. An independent 
body should oversee the generation and analysis of key education 
statistics, particularly standardized test scores, as suggested by 
Hanushek (2021). This entity, comprised of education experts, 
would manage national achievement tests and release education 
statistics on a fixed schedule, ensuring public access to vital 
educational data.
7 Edukalidad has four reform areas dubbed KITE: (a) K-12 curriculum review and update, (b) improvement 
of the learning environment, (c) teachers’ upskilling and reskilling, and (d) engagement of stakeholders for 
support and collaboration. The first three address school factors, while the fourth addresses household 
and community factors. 
=== PAGE 65 ===
34
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
4. Define and measure quality indicators. Clear quality indicators, such 
as the proportion of students achieving minimum proficiency 
l e v e l s ,  m u s t  b e  d e fi n e d  a n d  u s e d  t o  g u i d e  b u d g e t  a l l o c a t i o n s  a n d                                                                                                                                      
educational reforms. The effectiveness of these reforms in 
improving quality should be regularly evaluated and communicated.
5. Transform DepEd into a learning institution.  The Department of 
Education should evolve from a primarily teaching-focused 
organization to a learning institution for educators and 
administrators. Emphasizing classroom-based research and 
innovative teaching methods will inform policy and improve 
education quality. Schools should collaborate with nearby 
education colleges to establish effective teaching and learning 
strategies, thereby contributing to evidence-based improvements 
in education.
6. Monitor education system performance through sentinel schools.                  
Select representative “sentinel” schools for continuous monitoring 
to provide detailed and nuanced policy design and implementation 
insights, complementing the general administrative data collected. 
This approach can validate policy implementation, such as the 
MTB-MLE policy (Monje et al. 2021).
7. Develop remedial programs for lagging students. Systematic remedial 
programs are necessary for students who are below proficiency 
levels. Research by Broer et al. (2019) and Orbeta et al. (2020) 
highlights the importance of supporting these students to improve 
overall test scores and prevent grade repetition. Programs like 
“Teaching at the Right Level” provide models for addressing 
diverse learning needs, especially considering the pandemic-related 
learning losses in lower-income households.
8. Leverage private schools through vouchers. Expanding access 
to high-performing private schools via voucher programs 
can enhance efficiency and offer more educational choices                       
(ADB forthcoming). 
9. Harness technology for personalized learning. Embracing technology 
to facilitate learner-centered education can cater to individual 
learning styles and paces, as Christensen et al. (2011) noted. This 
approach includes providing diverse content, enabling learner 
control, and offering more personalized feedback.
=== PAGE 66 ===
35
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
10. Clarify MTB-MLE policy objectives. The objectives of the MTB-MLE                         
policy need to be clearly articulated and widely disseminated. 
Addressing both conceptual and implementation issues, as 
discussed by Monje et al. (2021), is vital for effective execution.
Specific recommendations based on Orbeta et al. (2020)
1. Promote parental involvement.  Strengthening parental support, 
particularly for lagging students, is crucial.
2. Improve school learning environments. Address the high incidence 
of bullying and foster a positive disciplinary climate in schools, as 
these factors correlate with better test scores.
3. Review learning time utilization.  Assessing the effectiveness of 
learning time in schools is essential, especially given its surprising 
negative correlation with test scores in PISA data.
4. Reevaluate teacher qualifications.  Understanding the lack of 
correlation between teacher qualifications and test scores is 
necessary and may warrant a reassessment of teacher training 
and certification.
Higher education
Where are we now?
Attendance is high, given the Philippines’ income level. Similar to basic education, 
the Philippines’ attendance in higher education is remarkable considering                  
the country’s income level, with enrollment rates comparable to the average 
of middle-income countries (Figure 6). However, as shown in the figure,                       
this advantage is rapidly eroding as neighboring countries continue to advance 
while the country’s enrollment rate has stagnated.
Quality is uneven, with very few universities ranked among the top 1,000             
in the world. Despite high enrollment rates, the overall quality remains 
inconsistent. Although graduates have been serving in global labor markets 
for decades (King 2020), only a few universities have made it into the                    
top 1,000. The passing rate in professional board examinations is below                                                                                                                          
40 percent. In terms of inputs, only half of the faculty hold graduate                          
=== PAGE 67 ===
36
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
degrees, and less than 20 percent have PhD degrees. Additionally, less 
than 30 percent of higher education institutions (HEIs) have accredited 
programs (Table 1). These results may reflect the ongoing challenges in the 
quality of basic education.
Inequitable access persists. In 2019, while 49 percent of the richest 
decile attended higher education, only 17 percent of those from the poorest 
decile were able to do so. The poor are underrepresented even in public 
HEIs. To resolve this issue, the government enacted the Universal Access 
to Quality Tertiary Education Act (UAQTE) or RA 10931, 8 which provides 
free tuition in public state universities and colleges (SUCs) and local                                                                            
universities (LUCs). It also offers tertiary education subsidies (TES) 
for the poor attending private HEIs. It remains to be seen whether this 
subsidy will increase participation among students from lower-income                                                                                  
households. In the initial assessment of the program’s implementation, 
interviews with higher education leaders revealed doubts about the               
program’s sustainability (Ortiz et al. 2019). Birthing pains were also noted          
in UAQTE’s implementation.
8 This law was approved by former President Duterte over the objections of his economic team.
