=== PAGE 69 ===
38
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The research and innovation system is underdeveloped. The Science, 
Technology, Research and Innovation for Development program 
assessed the Philippine innovation ecosystem by looking at five components: 
(a) education and human capital development; (b) research and knowledge 
creation; (c) collaboration between universities and industry; (d) intellectual                           
property, protection, licensing, and commercialization of technologies;                 
and (e) start-up companies based on technological innovation, using a survey 
of ecosystem stakeholders’ perception (Klich and Dix 2020). From the 
stakeholders’ perspectives, education and human capital development were 
deemed acceptable relative to global standards. However, the assessment 
highlighted that Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM) 
graduates lack exposure to research culture, and the training is overly 
focused on preparing students to pass board examinations.
In terms of university-industry collaboration in research and 
development (R&D), an earlier review revealed that the Philippines is 
still in an emerging stage regarding common forms of collaboration such 
as collaborative R&D, commissioned research, technology licensing, and 
the creation of spin-off companies (Vea 2014). The study recommends                                            
(a) establishing a massive science and technology (S&T) manpower-building 
program, (b) creating a university of science and technology, and                                         
(c) transforming existing public universities into research universities.
In terms of S&T manpower, a PIDS study (Albert et al. 2020) finds 
that the Philippines is not producing enough S&T graduates, and many of                           
these graduates do not pursue R&D jobs or fail to persist in roles requiring                                         
S&T skills. Another study (Orbeta et al. 2016) pointed out that science 
graduates are paid less than their peers in other fields.
Why are we here?
The low quality of higher education can be traced to the low quality of basic education.                   
The inputs in the form of low teacher qualifications also contribute to 
this low quality. The HEIs’ chosen self-regulation mechanisms do not 
adequately cover many of the offered programs. Moreover, poor-quality 
education can be traced (Tan 2011) to the government’s populist policy, 
which expanded the number of universities and their enrollment. Given 
the low income in the country, households and the government can only 
afford low-cost, low-quality higher education.
=== PAGE 70 ===
39
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
The recent changes in public financing programs will not resolve the equity                                    
of access issue. This issue cannot be readily addressed at the tertiary level 
since most low-income students do not reach college. This reality is the 
fundamental reason college education will always be skewed in favor of 
richer households, even assuming the same propensity to enroll in higher 
education across income classes. Increasing access for the poor needs to 
start with basic education and/or large-scale affirmative action targeted at 
students from low-income households with high school diplomas who are 
willing and able to pursue a college education.
Education policy is marginalizing the private sector. The growth of  
SUCs and LUCs is one reason why the private sector has a declining share.             
The expansion of public schools is driven by the satellite campuses of  
SUCs and LUCs. Private schools have also been negatively affected by                                   
the widening salary gap between teachers in public and private HEIs 
resulting from salary standardization interventions affecting government 
HEIs. This policy has caused the migration of faculty from private to public 
HEIs. Additionally, the UAQTE, which provides free tuition in SUCs                                                                                                                                            
and LUCs, is expected to further reduce the private sector’s share. 
The lack of exposure to research culture and academe-industry linkages 
that are still in an emergent stage. Currently, there are no drivers expected to 
spur innovation. An earlier assessment (Vea 2014) revealed that the country 
is in an emergent stage in four forms of industry-academe collaboration: 
collaborative R&D, commissioned research, technology licensing, and the 
creation of spin-off companies.
STEM graduates lack exposure to research culture, and their training               
is too focused on preparing students to pass board examinations (Klich and 
Dix 2020).
Ways forward9  
1. Improve education quality across HEIs. Address the uneven quality of 
education in HEIs by enforcing regulations on program closures 
for consistent failure in board examinations. Promote more 
Centers of Excellence and Development and encourage voluntary 
accreditation to elevate educational standards.
9 Draws heavily from Bautista et al. (2023) 
=== PAGE 71 ===
40
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
2. Enhance equitable access with targeted financing. The free tuition 
policy under RA 10931 needs refinement to address the current                  
and potentially worsening issue of inequitable access (Orbeta and 
Paqueo 2017). The Unified Financial Assistance System for 
Tertiary Education Act (UniFAST), or RA 10687, proposes               
using grants-in-aid for children from poor households, which 
could be a more targeted approach, as indicated by the success 
of the Students Grants-in-Aid Program for Poverty Alleviation 
(Silfverberg and Orbeta 2017). Additionally, the TES component  
of RA 10931, as grants-in-aid to fully fund the education of                
children from poor households regardless of whether they are in 
public or private HEIs, must be implemented as envisioned in the 
UniFAST law. When appropriately executed, TES can be more 
targeted and sustainable than free tuition policies, which currently 
benefit more middle- and upper-class students.
3. Develop a research and innovation ecosystem. Cultivate a research 
culture in Philippine HEIs by addressing challenges such as funding 
and teaching overloads. A functioning innovation ecosystem 
is crucial for developing a research culture, encompassing 
knowledge-producing organizations, regulatory frameworks, 
innovative firms, communication infrastructures, and access to 
global knowledge (World Bank 2002, p.24).
4. Leverage private education sector strengths. Promote complementarity 
between public and private HEIs. Utilize the strengths of the 
private sector, which is known for its agility and cost-effective 
education offerings. Correctly implement the TES for poor 
students in private schools.
5. Publish detailed HEI performance indicators. Regularly release granular 
data on HEIs, including program-level board examination passing 
rates, employment rates, and tuition fees, to inform household 
decisions about higher education.
6. Adapt lessons from the pandemic for remote learning. Draw lessons 
from the COVID-19 pandemic to redefine the role of technology 
in higher education. Factors such as internet connectivity, 
device availability in households, and the effectiveness of lower-tech                        
remote learning approaches for students from low-income 
backgrounds must be considered. The ambition of establishing 
=== PAGE 72 ===
41
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
smart campuses, as outlined in Bayanihan 2 (RA 11494), must be 
balanced with the challenges of internet connectivity and device 
access faced by students. A key lesson comes from a cluster of 
HEIs10 serving low-income students, where a lower-tech approach 
to remote learning—using printed modules and remote coaching 
via low-volume data plans—proved more effective for their needs.
Technical and vocational education and 
training (TVET)
Where are we now? 
There has been consistent improvement in TVET enrollment, graduation, 
assessment, and certification. Enrollment grew to around 2.5 million in 2019, 
up from about 1.6 million in 2010 (Figure 7). Similarly, graduation rose to 
2.2 million in 2019 from 1.3 million in 2010. Additionally, the proportion                           
of graduates assessed grew to about 80 percent in 2019 from a little over                 
50 percent in 2010. Notably, the lack of training regulations prescribing how                    
to conduct competency assessments was one reason for the low assessment 
rates. The figure also reveals that the assessment passing rates are notably 
high, above 80 percent. 
The proportion of graduates from community-based training is steadily 
increasing, while those from enterprise-based mode remain extremely small.  As 
seen in Table 2, institution-based providers dominated in earlier years in 
terms of the distribution of enrolled students and graduates by mode of 
delivery. However, in recent years, community-based training has produced 
a larger share and shown the highest proportion by 2019. This rise in 
community-based enrollees and graduates reflects the increasing emphasis                                       
on the equity objectives of TVET. Another noteworthy trend in enrollment                  
and graduation is the consistently minimal contribution of enterprise-based 
training (EBT) to the overall number of enrollees and graduates (Table 2).              
This situation persists despite widespread recognition that EBT is the      
most responsive to industry needs and the promotion of various schemes 
under this training mode, such as internships and the dual training system.
10 Based on interviews with PHINMA Education executives

=== PAGE 74 ===
43
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
The sectoral distribution of graduates appears to correspond to the 
fast-growing sectors of the economy. Tourism, social, and community                           
development remain in the top three. This result reflects the vigorous growth 
of tourism in the country in recent years (ADB 2021), at least before the 
pandemic. On the other hand, information and communications technology 
has shifted down from the top nine years ago (Orbeta and Esguerra 2016).
The composition and reasons for pursuing TVET have shifted. Traditionally, 
high school graduates who do not want to pursue a university education 
have been the target demographic for TVET. However, the 2019 Study on 
the Employment of Technical Vocational Education and Training Graduates 
covering 2018 graduates indicates that high school graduates are no longer 
the primary students of TVET, as previously observed in Orbeta and 
Esguerra (2016) for 2012 graduates. The largest group of TVET graduates in 
2018 consisted of college graduates and those with higher qualifications (36%), 
followed by university undergraduates (23%). High school graduates from 
the old curriculum and junior high school (JHS) graduates from the new 
curriculum ranked third, accounting for 16 percent.
The commonly cited reason for taking TVET has been employment,                   
as shown in Orbeta and Esguerra (2016). However, recent data show this                  
is no longer the primary motivation, in line with the changing composition                   
of TVET graduates. Data indicate that skills upgrading/enhancement (42%)  
is the most popular reason for taking TVET among 2018 graduates, followed                     
by employment (35%). Notably, for 2012 graduates, skills upgrading ranked 
third at only 7 percent. Additionally, personal use/interest/hobby (15%), 
unrelated to skills upgrading or employment, has gained prominence, 
increasing from a mere 2 percent for graduates in 2012.
Why are we here?
EBT promotion activities are insufficient, and the issues surrounding them remain 
unresolved. The lack of progress in promoting EBT is not due to a lack of 
effort. The country has enacted laws on apprenticeships (Presidential 
Decree 422), dual training (RA 7686), and, more recently, internships                                                      
(RA 10869). However, attempts to understand the underlying incentives 
affecting EBT uptake have been inadequate. EBTs face incentive 
incompatibility issues (Orbeta and Esguerra 2016). On one hand, firms 
are reluctant to finance training if competitors poach trained workers.                                                   
=== PAGE 75 ===
44
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
On the other hand, trainees may lack the resources to pay for their training.                      
The strategic behavior of firms using EBTs to avoid paying commensurate 
wages and benefits is difficult to monitor and enforce. This has resulted in 
an impasse on how to advance the agenda for more EBTs.
Emphasis has been placed on equity to the neglect of the efficiency objective.                    
TVET has the dual objectives of efficiency and equity, as outlined in the 
National Technical Education and Skills Development Plan. The focus on 
equity has promoted community-based training, making this mode dominant 
among the delivery modes. However, while this focus is laudable, it 
should not undermine the pursuit of training that addresses cutting-edge 
technology, especially given the changing labor market needs brought 
about by the Fourth Industrial Revolution.
The pursuit of employable skills and protection from labor market 
changes has encouraged college students and graduates to enroll in TVET. 
The rising proportion of college students and graduates among TVET 
trainees likely reflects their response to the dynamic labor market they are 
currently facing and anticipating. Those with college degrees and higher 
may no longer seek employment but rather aim to add competencies                                       
to their repertoire of skills to enhance their employability in light of changes                    
in the labor market and technologies.
Ways forward
1. Promote EBT. Enhance and address challenges in expanding EBT, 
known for its demand-driven approach and high employment 
rates. EBTs are particularly responsive to job-skill mismatches and 
technological advancements in the Fourth Industrial Revolution 
(ADB 2021; Dadios et al. 2018). Initiatives like TESDA’s “EBT to 
the Max”11 demonstrate the potential for increased private sector 
participation in TVET, aligning it closely with industry needs.
2. Ensure high training quality in community-based modalities. Maintain 
high quality in community-based training, particularly in 
areas where resources may be scarce. As this modality becomes 
increasingly prevalent, TESDA’s technical support is crucial in 
upholding training standards.
11 See, for instance, https://www.tesda.gov.ph/Media/NewsDetail/20145 (accessed on August 2, 2022). 
=== PAGE 76 ===
45
Synthesis of Philippine Education Experience
3. Incorporate 21st-century skills into the TVET curriculum. Embed 
21st-century (or transversal) skills in the TVET curriculum, 
as outlined by UNESCO (2016). These skills, including 
critical thinking, interpersonal and intrapersonal skills, global 
citizenship, and media literacy, are essential for meeting modern 
workforce demands. TESDA’s Circular 097 (s. 2019) mandates 
these skills in basic competencies, necessitating validation of their 
implementation and effectiveness in the curriculum.
4. Balance equity and advanced technology training. Navigate the                
dual objectives of TVET: equipping workers with skills for 
cutting-edge technologies to enhance global competitiveness 
while providing accessible training for new labor market 
entrants and marginalized sectors. This balance requires distinct 
approaches and equal commitment.
5. Develop flexible TVET delivery modes. Enhance TESDA’s capacity 
for flexible TVET delivery to accommodate emergencies such as 
the COVID-19 pandemic and frequent typhoons. This strategy 
includes building infrastructure and teacher capabilities for                            
online content delivery, as evidenced by the challenges and 
solutions identified in the TESDA Online Program and 
YouthWorks Philippines (Orbeta and Corpus 2021; Orbeta et 
al. 2021a).
6. Enhance TESDA’s regulatory and testing capacities. Strengthen TESDA’s 
regulatory and testing capacities to adapt to rapidly changing 
production technologies. This strategy involves organizational 
reforms for quality assurance and the development of strategies 
to foster private sector participation in TVET (ADB 2021).
7. Improve labor force indicators for TVET.  Address gaps in current 
labor force data to better represent the TVET sector, which 
currently underrepresents enterprise- and community-based 
training participants (Orbeta et al. 2021a). Accurate data is crucial               
for a comprehensive understanding TVET’s role in the economy.
8. Regularly publish detailed TVI performance indicators.  Provide 
detailed, training-level information on the offerings and 
performance of technical vocational institutions. Granular data 
on training costs, certification pass rates, and employment rates 
are vital for informed decisions by households and firms regarding 
technical education and hiring.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
Quality education remains a challenge for the Philippines. Even though 
the Philippine Constitution guarantees the provision of quality education 
and the country has attempted to expand access to education for children, 
issues regarding learning quality for Filipino students persist. 
The Philippines’ efforts to promote inclusive education have seen 
notable gains in access and participation, particularly in basic education, 
over the past decades. Interestingly, the UNESCO (2015) report, titled 
Education for All 2015 National Review Report: Philippines, highlighted that 
the country’s basic education quality has been improving. However, it 
is concerning that this trend of improving mean percentage scores based 
on National Achievement Test (NAT) results, spanning eight years (from 
Academic Year 2005–2006 to 2012–2013), remains a few percentage 
points behind the 75 percent mastery target. Thus, concerns over education                                                
quality continue. 
Such concerns led to the introduction of the K-12 (from Kindergarten               
to Grade 12) program through Republic Act 10533 (Enhanced Basic  
Education Act of 2013). Since 2018, the Senate of the Philippines has been 
quite resolute in advocating for a comprehensive performance review 
of the education system at all levels. This concern was heightened by the 
release in December 2019 of the 2018 Programme for International Student 
Assessment (PISA) results by the Organisation for Economic Co-operation 
and Development (OECD).1  
The country’s performance in these PISA results confirms the 
unexpressed suspicion that there is a long-standing problem with the quality 
of education in the country. Among 79 high- and middle-income countries, 
the Philippines ranked near the bottom in Reading and second to last in                     
both Mathematics and Science. These results prompted the formation of the 
Second Congressional Commission on Education, tasked with reviewing 
the issues affecting the education sector. 
1 The 2019 Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) results, in which the 
Philippines participated for Grade 4 students, were also released shortly after the 2018 PISA results. 
The results were similar, showing that the Philippines ranked last among 64 participating countries in 
mathematics and science. However, the raw data for TIMSS has not yet been released as of the writing of 
this paper. The country also participated in the Southeast Asia Primary Learning Metrics testing Grade 5 
students conducted in 2019. Finally, the country participated in the 2022 PISA, the results of which were 
released in December 2023.  
=== PAGE 84 ===
53
Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
Despite numerous pieces of research on the country’s education 
system, there is a glaring lack of studies that directly address a recurring 
theme in Philippine basic education: Where are we in terms of the quality of 
education? What can we do to improve education quality? As a modest attempt 
to contribute to a better understanding of what drives the test performance 
of Filipino students, this chapter focuses on the correlates of student 
performance measured by test scores (specifically on PISA 2018). It aims 
to (a) quantitatively measure the importance of selected individual, family, 
and school characteristics in students’ test performance and (b) compare                          
the correlates of test scores for high- and low-performing students.
Review of relevant literature
Dismal Philippine PISA results versus ASEAN neighbors
The Philippines has participated in several internationally administered 
student learning assessments (see Appendix A for a general comparison of 
international and regional assessments and Appendix B for a comparison 
of these assessments). To date, the DepEd has taken part in various 
international large-scale assessments, including PISA in 2018, Trends 
in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) in 2019, and 
Southeast Asia Primary Learning Metrics (SEA-PLM) in 2019. The results                                              
for PISA were released in December 2019, while the TIMSS and SEA-PLM 
results were made available in December 2020.
The results of the 2018 PISA showed that the Philippines ranked   
second to last in each of Mathematics and Science literacies and last in reading   
literacy among the 79 high- and middle-income countries that participated. 
Figures 1 to 6 illustrate the overall performance of the Philippines in the 
2018 PISA compared to neighboring countries and the OECD average. Also 
shown is the proportion of students achieving Level 2 proficiency—the  
minimum acceptable level. In terms of mean scores and the proportions 
of students achieving different proficiency levels, the Philippines has 
the lowest mean scores and the largest proportion below the minimum 
proficiency levels among neighboring economies in Southeast Asia.

Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
Previous estimations of education quality2  
One common adage states, “It takes a community to educate a child.”                               
The landmark study by Coleman et al. (1966) reported that once a student’s 
family background is controlled for, school resources have little effect on 
student test scores. Several subsequent studies have generally supported this 
insight (e.g., Hanushek 1986, 1996, 2003). However, this assessment is not 
universally accepted (e.g., Hedges et al. 1994).
On the estimation side, measuring the impact of school inputs on 
test scores presents several challenges. Hanushek (1979) provides an early 
discussion of these issues. One concern is the unit of analysis; ideally, the 
analysis should be at the student level, but student-level data is not always                       
available. Other studies aggregate data from the school level to the economy 
level, which can have consequences. Hanushek et al. (1996) were the first 
to highlight the tendency for school factors to exhibit a positive impact at 
higher levels of aggregation.
Another important issue is the appropriateness of using levels. 
Many have argued (e.g., Hanushek 1979) that using levels disregards the 
p r e e x i s t i n g  d i ff e r e n c e s  i n  a c h i e v e m e n t  w h e n  s t u d e n t s  e n t e r  s c h o o l s                                                                                                                                        
and combines these with the gains made while in school. They recommend 
that a better approach is to relate the gains in achievement to school 
resources. This idea is now known as the “value-added” approach. 
Yet another issue is that students are not randomly assigned to   
schools; rather, students self-select into them. Highly motivated parents 
will likely send their children to schools with smaller class sizes and                             
invest more in their children’s education than less motivated parents 
(Webbink 2005).
Finally, while the average estimates obtained from a regression 
on test scores on school, household, and community characteristics are 
informative, researchers have questioned whether there are heterogeneities 
in the responses of top and low-performing observation units. Among                           
the early attempts to answer this question is the work by Eide and                                                                                                                      
Showalter (1998). Using quantile regressions, they find significant differences 
in responses between low-performing and high-performing schools based 
2 Draws heavily from Orbeta (2008) 
=== PAGE 89 ===
58
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
on math scores. The length of the school year was found to be negative 
and insignificant for low-performing schools but positive and significant 
for higher-performing schools. Expenditure per pupil was also found to be 
significant for low-performing schools but not for high-performing schools. 
School enrollment is significantly positive except for top-performing 
schools. The pupil-teacher ratio and the fraction of teachers with advanced 
degrees have little effect on test scores.
Methodology and data
This chapter focuses on the role of individual, home, school, and community 
characteristics in education quality. This study limits its measures of 
education quality to PISA test scores.
Conceptual considerations
The underlying framework commonly used to estimate the determinants                
of education outcomes is the “education production function.” This 
function relates education outcomes, such as test scores, to school inputs 
and individual, household, and community characteristics. There are several 
challenges associated with estimating the education production function. 
Todd and Wolpin (2003) provide a recent summary of these issues. 3 They 
argue that an achievement production function can be expressed as

Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
The basic idea of the function is that achievement is a cumulative 
process resulting from a history of family, school, and community inputs 
and innate personal ability. There are at least three challenges in estimating 
the function: (a) data on inputs are incomplete, (b)

 is not observable, 
and (c) current inputs may be chosen endogenously in response to prior 
realizations of achievements.
The estimation of the function can be grouped into three categories: 
(a) contemporaneous, where all historical data are not considered;                                                                       
(b) value-added, the most common approach, which uses contemporaneous 
and baseline achievement measures as proxies for the history of inputs; 
and (c) cumulative, which relates the full history of inputs to unobserved 
endowments. Available data always determine the final specification at the 
time of estimation.
Assuming additive separability and parameters that are non-age 
varying will yield the following estimable specification:

𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃, 𝑄𝑄𝑄𝑄(𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃|𝑋𝑋𝑋𝑋) = 𝛼𝛼𝛼𝛼(𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃) + 𝑋𝑋𝑋𝑋′𝛽𝛽𝛽𝛽(𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃) 
 
∫ 𝑄𝑄𝑄𝑄(𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃|𝑋𝑋𝑋𝑋)𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑𝜃𝜃𝜃𝜃 = 𝐸𝐸𝐸𝐸(𝑦𝑦𝑦𝑦|𝑋𝑋𝑋𝑋) 
 
 
This specification boils down to the contemporaneous version of 
the education production function. Hence, what will be estimated are 
correlations rather than a causal relationship between test scores and 
personal, household, and community characteristics. 
Estimation method
The study estimates the average relationships between personal, household, 
and school characteristics and test scores using ordinary least squares (OLS). 
Additionally, it estimates quantile regressions to generate indications of 
the differential relationships across different levels of test scores. While 
the OLS estimates of the average relationship are highly informative, 
understanding the differential relationships for high and low performers 
further enriches the analyses. 
In particular, the OLS estimate provides the correlation of                 
covariates

𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖 = 𝑿𝑿𝑿𝑿𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝜶𝜶𝜶𝜶 + 𝛾𝛾𝛾𝛾𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖−1 + 𝜂𝜂𝜂𝜂𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖

This perspective allows for a more nuanced interpretation of the average 
correlation between y and X. For instance, when one observes that the 
coefficient of the OLS regression is not significant, it may indicate one of 
two possibilities: (a) the coefficient is not significant across all values of the 
dependent variable, or (b) the effects are offsetting across the values of the 
dependent variable.
Data source and measures of outcomes
The study uses data from the PISA 2018 round, in which the Philippines 
participated for the first time. The assessment aims to collect a representative 
sample of in-school 15-year-olds (N=7,233 students). To be eligible, 
students must have enrolled in Grade 7 or higher (Schleicher 2019). The 
test is conducted in English and delivered via a two-hour, computer-based 
assessment. An important limitation of this study is the unavailability of 
data on performance by level, as PISA assesses only 15-year-old students 
(equivalent to junior high school students in the country). Another 
limitation, given the nature of the data, is that the study focuses on the 
contemporaneous correlation of test scores and their correlates rather 
than estimating causal relationships.
Sampling was completed in two stages. The first stage involved 
selecting 187 schools from 17 regions in proportion to the number of 
15-year-old students, and the second stage required selecting 42 PISA-eligible 
students4 for each school (DepEd 2019). The Philippine dataset contains 
information at both the student and school levels. The teacher module 
was not implemented in the Philippines for the 2018 round. The same                                                             
dataset includes data on personal and household characteristics provided by 
students, as well as school characteristics reported by the school principals. 
The outcomes of interest in this study are test scores in Mathematics, 
Science, and Reading. Data includes 10 plausible values for each outcome 
to account for measurement error. Survey weights are applied for point 
4 Not all schools are able to have 42 students participate. For instance, one school had as few as                            
4 students participating.

estimation, along with 80 replicate weights for error estimation. The 
estimation utilizes repest, a wrapper for estimation commands that 
incorporates plausible values and replicate weights. As for the variables 
studied (see Table 1; refer also to Appendix C for the definitions of the 
variables), most indices are standardized with a mean of zero, centered 
around the OECD average.

Highlights of the estimation results
Mathematics 
The summary of the estimation results (see Table 2) shows the following:  
• Individual characteristics. The student’s age is positively 
associated with test scores, with each year corresponding to an 
8.5-point increase in test scores. This relationship is stronger for                                                                                                                                  
low-performing students than for high-performing students.                 
=== PAGE 93 ===
62
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Being male is not significantly related to test scores on average. 
However, being male has a significantly negative effect for low 
performers (at quantiles 0.1 and 0.25), shows no significant 
difference in the middle quantiles (at 0.5 and 0.75), and is 
significantly positive for very high performers (at quantile 0.9). 
Repeating a grade is associated with lower test scores by 
an average of 46.7 points, with the effect being greater for low 
performers compared to high performers. The age at which 
students start primary education has a negative relationship 
with test scores; students who start later score an average of                                                
-5.4 points for each year of delay. This relationship is less negative 
for low performers compared to high performers. The prospects 
of completing college positively correlate with test scores, 
contributing an average of 8 points.  This result is significantly 
higher for low performers and significant for all quantiles except 
for 0.9. 
Tardiness does not significantly correlate with test scores              
on average or across all quantiles. Experience with bullying 
negatively correlates with test scores, and this correlation becomes 
more pronounced for top performers.
• Household characteristics. Parents’ education level does not correlate 
with test scores on average or across all quantiles. Parental 
occupation positively correlates with test scores on average, 
with this positive relationship increasing for high performers. 
Parental emotional support positively correlates with test scores, 
and this relationship is stronger for low performers compared to 
high performers.
• School characteristics. The student-to-teacher ratio does not 
significantly correlate with test scores on average or across all 
quantiles. The number of computers per student is positively 
related to test scores, with an average coefficient of 29.2 points.            
This relationship increases in magnitude for higher performers. 
The proportion of computers connected to the internet is 
positively related to test scores, with an average coefficient of 
19.4 points. This variable matters more for high performers.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The proportion of certified teachers is not correlated with 
test scores on average or across all quantiles. The proportion 
of teachers with Master’s degrees is also not correlated with 
test scores on average or across all quantiles. Class size is not 
correlated with test scores on average or for low performers; 
however, it is negatively correlated with top performers. Learning 
time in mathematics is not related to test scores on average or 
for low performers but is curiously negatively correlated with 
top performers. Admission requirements based on academic 
performance are not correlated with test scores on average 
or across all quantiles. The disciplinary climate is positively 
correlated with test scores on average, and this relationship 
increases for top performers.
Science
What the estimation results (see Table 3) show are the following:  
• Individual characteristics. Age at the time of the test does appear 
to be correlated with test scores on average and in all quartiles. 
The same is true for gender. The incidence of grade repetition 
negatively correlates with test scores (reducing average test 
scores by 39.5 points). This correlation increases with high 
performers: while those at the 0.1 quantile see a reduction of 
27.7 points, top performers at the 0.9 quantile experience a 
reduction of 46.4 points. 
The age when the student started primary schooling 
negatively correlates with test scores, with a one-year increase 
associated with a decline in test scores of 4.8 points. This negative 
relationship becomes stronger with high performers. The 
prospect of completing college is not correlated with test scores 
on average and has a complex relationship across the quantiles. It 
is positively correlated for low performers, while the relationship 
becomes negative for high performers. 
Tardiness has a more perverse than expected relationship 
with test scores, showing a positive correlation, and this relationship 
=== PAGE 96 ===
65
Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
increases in magnitude as one moves up the performance quantiles. 
Experience with bullying is negatively correlated with test scores, 
increasing from a low negative to a high negative as one ascends 
the performance quantiles.
• Household characteristics.  Parents’ educational attainment is 
not correlated with test scores, but the relationship becomes 
significantly positive for high performers, starting at the median. 
Parents’ occupation positively correlates with test scores, and this 
correlation increases as one moves up the performance quantiles. 
Parents’ emotional support positively correlates with test scores, 
and this relationship is stronger for low performers compared to 
high performers.
• School characteristics. The student-teacher ratio is not significantly 
correlated with test scores on average but has a negative correlation 
with high performers starting at the 0.25 quantile. The proportion 
of certified teachers does not significantly correlate with average 
test scores across all performance quantiles. The proportion 
of teachers with master’s degrees is not significantly related to 
test scores in most quintiles, except for the top performers at 
the 0.9 quantile, where a positive relationship is observed, 
corresponding to a 16.6-point increase in science test scores
Class size is not significantly correlated with test scores 
on average and for low performers but becomes negatively               
correlated starting from the median quantile. Curiously,  
learning time in science is negatively correlated with test scores. 
This result for learning time in science is not significant for 
low performers but gradually becomes increasingly negative 
as one moves up the performance quantiles. Admission based 
on academic performance does not significantly correlate with 
test scores on average and across the performance quantiles. 
Disciplinary climate positively correlates with test scores, and 
this positive relationship strengthens as a student progresses 
through their education.

67
Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
Reading
Estimation results for Reading (see Table 4) show the following:
• Individual characteristics. Age at test positively correlates with test 
scores on average, increasing scores by 9 points for each additional 
year. This effect is greater for low performers compared to high 
performers. Being male is negatively correlated with test scores 
on average, reducing scores by 9.2 points; this effect is again larger                  
for low performers and becomes insignificant for quantile 0.9. 
Grade repetition negatively correlates with test scores on average, 
decreasing scores by 45.2 points for those who repeated a grade, 
with a larger negative impact for high performers. Starting age 
of primary schooling is negatively correlated with test scores on 
average, reducing scores by 5.1 points, with a more pronounced 
effect for high performers. Higher self-rated reading ability 
positively correlates with test scores on average by 9.6 points,                                                                                                                                    
and this correlation becomes stronger for high performers.               
Joy and a liking for reading positively correlate with test scores 
on average, increasing scores by 16.3 points, which is more 
pronounced for low performers. The prospect of completing 
college does not correlate with test scores on average but positively 
correlates for low performers and negatively correlates for high 
performers. The incidence of tardiness has a counterintuitive 
relationship with test scores, yielding a positive correlation 
that becomes stronger among high performers. Experiencing 
bullying is negatively correlated with test scores on average, 
with a larger negative impact for high performers.
• Household characteristics. Parents’ education positively correlates 
with test scores on average, increasing scores by 1.1 points, with a 
greater effect for high performers. Parents’ occupation positively 
correlates with test scores on average, and this correlation 
is stronger for high performers. Parents’ emotional support 
positively correlates with test scores, with a more significant 
effect for low performers.

=== PAGE 100 ===
69
Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
• School characteristics. The student-teacher ratio does not 
significantly correlate with test scores on average but negatively 
correlates for high performers (quantiles 0.75 and 0.9). The 
proportion of certified teachers does not significantly correlate 
with test scores on average; however, it is negatively correlated 
with test scores for low performers (quantiles 0.1 and 0.25) but 
not significantly correlated for high performers. Similarly, the 
proportion of teachers with master’s degrees does not significantly 
correlate with test scores. This share of master’s degree-holding 
teachers is also negatively correlated for low performers 
(quantiles 0.1 and 0.25) but is not significant for high performers. 
Class size does not significantly correlate with test scores on 
average, but it is negatively correlated, starting with quantile 0.25                
and extending to higher quantiles. Learning time in English 
negatively correlates with test scores on average, but this is not 
significant for low performers and negatively correlates with 
higher performers. Admission based on academic performance 
does not significantly correlate with test scores on average. It 
does not significantly correlate for low performers, positively                
correlates for those in the middle quantiles (quantiles 0.25 to 0.75),                                                                                                                                           
but does not significantly correlate for top performers. 
Disciplinary climate positively correlates with test scores, 
and this positive correlation increases as one moves up the 
performance levels.
What measures matter moving forward?
Drawing from the abundant literature in other contexts showing that 
education quality is a product of the interplay between individual, 
household, school, and community-related factors, the correlation of test 
scores with select personal, household, and community characteristics was 
estimated using the 2018 PISA dataset of the Philippines, where the country 
participated for the first time. This study aligns with the current efforts of                                                
both the administrative and legislative branches of government to improve 
education quality. 
=== PAGE 101 ===
70
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Estimation results show that among the individual characteristics 
considered, repeating a grade, age of starting primary education, and 
bullying have consistent negative correlations across subject areas. In terms 
of family background, parental occupation and parental emotional support                
have a positive correlation with test scores. As for school characteristics, 
only the disciplinary climate consistently correlates with test scores. These 
results highlight the roles of factors beyond the confines of the school. 
Another important policy question is which factors help low 
performers improve. Estimation results show that the prospects of completing 
college help low performers more, even if the correlation is not statistically 
significant on average. Parents’ emotional support also helps low performers. 
The correlation of school characteristics with test scores is mixed. 
The number of computers per student and the proportion of computers                   
connected to the internet are associated with higher scores in Mathematics.  
The student-teacher ratio is associated with lower scores for high 
performers, even if not significantly correlated on average. Similarly, class                                      
size also correlates with lower test scores for high performers. In terms               
of gender, only in reading is there evidence of lower test scores for males 
than for females.
There are also puzzling estimation results that require closer scrutiny.   
One is the negative association between test scores and learning time. 
Given that correlation is being estimated, this may indicate that it is not a 
puzzle after all, as the result implies that smarter students learn quickly and   
hence require less time to learn. Teacher qualifications (i.e., being certified 
and having master’s degrees) are either insignificantly related to test scores 
or negatively correlated (e.g., in reading). This result highlights that better 
qualifications do not always translate into better pedagogy.
Given these estimation results, what measures matter in finding levers                  
to improve school achievement?
First, there may be a need to address grade repetition since estimation 
results show that repeating a grade has a double-whammy effect. Grade 
repetition not only wastes investments for the school year but is also 
associated with lower test scores. Second, it may be beneficial for parents to 
have their children enter primary education early since starting primary 
school later is associated with lower test scores. Third, promoting parental                                                            
support matters since the estimation results indicate that parental support is 
positively correlated with test scores consistently.
=== PAGE 102 ===
71
Correlates of Test Performance of 15-Year-Old Students in the Philippines
Fourth, bullying in schools should be addressed. The PISA reports 
that 64.9 percent of students in the Philippines reported being bullied at 
least a few times a month. Besides the fact that bullying is inherently 
unacceptable, it is also negatively correlated with test scores. A fifth and 
related recommendation is that schools should instill a better disciplinary 
climate in classrooms since such a disciplinary climate consistently correlates 
positively with test scores.
Sixth, regarding study habits, promoting reading for boys is 
recommended since males lag behind females in reading test scores. Seventh, 
the negative correlations between learning times and test scores should be 
examined more closely. While the abilities of fast learners are recognized, 
this examination of learning time should encourage an assessment of how 
schools and teachers use instructional time.
Eighth, further investigation is needed to understand why teacher 
qualifications emerged as not being correlated with test scores. If possession 
of a master’s degree does not increase test scores in Mathematics and Science                     
and is even negatively correlated with low performers’ test scores in 
Reading, studying the role of teacher qualifications in student learning 
becomes ripe for research. 
Ninth, the DepEd’s National Achievement Tests and other 
standardized test results should also be studied further. One concrete step 
is for these national standardized test results to be disseminated more widely  
and for public use files (PUFs) to become more accessible, similar to those                  
of the surveys by the Philippine Statistics Authority. 
Finally, amid the distressing scores for the country in these 
international student assessments, it is recommended that the Philippines 
continue participating in international benchmarking tests and learning 
from the results. Proof of this participation is evident in the 2022 PISA, 
with the country posting results almost similar to those of the 2018 PISA.                
Despite any despair in observing these results for the Philippines, let this 
assessment fuel the demand for continued reforms in delivering quality-oriented 
basic education based on reliable quality measures such as test scores.

Chapter 3
Mobility and Inequality

Lawrence B. Dacuycuy

=== PAGE 111 ===
80
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
Social mobility connotes a dynamic process that pertains to how 
younger—relative to older—generations transition to better education 
and labor market outcomes. Societies classified as mobile are those that 
can weaken the influence of socioeconomic status or family background 
on these outcomes. The world continues to be divided into socially mobile 
and relatively immobile societies, with the former represented by Nordic 
countries with robust welfare systems. The national social mobility 
record of developing economies like the Philippines is not encouraging, 
however. Based on the World Economic Forum’s 2020 Global Social Mobility                                                                        
Index, the Philippines ranked 61st out of 82 countries, just ahead of its                                                                                                                                              
ASEAN peers, such as Indonesia (67th) and Laos (72nd). Due to its 
heightened emphasis on social mobility, Singapore ranked as the top economy 
within the ASEAN region at 20th place, followed by Malaysia (43rd),                           
Viet Nam (50th), and Thailand (55th).1
Education is perceived as one of the critical drivers of social mobility.2              
The importance of education is embedded in the sectoral targets laid out in 
the Philippine Development Plan (PDP) 2017–2022 , which include increasing  
mean years of schooling, net enrollment rates for elementary and junior 
high school students, and completion rates (NEDA 2017). Despite this 
emphasis, a critical concern is the quality of education, which has issues 
on both the supply and demand sides. As noted in the PDP, supply-side                                                                                                                    
concerns affecting basic education include the availability and skills of 
senior high school teachers, mismatches between teacher specialization and 
subjects taught, and delayed release of education inputs needed to inform 
pedagogical/learning strategies. 3 At the tertiary level, Philippine higher 
education institutions (PHEIs) need to foster international collaboration 
1 There are 10 pillars used in computing the 2020 Global Social Mobility Index (WEF 2020), 3 of which 
pertain to education outcomes. Out of 82 countries considered, the Philippines ranked 59th in Pillar 2 
(Education Access). With respect to Pillar 3 (Education Quality), the Philippines ranked 71st. Considered a 
bright spot, it ranked highly at 25th in Pillar 4 (Lifelong Learning).
2 Education has been established as a key determinant of mobility. However, expanding educational 
access may not be sufficient to spur mobility, as inequalities may persist, thereby depriving education of 
its usual mechanism to equalize learning opportunities among students (OECD 2018).
3 These problems were identified in the updated PDP’s chapter on Human Capital Development Towards 
Greater Agility.
=== PAGE 112 ===
81
Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
with foreign institutions of learning and research to facilitate knowledge 
transfer, improve the research capabilities of graduate students, and 
innovate curriculum development to promote partnerships between PHEIs 
and industry, which may reduce problems related to matching learner 
competencies to labor market requirements. 
On the demand side and in the context of realizing social mobility 
gains, one pathway involves reducing inequities in educational outcomes. 
As clearly articulated in the OECD (2018) report, a key requirement 
for social mobility is educational mobility. This means that students, 
regardless of their social origins, should have the opportunity to complete                                         
college. This aligns with the principle of Equality of Opportunity, which 
asserts that a child’s success in life should depend on hard work rather than                                     
the socioeconomic status of the child’s parents (Mitnik and Grusky 2015). 
Due to data limitations, there are few intergenerational studies on 
education in the Philippines. Despite these challenges in data availability, 
this chapter demonstrates that a cohort-based perspective can be used to               
analyze patterns of intergenerational mobility and inequality in education. 
The study then shares insights that offer directions for future research.
Educational mobility in the Philippines
Patterns in schooling years 
In the Philippines, past studies have shed light on how intergenerational 
transfers affect schooling outcomes (Quisumbing 1994; Lauby and Stark 1998; 
Estudillo et al. 2001; Quisumbing and McNiven 2010; Yamauchi and 
Tiongco 2013). With respect to the intergenerational income elasticity,                                 
Bevis and Barrett (2015) used the Bukidnon Panel Survey to analyze how 
parents’ endowments affect the income of their children and found that 
maternal education had a stronger effect than paternal education. Yamauchi 
and Tiongco (2013) used data from several school divisions in the Philippines 
and found that parents invest more in their daughters’ education in 
anticipation of labor market discrimination against women. Several studies 
have examined intergenerational education elasticity in the labor market 
context (Lanzona 1998; Dacuycuy 2018).
=== PAGE 113 ===
82
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Recently, a study by Dacuycuy and Bayudan-Dacuycuy (2020) focused 
on the gender and geographical dimensions of social mobility, identifying 
regions where the connections between the educational outcomes of children 
and their parents are deemed persistent. The study utilized the 2010 Census 
of Population and Housing (CPH, Form 3, 20% sampling). To understand 
the intertemporal changes in educational outcomes, the paper defined                             
five-year cohorts of sons and daughters: 1960–1964, 1965–1969, 1970–1974, 
1975–1979, and 1980–1984. At the national level, the average years of 
schooling for females were higher than that for males. While this was true 
across cohorts, the gap had somewhat narrowed in the youngest cohort. 
The averages of schooling years at the regional level showed useful spatial 
patterns. These include the following:
1. Daughters enjoyed more years of schooling relative to their 
parents. However, in several regions, including NCR and CAR,     
sons displayed comparable years of schooling relative to their fathers. 
2. Regional disparities in mean schooling were also observed, 
indicating unequal educational outcomes between progressive 
and less progressive regions. Sons and daughters in the ARMM 
had the lowest mean years of schooling. The youngest cohort 
(1980–1984) of daughters in the ARMM had a mean of around 
nine years of schooling, which was not even on par with the 
oldest cohort in other regions. The youngest cohort of sons had 
a mean of around eight years of schooling, merely matching the 
mean of the oldest cohort in other regions.
3. While far from representing the norm, some regions like the 
NCR had achieved low variability in human capital investments 
across cohorts and between genders, indicating sustained 
expansion in education across grade levels and a relatively equal 
playing field between genders across cohorts in this region. 
CALABARZON, Northern Mindanao, and Caraga exhibited 
differentials in schooling years within a narrow band across 
cohorts. However, compared with the NCR, these regions had 
higher differentials, with daughters possessing two more years 
of schooling than sons in Northern Mindanao and Caraga. 
=== PAGE 114 ===
83
Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
Intergenerational education mobility
Relative intergenerational educational mobility is measured using linear 
regression methods (Azam and Bhatt 2012; Chetty et al. 2014; Azam 2015; 
Azomahou and Yitbarek 2016; Aydemir 2019). Following Dacuycuy (2018), 
the equation of interest is given by the following:
where        and        are the schooling achievements of the child and                                                                                                                                              
parent, respectively. Both schooling achievements are measured in terms 
of years of schooling;                 is a known linear function associated with                    
parental educational achievements, the index h refers to the household                     
to which child i belongs and resides in region r, and      is the disturbance                  
term pertaining to unobserved attributes of the child and other processes. 
Known as the persistence parameter,   pertains to the effects of parental 
education.    is between 0 and 1.   close to 1 implies that children’s educational 
achievements are similar to those of their parents. The intergenerational 
mobility measure             is also referred to as intergenerational educational 
mobility (IEE), thus implying low mobility when the measure is close to 0                  
(i.e., high persistence implies low mobility). 
Based on the study by Dacuycuy and Bayudan-Dacuycuy (2020), 4                 
a simple classification scheme is created based on thresholds for mobility 
measures and human capital accumulation. Those with a mean IEE less 
than 0.3 are considered highly mobile (HM). In terms of human capital 
accumulation, those to the left of the 10-year line are regions with currently             
low mean years of schooling (LS) or under-accumulation. Those to the right 
are regions with high mean years of schooling or accumulation (HS). Given 
these, four regions are created: LM-LS (regressive under-accumulation), 
LM-HS (regressive accumulation), HM-HS (progressive accumulation), and 
HM-LS (progressive under-accumulation). 
The following observations are noted: 
1. Daughters in all regions, except ARMM, experienced improvements 
in human capital accumulation. Daughters in ARMM experienced 
either progressive under-accumulation of human capital, 
indicating persistently low educational outcomes across cohorts. 
4 The data used in the study is from the Census of Population and Housing 2015, which did not account               
for regional mobility.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
2. Daughters from the young cohort in NCR had the most 
progressive human capital accumulation. On the other hand, 
daughters from the young cohort in CAR had progressive 
accumulation. This indicated the high educational outcomes of 
older cohorts in CAR so that in terms of mobility, young cohorts 
would need even higher educational attainment to outperform 
their parents. 
3. Sons from across cohorts in some regions exhibited persistent 
under-accumulation of human capital. In MIMAROPA, Bicol, 
Western/Central/Eastern Visayas, ARMM, and the Zamboanga 
Peninsula, sons persistently faced regressive under-accumulation 
of human capital. In SOCCSKSARGEN and Caraga, sons remained 
in the progressive under-accumulation region. This means that 
despite achieving better educational outcomes than their parents, 
their educational attainment remained low. 
4. In NCR, CAR, Ilocos Norte, CALABARZON, and Central 
Luzon, a progressive accumulation of human capital among 
sons was observed. 
Educational inequality in the Philippines
Inequities in education dampen prospects for social mobility (OECD 2018). One 
plausible manifestation of this occurs when educational inequality among 
young people exceeds that of the older generation. This dynamic may be 
reversed through education expansion. The literature is clear—addressing 
educational inequities does not necessarily result in the same outcomes 
for everyone. Rather, it is the equalization of educational opportunities 
that matters. A study by Mesa (2007) focused on regional and provincial 
characterizations of educational inequality and its correlates. However, 
exploring the intergenerational mobility‒inequality link through cohort 
analysis has yet to be done in the Philippines, opening up another opportunity 
for empirical inquiry. 
While a diverse set of attributes has been identified to explain social 
mobility outcomes, one issue that has attracted scant attention in the 
Philippines concerns the empirical relationship between inequality and 
intergenerational mobility in education. As one of the more comprehensive 
=== PAGE 116 ===
85
Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
studies on education inequality, Mesa (2007) estimated the education                 
G i n i  c o e ffi c i e n t  t o  m e a s u r e  i n e q u a l i t y  a c r o s s  r e g i o n s  a n d  p r o v i n c e s  i n                                                                                                                                                
the Philippines. Although the Gini measure of inequality may not be                        
as informative as the more detailed educational assessments such as                                                                                                                                 
PISA, it can nonetheless provide an acceptable measure for interpreting 
the distribution of education across cohorts. During periods of educational 
expansion, the younger generation can surpass their parents’ educational 
attainment, leading to a more favorable mobility picture (Cuaresma et                            
al. 2013). 
Following Alesina et al. (2018), the Gini index, based on the individual 
educational achievements of sons and daughters as inputs, is computed. For 
each region r, individuals are ranked based on their highest educational 
attainment (in years). The Gini index 5 for region r is given by the formula,                                                                                                                                         
                                 , where     represents the mean years of schooling in 
region r, and     the number of individuals in the regional sample. The 
higher the value of      , the greater the education inequality. 
This formula is applied to the CPH data, and the results, shown in 
Table 1, reveal the following patterns:6
1. There was heterogeneity in education inequality, with the oldest 
and youngest cohorts in regions such as the NCR experiencing 
the lowest inequality, while those in the ARMM experienced the 
highest inequality.
2. By gender, inequality in years of schooling was lower for females              
than for males in both cohorts. Additionally, the reduction rate             
of education inequality among females was faster than that 
among males. 
3. Among the regions, CAR showed the most dramatic reduction 
in inequality and improvement in mean years of schooling over 
20 years. Luzon regions (Ilocos, Cagayan Valley, and Central 
Luzon), Visayas regions (Central and Western), and ARMM also 
showed substantial reductions in education inequality.
5 Unlike the methodology developed by Thomas et al. (2001) and used by Mesa (2007) in her inequality 
analysis, the Gini index used in this study yields an individual-based inequality measure.
6 Geographical mobility was not controlled for due to limitations in the CPH data. The authors recognize 
that geographical mobility may affect regional educational composition, as younger cohorts move to 
regions with better academic institutions. Accounting for migration is an area for future research.

Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
The correlation between mean schooling and inequality is estimated 
using a model that nets out cohort effects for both males and females. As 
shown in Figure 1, the correlation is negative. However, among sons, 
the data cloud clusters around the center of the regression line, with 
the ARMM and NCR positioned at extremes. 7 This pattern does not 
appear among females, whose data cloud forms more tightly around the                                                                     
estimated regression line, indicating a better model fit. The model’s overall 
explanatory power is 97 percent for daughters and 88 percent for sons. 
A distinction between the other regions and the ARMM is also evident.                                                                                                   
The slope of the regression line for daughters is steeper than that for sons, 
indicating a stronger response in mobility outcomes for daughters, given a 
reduction in education inequality compared to sons.
The association between education mobility 
and inequality

al. (2021) is adopted. Equation 1 (see page 83) is modified as follows:

While the negative correlation between education mobility and 
inequality is evident (Figure 2), several patterns emerge: 
1. Several regions in Luzon achieved low inequality and 
high mobility, especially regarding daughters’ educational 
achievements. Notably, the NCR and CAR were top performers.
2. The ARMM achieved some improvements in education mobility 
relative to its peers, although it has yet to narrow its inequality.

Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
This is evident in the contrast between Eastern Visayas and the 
Zamboanga Peninsula, where daughters experienced relatively 
lower inequality than those in the ARMM, despite similar 
mobility levels. 
3. In most regions, except the NCR, sons were characterized by low 
mobility and high inequality in educational outcomes. Despite 
modest mobility improvements in the ARMM, sons have yet to 
narrow the inequality.
One contributing factor to inequality in education is the differential 
expansion rates in education. For mobility gains to materialize, expansion 
should occur progressively at higher levels of schooling. To investigate 
this, the cumulative density functions (CDF) of years of schooling for both 
sons and daughters were estimated. The CDF indicates the proportion of                           
the population whose educational achievement is less than or equal to 
a  s p e c i fi c  y e a r  o f  s c h o o l i n g .  S p e c i a l  e m p h a s i s  i s  p l a c e d  o n  t h e  A R M M                                                                                                                         
and NCR due to their stark differences in educational achievements                           
across cohorts.
For daughters, there is a clear downward shift in the distribution                    
of years of schooling across cohorts, suggesting that younger cohorts                      
have made solid gains in education over time. In the NCR, the respective     
CDF values at lower levels of educational achievement for younger and 
older cohorts are significantly lower than those in the ARMM. For instance,                   
nearly 19 percent of the ARMM’s oldest cohort (1960–1964) did not 
complete any schooling. In contrast, the share of individuals in the NCR                                                                                                                                              
who did not finish any schooling is well below 1 percent, reflecting the 
relatively low educational inequality in that region. 
Most of the increases in years of schooling in the ARMM stem 
from rising elementary school attainment, while the shift in the NCR 
indicates tremendous expansion in tertiary education. This is evident in 
the significant increase in the respective proportions of daughters who 
graduated from high school and college in the NCR. In the ARMM, which                                                               
has the highest inequality across cohorts, educational gains for daughters                   
at the tertiary level have been modest compared to those for sons. This 
helps explain why inequality among daughters in the ARMM is high 
relative to other regions.

=== PAGE 122 ===
91
Mobility and Inequality in Education in the Philippines
Ways forward
Using the 2010 CPH, this chapter characterizes intergenerational mobility 
and inequality in education. Due to the lack of migration information in the 
CPH, the chapter cannot account for geographical mobility across cohorts. 
It is recognized that geographical mobility may affect regional educational 
composition as younger cohorts move to richer regions with good academic 
institutions. Despite this limitation, the educational characterizations in a 
mobility-inequality context, based on the available data, provide insights 
that inform future research directions. 
What have we learned thus far?
First, the experiences of certain regions in facilitating distributional shifts 
in schooling years warrant in-depth investigation. Progressive regions 
like the NCR, Central Luzon, and CALABARZON exhibited favorable 
distributional profiles even for older cohorts. This indicates that these 
regions were able to reduce the proportion of their population with low 
levels of schooling early on. Additionally, the NCR is a region where the 
education mobility-inequality outcomes for sons are on par with those of                                            
daughters. Thus, understanding the school and home environments in these 
regions, especially in the NCR, can offer substantial directions for improving 
mobility and human capital outcomes in other areas.
Second, education outcomes are marked by spatial heterogeneity. 
Regions, especially those in Luzon, have experienced progressive human 
capital accumulation. This contrasts with some regions in the Visayas and 
Mindanao, where mobility has improved but inequality remains high. 
Understanding why regions in Luzon achieved better outcomes in tertiary 
education is essential. A contributing factor to education inequality is the 
difference in expansion rates across regions. For mobility gains to be realized, 
expansion should occur progressively at higher levels of schooling—an 
objective that regions in Luzon appear to have substantially achieved. 
Third, gaps exist in the education outcomes between daughters 
and sons. Uncovering the factors that foster good educational outcomes for 
daughters and understanding how these can be applied to the context of 
sons may help reduce these gaps. Not only is inequality in schooling years 
=== PAGE 123 ===
92
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
lower for females than for males in both young and old cohorts, but the 
rate of reduction of inequality among females is also faster. The factors 
contributing to the impressive mobility-equality achievements of daughters 
in the NCR and CAR also need to be investigated.

Chapter 4
Public School Teachers,

=== PAGE 127 ===
96
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
Teachers are the most important resource in any educational institution 
because they facilitate learning. Studies show that several factors affect the 
quality of education in schools: human resources (teachers and principals), 
material resources (textbooks, classrooms, and school facilities), financial 
resources (teacher salaries and public expenditure), and characteristics of 
learners’ households (e.g., Lee and Barro 1997; Hattie 2009; OECD 2009) 
(see Chapter 3). However, teacher quality is the single most important                            
in-school factor that influences learning (Albert et al. 2021a). How can 
students learn well if teachers are unable to teach effectively? Specifically for 
public schools, how can the government equip teachers with the necessary 
skills and motivation?
The requirements for embarking on a teaching career are specified                
by law: teachers must have at least a four-year bachelor’s degree and pass 
the Licensure Examination for Teachers (LET). 1 There are specialized 
education courses for primary school teachers (Bachelor of Elementary 
Education) and high school teachers (Bachelor of Secondary Education) with 
both programs consisting of general education subjects, education-related 
subjects, subject specialization, and practical teaching (David et al. 2018a). 
Those with a bachelor’s degree in other fields can also qualify to become a 
teacher if they complete a postgraduate program in Education, commonly 
referred to as a Certificate of Professional Education. 
If hired by the government, teachers are provided professional 
development through training activities conducted between school sessions. 
However, the effectiveness of these training activities is questionable 
since they involve neither training needs assessments (generated from 
the results of the National Achievement Tests) nor a monitoring and 
evaluation mechanism. 
Drawing lessons from several evaluation studies on teacher incentives 
and policy studies on out-of-school children and education interventions, 
this chapter aims to answer several key questions: Do we have enough 
teachers? What incentives are provided to teachers that affect their 
teaching quality? Are incentives being used effectively to improve teacher 
skills and motivation?
1 Republic Act 7836, passed in 1994, makes it mandatory for teachers to first pass the LET.
=== PAGE 128 ===
97
Public School Teachers, Incentives, and Learning Quality
An overview of the teaching workforce 
in public schools
Public school teachers and other career employees of the Department of 
Education (DepEd) comprise the bulk of the Philippine bureaucracy, with 
60 percent being women (see Table 1). Data also show that the number 
of public school teachers grew by 85.7 percent in the last two decades, 
resulting in over 800,000 teachers as of 2019 (see Figure 1).
The increase in the teaching workforce over the past two decades is                  
a result of the addition of three more grade levels when the basic education 
system adopted the K-12 (from Kindergarten to Grade 12) program. From 
2012 to 2016, DepEd hired more primary school teachers for kindergarten 
and elementary (around 200,000) and senior high school teachers (nearly 
40,000). During this period, the demand for new teachers also extended to 
private schools since the K-12 program applies to all educational institutions 
in the Philippines.
From 2000 to 2011, the pupil-to-teacher ratio (PTR) was 37 high 
school learners for every high school teacher. After the increase in the 
teaching workforce, PTRs dropped to 27 in 2014 and further to 24 by 2017.

This change is expected to improve learning quality, as less crowded 
classrooms allow teachers to dedicate more time to the learning needs of             
all pupils.2  
Since 1971, the teaching bureaucracy has been dominated by women 
(UIS 2020). The proportion of female primary education teachers (full-time 
and part-time teachers) averages 86.9 percent. It has ranged from 80.0 to 
95.1 percent, while the share of females among secondary education teachers 
averages 76.2 percent and ranges from 70.7 to 95.1 percent. This gender                                                                                                 
imbalance among public school teachers is believed to contribute to why 
boys have lower school participation and achievements than girls (David et al. 
2009; Mulji 2016; David et al. 2018b; Paqueo and Orbeta 2019; Terrier 2020). 
Another study shows that while the sex of the teacher has no effect at the 
elementary level, small positive effects are found at the middle and high 
2 While no ideal PTR has been suggested in the literature, low PTRs are desirable, with developing 
countries benchmarking their PTRs with those of neighboring countries of similar development, as well    
as those of developed countries. Reducing PTRs would also entail expenditures not only for teacher 
salaries but also for more classrooms needed for smaller classes.

school levels (Winters et al. 2013). However, other studies suggest that there 
is insufficient evidence to support the idea that the sex of a teacher has a 
causal effect on student outcomes (Holmlund and Sund 2021) and that it 
is insignificant in developing student interest and self-efficacy in science, 
technology, engineering, and math (STEM) subjects (Sansone 2019).
Tasks and incentives of teachers
Nearly 70 percent of the DepEd annual budget is allotted to personnel 
services, specifically teacher and staff salaries (Albert et al. 2021b), to ensure                              
that teachers are adequately compensated. Data from the October 2020 Labor 
Force Survey show that average wages for teachers are PHP 22,074 per 
month, nearly double the median monthly wages of wage and salary 
workers (PHP 11,277) in the country (PSA 2021), contradicting the popular 
notion that public school teachers are underpaid. 
In addition, public school teachers are scheduled to receive salary 
increases in four tranches from 2020 to 2023 (Sevilla 2021) with the passage 
of the Salary Standardization Law of 2019 (Republic Act 114661). For the     
lowest rank, the salary for Teacher I (SG 11) was increased to PHP 22,316                                 
in 2020, PHP 23,877 in 2021, PHP 25,439 in 2022, and PHP 27,000 in 2023.                                                                                                                                           
For the highest rank, those with a Master Teacher IV post (SG 21) saw 
their salaries increase to PHP 59,353 in 2020, PHP 60,901 in 2021,                                                                                                                                           
PHP 62,449 in 2022, and PHP 63,997 in 2023. As a result, public school 
teachers earn salaries higher than their counterparts in the private sector,                
who, in 2019, were receiving average monthly salaries of PHP 11,416, 
compared to average salaries of PHP 19,566 for public school teachers. 3              
The salary increases further widen the wage gap between public and private 
school teachers. 
Beyond salary, remuneration for public school teachers includes 
other employment benefits associated with being a public servant, as well 
as additional financial support specifically tailored for teachers. Basic 
employment benefits for government employees comprise the following: 
• Membership with the Philippine Health Insurance Corporation 
(PhilHealth), which includes health insurance for hospitalization 
and an annual physical exam 
3 https://www.teacherph.com/public-school-teachers-salary
