• Government Service Insurance System (GSIS) membership 
benefits: Retirement and life insurance plan (teachers contribute 
9% of their basic pay, while the government contributes an 
amount equal to 12% of the basic pay)
• Vacation credits of up to 15 days per year 
• Leave privileges (e.g., Maternity Leave, Paternity Leave, and 
Study Leave)
• Clothing/uniform allowance of PHP 6,000 per year, released no 
earlier than April 15  
• Mid-year bonus of one month’s basic salary, released no earlier 
than May 15
• Year-end bonus of one month’s basic salary, released in November
• Cash gift of PHP 5,000, released in November
• Anniversary bonus of PHP 3,000, awarded only during milestone 
years (i.e., every 5 years) 
• Personal Economic Relief Assistance of PHP 2,000 per month
• Representation and transportation allowance, ranging from 
PHP 5,000 to PHP 14,000 monthly, based on the position
• Step increment for length of service, awarded every 3 years of

• One of two-step increments for meritorious performance using

• Loyalty Cash Incentive based on years of service, starting in

5 years
• Productivity Enhancement Incentive of PHP 5,000 per year 
• Performance-Based Bonus (PBB): Annual bonus ranging from 
50 to 65 percent of the basic monthly salary depending on 
school performance
• Employment benefits and financial support specific to teachers

• World Teachers’ Day incentive of PHP 1,000 per year
• Proportional Vacation Pay equivalent to 70 days during

full service during the school year 
• Cash/Chalk Allowance of PHP 5,000 per year, subject to 
special provisions in the Government Appropriations Act

• Step Increment for Specializing in Teaching Science and/or

• In-service training (Program Support Fund)

• For mobile teachers and district alternative learning coordinators, 
PHP 5,000 per year for instructional materials and PHP 2,000 per 
month for transportation allowance 
• Additional incentive/allowances from local government units 
(select divisions) 
The financial rewards for public school teachers are substantial; 
however, they serve as compensation for the significant workload, which 
involves both teaching and nonteaching tasks (Esguerra 2018). Actual 
teaching is being sidelined by many nonteaching responsibilities and roles 
that teachers must fulfill (David et al. 2018b; David et al. 2019). 
Under the Magna Carta for Public School Teachers, each public 
school teacher has a regular full-time teaching load and is mandated to 
devote at most 6 hours of actual classroom instruction a day. Regular office 
hours only comprise 9 hours, but when teachers factor in the various 
administrative and student support roles, in addition to their teaching 
hours, they can become overwhelmed by the demands of the position. 
These support roles include writing reports on seminars and training they 
are required to attend, fulfilling designations related to student guidance, 
managing budgets, responding to disasters, and addressing health-related 
matters (David et al. 2019).
 Moreover, various government agencies looking to implement 
programs rely on the assistance of the DepEd, the schools, and                                                                                                                                              
teachers—particularly for programs that require direct access to large 
populations of children. Teachers are expected to participate in implementing 
programs such as community mapping, mass immunizations, deworming, 
school feeding, population census, conditional cash transfers, antidrug 
=== PAGE 133 ===
102
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
initiatives, and elections, among others. However, as teachers report, these 
nonteaching tasks are not included in their staffing patterns, as support 
and administrative staff are optional in public schools. In contrast, private 
schools employ nonteaching staff to perform administrative activities such 
as enrollment, registration, record keeping, guidance counseling, daily 
operations, and janitorial services; this arrangement allows the faculty to 
focus on teaching.
According to the teachers, they prefer to focus on their actual teaching 
responsibilities, spend more time with students, and apply what they have 
learned about differentiated teaching. Under these conditions, the main 
impediment to ensuring quality learning is the workload and not salaries. 
Furthermore, higher salaries will not create more time for them to devote 
to teaching.  
Implementation of the PBB in the basic 
education sector
The PBB is provided to all public sector employees in the Executive 
Department (Albert et al. 2021c; Albert et al. 2021d) as a means to 
motivate government workers and increase productivity. 4 To support 
the government’s reform agenda (EO 43, s. 2011), the PBB links monetary     
incentives to organization-wide performance, aligning individual personnel                           
a n d  t e a m - l e v e l  e ff o r t s  w i t h  a g e n c y - w i d e  t a r g e t s  a n d  i m p r o v i n g  p u b l i c                                                                                                                          
service delivery. The DepEd, like all government agencies in the Executive 
Department, is required to meet good governance conditions and performance 
targets and commitments to qualify for the PBB incentive. 
Focus group discussions and key informant interviews with teachers                 
and DepEd staff revealed that the PBB is generally viewed positively as           
it is associated with rewards. There is a consensus that, in theory, it is                                                                                        
meant to motivate improved performance and meet targets. In practice,                                                                     
however, the PBB system is flawed, easily manipulated, and can lead to 
increased inefficiency. Several participants also reported that the PBB 
unfairly rewards freeloaders (Torneo et al. 2017). During discussions 
4 When the PBB was first implemented in 2012, it was meant to cost-effectively deliver vast productivity 
improvements in the public sector bureaucracy in the context of broader results-based management 
reforms (EO 80, s. 2012; AO 25 s. 2011).

with teachers on transparency and fairness, allegations were raised about 
government staff “gaming” the system to obtain higher PBB incentives.                                                        
Some teachers claim that principals do not understand the PBB and fail to 
cascade information to their subordinates. Additionally, some teachers 
mentioned that they do not understand how schools are ranked or how 
they could improve their ranking. Furthermore, because the parameters                            
for small and large schools are the same, small schools have a better chance              
of receiving higher rankings or PBB payouts. As a result, the system can 
foster jealousy and unhealthy competition. 
Paperwork for the PBB is also assigned to teaching personnel,                                 
such as liquidation and other clerical tasks, which can compel them to 
deprioritize their teaching responsibilities. Some complain about the 
arbitrary dates for the release of PBB payouts; they have even coined the                
term PBB as “paasa buwan-buwan” (hoping for the best every month). 
Support for the PBB appears to be strong among public school 
teachers; however, studies argue that it is not fully effective and needs

Public school teachers and school heads/principals often perceive the 
PBB as a reward for taking on more work, rather than for improving                                                
the quality of their work (Albert et al. 2021c). Additionally, DepEd staff                      
noted that when targets focus on learning achievements and maintaining                                                    
zero dropouts, there is a tendency to manipulate outcomes. For instance, 
poorly performing students are encouraged to skip the National Achievement 
Test to secure better scores, or mass promotions may be implemented                      
to achieve zero dropouts and qualify for the PBB. Some principals 
reportedly take the path of least resistance by informing teachers that a 
“satisfactory” rating is sufficient to receive the PBB.
Unintended consequences of the PBB 
on the basic education system
At the end of the school year, each teacher reports the number of dropouts,              
and these data are aggregated at various levels (i.e., school, division, 
region) before being consolidated by the DepEd Central Office. This 
process allows the dropout rate for each class to be traced back to individual 
teachers, implying that they bear the responsibility for ensuring that 
students do not drop out. Across the education system, multiple layers of                                                                             
formal incentives, including the PBB, and institutionalized practices drive 
teachers to strive for zero dropouts.
A constructive approach to addressing this issue is for teachers to 
closely monitor students at risk of dropping out (SARDOs). This includes 
investigating the reasons behind chronic absenteeism, visiting students’ 
residences, and discussing various strategies with parents to help keep 
their children in school. If a student performs poorly in examinations                   
or repeatedly fails to attend classes, the teacher should not promote the                
child to the next grade level or may require the student to attend summer 
remedial classes. However, the teacher must report to the principal, 
providing an explanation and justification for the status of each SARDO. 
The reasons a student may fail to meet academic requirements are not 
always linked to poor teaching. During home visits, teachers often discover 
personal challenges that students face, many of which are economic in 
nature, such as hunger, the need to work to support their households, or 
responsibilities related to caregiving and domestic tasks—especially caring 
=== PAGE 136 ===
105
Public School Teachers, Incentives, and Learning Quality
for younger siblings. In some cases, siblings may take turns attending                                          
school due to the hidden costs of schooling. Teachers often empathize   
with these circumstances and may allow students to skip school and not 
submit academic requirements.
The unintended effect of the PBB, which results from linking 
incentives to the zero dropout target, was raised during interviews with 
teachers (Albert et al. 2021c; Albert et al. 2021d) and in separate research 
about out-of-school children (David et al. 2018b). The use of the dropout 
rate sends a problematic incentive signal to teachers, as they are evaluated 
based on their ability to achieve zero dropouts rather than the actual 
quality of student learning. Teachers may get the impression that the system 
prefers promoting children to the next school level rather than risking                                                                  
having the SARDOs leave school. Instead of disallowing promotion to 
the next grade level, teachers may promote nonperforming students                                     
to meet the zero dropout target, qualify for the PBB, and receive the 
attached incentives. 
As an unintended consequence of the PBB system and the zero 
dropout target, schools across the country have created the phenomenon 
of “mass promotion”, which further exacerbates the quality of education. 
Consequently, students end up in seventh grade without even knowing 
how to read for comprehension. This reading problem worsens throughout 
high school and higher education, with students exhibiting attitude and 
motivation issues due to their poor or undeveloped skills. SARDOs are 
mostly children failing their classes, not following the lessons, and having 
trouble understanding their books and passing their exams, which can 
be traced back to poor reading skills. They eventually lose motivation to 
stay in school because they spend the entire day sitting in class without 
understanding anything.
All these consequences are connected to the finding in national surveys 
conducted by the Philippine Statistics Authority that the reason why more 
than half of children of (lower) secondary age are currently not in school                      
is due to a “lack of interest” (David et al. 2018b). In the absence of                                                                   
clearer student performance-based measurements that can be traced back 
to the quality of teaching, dropout rates become the metric for evaluating 
teacher quality. At present, achievement test scores are not linked back                  
to individual teachers, nor are graduation rates, cohort survival rates,                                                
and enrollment rates. 
=== PAGE 137 ===
106
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Ways forward 
The Philippines must significantly improve the quality of its future labor 
force given the drastic changes in the nature of work stemming from 
increased digitalization and the use of other frontier technologies. The 
educational system should be better aligned with 21st-century needs. 
Rising to the challenge, the government has heavily invested in                                                                                                                             
the education sector. The number of public school teachers has nearly 
doubled in the last two decades, and the overall PTR is low. The budget for 
DepEd has increased to the point that teacher salaries and benefits packages 
are more competitive than those in the private sector. However, the                            
basic education system still faces issues regarding the quality of students’ 
learning. Moreover, DepEd needs to send stronger signals to all teachers 
and schools that practices like mass promotion should stop, especially as  
this practice runs contrary to the SDG4 aspiration to “ensure inclusive                                                                                                                                
and equitable quality education and promote lifelong learning opportunities                 
for all”.
Overburdened by responsibilities unrelated to teaching, teachers lack 
the time to ensure quality learning for their students. Even worse, incentive 
mechanisms fail to reward teachers based on the quality of teaching and 
create the unintended consequence of “mass promotion”, which further 
exacerbates the learning challenges faced by underperforming students. 
DepEd needs to examine its human resources shortages more 
systematically, looking into the time use of faculty and allowing teachers                         
to focus solely on teaching and developing students. The Department 
of Budget and Management should be enjoined to allocate resources to                                       
provide schools with administrative staff, including guidance counselors, 
who would relieve teachers of the bulk of nonteaching duties and 
responsibilities. In the interim, public schools can accept undergraduate 
students pursuing primary and secondary education programs to assist                                                                                                                       
with nonteaching tasks as part of their on-the-job training.

Chapter 5
Do We Have Enough 
School Infrastructure? 
Assessing Philippine 
School Infrastructure in 
Basic Education*1 
Adoracion M. Navarro
* This chapter is a condensed version of PIDS Research Paper Series 2024-06, titled “School 
Infrastructure in the Philippines: Where Are We Now and Where Should We Be Heading?”. The 
full study is available at https://doi.org/10.62986/rps2024.06. 
=== PAGE 143 ===
112
Introduction
This chapter assesses the current inventory of school infrastructure 
to highlight the issues that plague this component of the basic 
education sector—particularly spatial inequality in addressing classroom 
c o n g e s t i o n ,  f a i l u r e  t o  a c h i e v e  u n i v e r s a l  e l e c t r i fi c a t i o n  ( a  b a r r i e r  t o                                                                                                                                                
adopting digital technology for learning), low access to computers and 
the internet, and inadequate basic water and sanitation facilities. It also 
compares the school infrastructure indicators of the Philippines with those of                
its neighbors in East and Southeast Asia to provide a clearer understanding 
of where the gaps lie.
Broadly, physical infrastructure in the education sector includes 
buildings, classrooms, libraries, laboratories, furniture (e.g., desks, chairs, 
cabinets), and equipment, as well as facilities enabling access to electricity, 
water, sanitation, and internet connection. As reports on the basic education 
sector often use the term “school infrastructure” (e.g., World Bank 2016) 
to refer to these physical infrastructures, this chapter adopts the same 
terminology. However, the discussion here does not cover furniture and 
fixtures, libraries, playgrounds, sports facilities, and dormitories because 
comprehensive data are not available.
Why school infrastructure matters
The impacts of issues in the basic education sector extend far beyond 
individual student performance. For example, by the time a Filipino 
reaches working age, that individual should possess a level of education                                
and standard of health required to join the workforce. The level of education 
among the Filipino workforce will therefore impact productivity and 
the national economy. This link between the education and health of 
individuals and the national economy applies universally across the world. 
To establish a benchmark for comparative assessment between countries, 
the World Bank developed the Human Capital Index (HCI) to measure                                                    
“the amount of human capital that a child born today can expect to acquire                  
=== PAGE 144 ===
113
Do We Have Enough School Infrastructure? 
by age 18, given the risks of poor health and poor education that prevail 
in the country” (World Bank n.d.). The goal of countries is to achieve                              
the highest human capital potential, represented by a score of 1 in the HCI.
The Philippines got a score of 0.52 on the 2020 Human Capital 
Index (World Bank 2020), which implies that Filipino children born in 
2020 are expected to achieve only a little more than half their potential                                       
by the time they reach 18 years old. This dismal score becomes even                     
more alarming when we consider that the country scored 0.55 in 2018,                          
the first year of publication of the HCI (World Bank 2018). This underscores 
the need for the government and its development partners to radically                          
raise the country’s human capital potential. School infrastructure in the 
basic education sector will play a key role in this effort. 
The empirical literature has repeatedly confirmed that better school 
infrastructure is inextricably linked to better learning outcomes. Evidence 
from Latin America shows that providing basic standard infrastructure 
services positively affects test performance (Murillo and Román 2011). 
For specific services like electrification, data from 56 countries demonstrate 
a strong correlation between electricity access and the primary school 
completion rate (Sovacool and Ryan 2016). Moreover, studies worldwide 
demonstrate that electrification can compensate for teacher shortages                                                   
(Kirubi et al. 2009), promote gender equity (Gurung et al. 2011), 
and facilitate the functioning of other educational infrastructure such as 
information and communication technology (ICT) facilities (Welland 2017). 
Prior to the 2020 global pandemic, access to computers, other ICT                                                                                                                                       
devices, and the internet were seen as resources that enhance learning 
(Nketiah-Amponsah et al. 2017; Diaz et al. 2019). However, as the pandemic 
progressed, these resources became the primary vehicle for learning. 
Investment in ICT is no longer optional but has become essential in today’s 
education landscape.
As such, the government needs to invest considerably in quality                 
school infrastructure and enable equitable access to it to ensure that it 
ultimately improves education service delivery and learning environments. 
However, before discussing what needs to be done, understanding the 
status on the ground is necessary.
=== PAGE 145 ===
114
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Adequacy of school infrastructure
In an ideal scenario, school infrastructure should be able to preemptively 
address the demands of increasing enrollment numbers and structural 
damage caused by regular use or calamities. Unfortunately, the school 
building inventory indicates that the government is failing to meet the 
demands of the growing student population, as well as equity considerations                  
in the basic education sector. 
Data show that past school building programs have made progress in 
decongesting schools. The DepEd reported that as of the school year 2019–2020, 
the classroom-student ratio was 1:29 for elementary school, 1:39 for junior 
high school, and 1:31 for senior high school. 1 These ratios are within the 
Philippine Development Plan (PDP) target ratios of 1:30 for Grades 1-3 and 
1:40 for Grades 4-6, junior high school, and senior high school (NEDA 2021).
However, spatial inequality in the classroom-student ratio remains. 
Classroom overcrowding is evident in high classroom-student ratios in the 
National Capital Region (1:46 in elementary and 1:48 in junior high school), 
Region IV-A (1:48 in junior high school), Region XI (1:41 in junior high 
school), and the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao 
(1:40 in elementary, 1:54 in junior high school, and 1:48 in senior high school) 
(Navarro 2022).2 Moreover, classrooms in 9,225 schools in geographically 
isolated and disadvantaged areas do not meet quality and safety standards 
(DepEd-OUA 2020a).
Based on the 2019 National School Building Inventory, the DepEd 
estimated that 110,954 classrooms still need to be built to bridge the 
existing gap for SY 2019–2020. To keep pace with the increasing number of 
enrollments, the DepEd needs to build an additional 10,000 classrooms 
each year. Furthermore, structural assessments show that 28,508 school                                                                                                                                      
buildings pose a danger to students and need to be demolished and immediately 
replaced, implying an additional requirement of 85,524 classrooms                                                                                                                                
(DepEd-OUA 2020a). 
1 Based on email exchanges on school infrastructure indicators (updates on accomplishments) between the 
author and the Department of Education’s Educational Management Information System Division–Planning 
Service from February 10 to March 1, 2022.
2 The ratios are computed in Navarro (2022) using data from DepEd (2021a and 2021b).

Compounding this problem is the lack of electricity in some school 
buildings. While many countries in the East and Southeast Asia region 
have achieved universal access to electricity, the Philippines still struggles 
to achieve universal electrification in schools (Figure 1). As of 2020,                     
1,562 schools lack electricity access. 3 The electrical connections of                                                                                                                              
39,335 schools also need to be upgraded to stabilize electricity fluctuations     
and avoid fires (DepEd-OUA 2020b). The electrification issue also 
exacerbates the gaps in access to digital technology in Philippine schools.
3 Key informant interviews with DepEd personnel via personal communication with the author on 
October 21, 2021, and October 25, 2021.
=== PAGE 147 ===
116
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Universal or near-universal access to computers and the internet 
has already been achieved by many neighboring countries (e.g., Malaysia, 
Singapore, South Korea, Thailand, Viet Nam), but computer and internet 
access remains low in the Philippines (Navarro 2022). As of 2020, access                    
to computers for pedagogical purposes in the Philippines was 81.6 percent                    
at the elementary school level, 84.4 percent at the junior high school level, 
and 81.5 percent at the senior high school level. Internet access, on the 
other hand, was 64.2 percent at the elementary school level, 60.4 percent 
at the junior high school level, and 67.3 percent at the senior high school                                                                                                                                           
level.4 There were programs for the delivery of computer packages to 
public schools and for connecting these schools to the internet. However, 
the annual targets set by the PDP were not met. The nonachievement 
of targets suggests public investment problems, particularly in the 
implementation of the DepEd Computerization Program and the DepEd 
Internet Connectivity Project (Navarro 2022). 
School buildings also require water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) 
facilities to ensure that students learn in a clean and healthy environment and 
do not risk being infected with parasitic worms and waterborne diseases. 
However, despite the implementation of the DepEd’s comprehensive WASH                 
in Schools Program, there were still 3,861 schools (approximately 7% 
nationwide) without access to potable water in 2020, five years after the 
program was launched (DepEd-OUA 2021). Moreover, when benchmarked 
against East and Southeast Asian countries, the Philippines falls short 
in providing WASH facilities in schools. The country lags behind even 
when compared with neighboring countries that have lower per capita 
income, such as Myanmar, Cambodia, and Timor-Leste. With respect to 
access to drinking water and sanitation, access rates are higher in schools                                                                         
in Myanmar (75% basic drinking water access and 68% basic sanitation 
access), Cambodia (73% and 32%, respectively), and Timor-Leste (69% 
and 38%, respectively) than in schools in the Philippines (47% and 39%, 
4 Based on email exchanges on school infrastructure indicators (updates on accomplishments) 
between the author and the Department of Education’s Educational Management Information System                             
Division–Planning Service from February 10 to March 1, 2022.
=== PAGE 148 ===
117
Do We Have Enough School Infrastructure? 
respectively). For access to hygiene facilities, the access rates in schools in 
Timor-Leste (60%) and Myanmar (59%) are higher than in schools in the 
Philippines (54%) (Navarro 2022).5
Steps forward
Benchmarking against targets and comparing them with indicators from 
other countries highlighted the gaps in school infrastructure provision in 
the Philippine basic education sector. While progress has been made in 
decongesting classrooms, this progress is not consistent nationwide, and 
spatial inequality remains. Gaps in future-proofing school infrastructure 
also persist, as some classrooms do not meet basic quality and safety                                   
standards, WASH facilities are inadequate, and universal access to electricity              
and ICT services has not yet been achieved.
All these factors imply the need for increased investment in school 
infrastructure and the pursuit of policy improvements. Both the public 
and private sectors must assume responsibility for improving the students’ 
learning environments through better and adequate school infrastructure. 
This should be viewed as an investment that results in improved student 
learning outcomes today and higher productivity of workers in the future.
In addition, the COVID-19 global pandemic brought to light the need 
to adjust classroom standards to incorporate good ventilation and scale 
up efforts that support hygiene and disinfection. It also highlighted the 
importance of electricity access and ICT infrastructure to support distance 
learning and blended learning modes. 
Necessary policy responses must be identified. Within the DepEd, 
department-level policies on investment requirement programming, 
innovative financing modalities, and improved implementation approaches 
must be developed (Navarro 2022). Moreover, legislative proposals should 
aim to promote and fund inclusive approaches to addressing school 
infrastructure issues.
As the national government contemplates the effective implementation               
of the Supreme Court ruling on the Mandanas-Garcia case, initially 
5 Comparison using data from the Joint Monitoring Programme of the United Nations Children’s 
Fund and the World Health Organization (UNICEF and WHO 2020).
=== PAGE 149 ===
118
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
through Executive Order (EO 138, s. 2021), 6 addressing investment 
requirements through public sector spending becomes more complex                                                  
in coordinating the investment programming. 
To illustrate, the Basic Education Facilities budget of the DepEd decreased   
from the GAA 2021 allocation of PHP 11.15 billion7 to the GAA 2022 allocation                 
of PHP 5.95 billion. 8 The full devolution scheme under EO 138 (s. 2021) 
directs that functions contemplated by the Local Government Code of 1991                         
(RA 7160) be fully devolved to LGUs by the national government agencies                        
in the executive branch. In the case of the DepEd, the implementing                                                                                                                     
guidelines of EO 138 (s. 2021) provide that, based on the Local Government 
Code, public spending for the following shall be specifically devolved to 
municipalities: school buildings and other facilities for public elementary and 
secondary schools, and information services, which include the maintenance                                       
of public libraries. In addition, public spending for information and reading 
centers shall be devolved to barangays. 9 In a sense, EO 138 (s. 2021) already 
provides the policy for the utilization of the LGUs’ increased share in national 
taxes in a way that addresses school infrastructure needs. Its coverage 
should not be interpreted as limited to classroom construction given that 
the implementing guidelines cover “other facilities”. Going forward, policy 
improvements can be made in the areas of targeting, prioritization, equity, 
monitoring, and public accountability. (Navarro 2022, p.40-41)
There is no guarantee that the local government units (LGUs) will 
be able to prioritize school infrastructure, much less adequately cover 
the costs to address the gaps. With the national government reduced to 
providing oversight, geographic information system-based monitoring of 
school infrastructure needs vis-à-vis spending, results, outcomes, and gaps 
will be crucial for effective monitoring. Moreover, monitoring systems 
should use open access to properly track school infrastructure indicators.           
The DepEd’s Basic Education Information System (eBEIS), which operates                            
on a limited access basis, can be redesigned to allow open access to main 
6 President Marcos suspended the implementation of EO 138 s. 2021 in 2023. Nevertheless, he committed   
to issue amendments to it and implement the devolution with clearer parameters.
7 See the DepEd budget in Republic Act 11518 or the General Appropriations Act Fiscal Year 2021.
8 See the DepEd budget in Republic Act 11639 or the General Appropriations Act Fiscal Year 2022.

=== PAGE 150 ===
119
Do We Have Enough School Infrastructure? 
school infrastructure indicators so that it can be useful to policymakers, 
potential partners in the private sector, and the communities where the 
schools are located. 
In addition to the increased revenue allotment, LGUs can also access 
their Special Education Fund (SEF) to finance school infrastructure investments. 
Previously, the law (RA 7160) prescribing the collection of the SEF surcharge 
on real property tax was considered very restrictive when it comes to the use 
of the proceeds and, thus, legislative proposals were submitted to clarify and 
expand the coverage (e.g., Senate Bill 396 in the 18th Congress). However, it 
turned out that a department-level policy could substitute for the legislative 
proposal. Thus, Joint Circular 2 (s. 2020) was issued by the DepEd, DBM,                 
and the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) to clarify 
the allowed expense items, among other provisions. The permitted expense 
items under the operation and maintenance of public schools include external 
storage devices for digital files, communication expenses (including mobile 
phone load and Wi-Fi connection), subscription fees for remote applications 
or platforms, and health and sanitation expenses. The spending for the 
construction and repair of school buildings also includes the installation               
of health facilities such as health clinics and wash areas. Expenditures for 
facilities and equipment include personal computers and ICT devices. 10                                                                                                                         
This policy on expanding the coverage of SEF expense items should 
present an opportunity to engage more LGUs in accelerating public 
sector investments in school infrastructure. Possible policy improvements 
going forward should be in the areas of equity and monitoring. The 
equity aspect needs improvement because the size of the SEF of LGUs                                                                              
may not be commensurate with the needs of their constituents in the basic 
education sector. Since the SEF is a surcharge on the real property tax, 
high-income LGUs with high valuations of real properties receive higher 
SEF contributions, while low-income LGUs with low valuations of real 
properties receive lower SEF contributions. As an input to equity assessment, 
monitoring of needs, targets, accomplishments, results, and gaps should be 
undertaken (Navarro 2022, p.41-42).
Moreover, the national government should consider involving                       
the private sector through public-private partnerships (PPPs). In 2011, 
the PPP for the School Infrastructure Project, designed to build additional 
10 See DepEd-DBM-DILG Joint Circular 2 (s. 2020)
=== PAGE 151 ===
120
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
classrooms, was plagued by delays (Navarro 2022). However, learning from 
this experience, DepEd might better navigate a smoother implementation 
by ensuring that:
There is enough time for conducting site inspections and other preparation 
activities for site identification to avoid delays related to project site issues; 
improving the Site Appraisal Report by including information such as hazards, 
climate type, presence of obstructions on the site, setback requirements, 
possible security issues, distance and travel time from the town proper or distance 
from the port for island locations and other incidental works; incorporating 
clear contract provisions on lease payment, periodic maintenance works, 
and furniture and fixtures warranty; improving the minimum performance 
standards and specifications to ensure that all classrooms are conducive to 
learning; having a dedicated PPP unit in DepEd; ensuring close coordination 
among the DepEd regional offices, LGUs, and other stakeholders as early as 
during the project development stage; greater effort in marketing the PPP 
opportunities to encourage and increase bidder participation; ascertaining 
the payment mechanisms prior to the bidding proper and sharing relevant 
guidelines with the bidders; engaging the independent consultant early in the 
project procurement process and improving the evaluation and certification 
procedure of the independent consultant (DepEd and PPP Center 2020, p.7-8). 
Moreover, since ICT service providers primarily come from the 
private sector, a PPP approach to ICT for schools can also be explored. 
A study by Sarvi et al. (2015, p.2) provides examples of ICT services that are               
suitable for a PPP approach: 
Providing connectivity; providing centrally managed systems; providing               
fit-for-purpose online systems; providing ICT hardware and software; 
managing online professional development systems; delivering preservice 
and in-service professional development opportunities and support personnel;                  
setting up and running online communities of practice; providing access 
to repositories of digital knowledge; making educational resources and 
tools electronically accessible; and providing access to online and distance       
learning courses. 
However, PPP does not always guarantee success, especially if 
the terms of the partnership distort incentives and lead to unintended 
consequences. The government must therefore conduct a comprehensive 
=== PAGE 152 ===
121
Do We Have Enough School Infrastructure? 
assessment of the needs and options for investment and financing prior                                  
to adopting any models that utilize the PPP approach. Notably, the openness 
to this approach is reflected in the proposed bill on the Public Schools                         
of the Future in Technology Act (Senate Bill 516 in the 19th Congress),                                                                                                                       
which includes provisions for PPP models for establishing partnerships       
with nonprofit entities, civil society, the business and industrial sector,                 
and other concerned sectors. 
Partnerships with the private sector can also occur through their 
corporate social responsibility (CSR) activities. An example of a successful 
CSR initiative is the Gearing Up Internet Literacy and Access for                    
Students (GILAS) program, which was initiated by a consortium led by 
the Ayala Foundation. The GILAS program provided computer packages, 
internet peripherals, and training support to public high schools for                     
six years until it was turned over to the DepEd in 2011 (Ronda 2011). 
Policies that liberalize access to markets can also accelerate private 
sector participation in ICT for education. For example, EO 127 s. 2021 
allows value-added service and internet service providers to tap satellite 
services to build and operate broadband facilities. This broadens the 
options available to schools and students, which were previously limited 
to telecommunication companies. Furthermore, the proposed bills on open 
access in data transmission could establish policies that allow for data 
transmission infrastructure sharing and co-location. 
Investments in water supply and sanitation facilities in schools can 
also be promoted as a worthwhile undertaking in CSR activities or as part                
of the environmental, social, and governance scorecards of private firms. 
In the final analysis, the developmental imperative to contribute to 
improving students’ learning environments through better and adequate 
school infrastructure is a shared responsibility of the government and the 
private sector. After all, any resource spent on building a good learning 
environment is an investment in better student learning outcomes today, 
which will result in higher productivity of workers in the future. This is 
an important component of human capital development, which in turn is 
an important driver of endogenous economic growth. The fact that the 
Philippines’ Human Capital Index has deteriorated in recent years means 
that we have not been investing enough in human capital development. 
Recognizing this is the first step in reversing the deterioration; taking 
urgent action is the next one.

=== PAGE 157 ===

Introduction 
A national survey commissioned by the National Economic and Development 
Authority (NEDA) was conducted in 2015 to learn about Filipinos’ 
aspirations, values, and principles. In this AmBisyon Natin 2040 survey, 
nearly all respondents who either have or intend to have children expressed 
that their children’s education is important for attaining their ambitions or                                                                    
plans in life. Ninety percent of these respondents were confident that their 
( f u t u r e )  c h i l d r e n  w o u l d  fi n i s h  t h e i r  s t u d i e s .  H o w e v e r ,  8 6  p e r c e n t  i n d i c a t e d                                                                                                                    
that insufficient livelihood or family income might pose a potential challenge 
to achieving their educational dreams (NEDA 2016).  
Across the world, the public sector plays an important role in 
providing education services. This is warranted for at least two reasons. 
First, education is known to yield many positive benefits that extend from 
individuals and households to society at large. These benefits include 
increasing incomes (e.g., Acemoglu and Angrist 2000), lowering crime rates 
(e.g., Lochner and Moretti 2004), and breaking intergenerational poverty 
transmissions (e.g., Duarte et al. 2017).1 Without guidance,  households that 
are unaware or indifferent to the impact of education on society and future 
generations are likely to underinvest in it. Second, concerns for equity 
may also drive public spending on education. In particular, paternalistic 
governments may establish minimum social protection guarantees for 
primary social goods, including education (c.f. Rawls 1971; Gutmann 1980),                
to which everyone is entitled and even compelled to consume. 
That being said, both households and governments spend substantial 
amounts on education. Recent basic education reforms in the Philippines, 
particularly through the passage of Republic Act 10533 or the Enhanced 
Basic Education Act of 2013, along with expanding school-aged population 
and increasing household incomes, have contributed to higher education 
spending in the country. In 2019, Filipino households spent a total of                
PHP 753 billion on education (PSA 2021), while the national government 
1 See McMahon (2004) for a review.
=== PAGE 158 ===
127
If You Pay Peanuts, You Get Monkeys? 
spent PHP 655 billion 2 (DBM 2021). These amounts are more than double            
the combined spending levels from 10 years prior. 
Beyond similar within-country analyses, little is known about how 
the Philippines fares in comparison with its regional and aspirational 
peers with regard to education spending. The lack of available comparable 
international education financing data has effectively hampered cross-country 
appraisals and benchmarking, which would have been beneficial for 
policymaking. Several countries have estimated National Education 
Accounts3 (NEA) to complement their System of National Accounts (SNA). 
However, global education spending data are largely confined to those 
based on public administrative records, which only provide a partial view 
of the entire education sector’s financing. Even with public sector records, 
only about 60 percent of countries report data on total public education 
expenditures, and there is often a considerable time lag among reporting 
countries (UNESCO 2016b).
This study draws from recent National Transfer Account (NTA) 
estimates for the Philippines and countries worldwide to uncover and 
d e s c r i b e  t r e n d s  i n  g l o b a l  e d u c a t i o n  fi n a n c i n g ,  w i t h  a  p a r t i c u l a r  f o c u s  o n                                                                                                   
the Philippines as a case study. The NTA is a system of accounts that 
measures how different generations produce, consume, and reallocate 
resources in a manner consistent with the United Nations (UN) SNA                                
(UN 2013; Lee and Mason 2011). Earlier studies (e.g., Mason and Lee 2011; 
Tung 2011; Abrigo et al. 2018) used NTA to characterize human capital 
investments, including both education and health, and found a wide 
variation in spending patterns across economies. 
This chapter focuses on three critical questions about education 
financing. First, how much are countries, particularly the Philippines, 
spending on education? Second, what drives the differences in spending 
levels? Finally, do education spending levels matter? Providing evidence-based

answers to these questions may help guide future policy directions and 
open new research on education financing using alternative data sources,

How much are Filipinos spending 
on education?
Over the last 15 years, total education expenditures in the Philippines have 
increased by about 6.4 percent annually, with a significant portion of this 
growth occurring in recent years. In 2019, the total current education 
bill—excluding capital outlay—for all school levels reached almost 
PHP 1.2 trillion (in 2012 prices), up from PHP 0.8 trillion in 2015 and 
PHP 0.5 trillion in 2005 (Table 1). During this period, households bore 
the majority of the expenses, reaching as high as 59.3 percent in 2005; 
however, increasing government expenditures contributed to a decline in 
the household share, which settled at 54.5 percent in 2019.

Education spending has also been increasing as a share of the country’s 
gross domestic product (GDP). By 2019, education spending in the Philippines 
represented 7.5 percent of GDP, a considerable increase from the recorded 
5.8 percent share in 2005. Over the same period, public sector spending 
on education increased from 2.1 to 3.1 percent of the GDP 4―an important 
achievement, yet still falling short of the benchmark of 4–6 percent set by 
the Education 2030 Incheon Declaration. 
The Philippine Constitution guarantees the highest budgetary 
priority to education. 5 In 2019, actual education spending by the national 
government reached 18.1 percent of its total expenditures. Programmed 
and proposed education spending for 2020 and 2021 both amounted to

15–20 percent thresholds suggested by the Education 2030 Incheon 
Declaration for this metric. 
Among government units, basic education expenditures by the 
Department of Education (DepEd) accounted for about 75–80 percent 
of total current public education spending between 2005 and 2019. In 
comparison, public higher education, represented by state universities 
and colleges (SUCs) and the Commission on Higher Education (CHED), 
comprised about 11–17 percent. Public technical and vocational education 
through the Technical Education and Skills Development Authority (TESDA) 
captured about 1–2 percent. Finally, local governments, which fund various 
education and manpower training services, together with museums, education 
institutions in other government offices, and other education-related 
institutions, represented roughly 5–10 percent.
Compared with other countries in Asia and the Pacific region,                     
the Philippines’ public spending per student in primary and secondary 
education levels lags behind its regional and aspirational peers (Table 2). 
While per-student public spending is strongly correlated with per capita 
income, the Philippines spends only about 60–72 percent of Indonesia’s 
per-student public spending for primary and secondary education levels, 
respectively, even though the Philippines’ per capita income is 84 percent 
of Indonesia’s for the years presented. Singapore, which leads the countries 
presented, spends―in terms of international dollars in purchasing power

An important issue with relying solely on public expenditures to

An alternative to NEA is NTA. Similar to NEA, NTA is an accounting 
framework consistent with the UN-SNA. Unlike NEA, which is designed 
specifically as a satellite education account to the SNA (UNESCO 2016a), 
NTA elaborates on the SNA to capture economic flows across age groups 
(UN 2013). There are important conceptual differences between the two 
frameworks. First, NTA records the value of consumption, i.e., expenditures 
less taxes net of subsidies on products, while NEA includes taxes as part of 
expenditures. Second, the main economic agents in NTA are the generations 
or age groups in an economy, whereas in NEA, they are the private and 
public sectors. In NTA, the public and private sectors are viewed primarily 
as mediators through which economic resources flow. Third, NEA has a 
more elaborate treatment of economic flows related to   education, while 
in NTA this is confined to age-specific public and private consumption 
and the related resources financing that consumption. Fourth, although 
it is possible to disaggregate NTA education consumption by levels of 
education, as in NEA, this is seldom reported, and only aggregates by age  
are usually available. 6
A key advantage of NTA over NEA, however, is that NTA is more 
widely estimated, although it is not particularly suited for comparing 
cross-country education spending. In 2021, NTA was estimated for more 
than 90 countries, representing over 80 percent of the world’s population. 
In addition, modeling exercises have allowed for the estimation of NTAs              
for the remaining countries around the world (e.g., Mason et al. 2017). 
Figure 1 presents estimates of per capita NTA public and private 
education consumption in the Philippines for age groups 5 to 24 years 
between 1990 and 2015. 7 These values capture age-specific differences 
in school attendance and schooling costs, as well as various factors that                        
affect these variables. Public education consumption includes government 
expenses on the direct provision of education services in public institutions, 
excluding capital outlays. Private education consumption, on the other hand, 
includes expenses for education supplies procured by the private sector, 
tuition and other fee payments, and transportation costs related to education, 
6 Estimates of age-specific NTA education consumption by education level are typically available to 
NTA country team compilers as part of the NTA estimation process, but these are excluded in the 
standard template reported in the global database.
7 For a discussion of the quinquennial Philippine NTA between 1990 and 2015, see Abrigo et al. (2020).            
For a discussion of the NTA estimation methodology, please refer to the UN NTA Manual (UN 2013). 
=== PAGE 163 ===
132
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
regardless of whether a student is enrolled in a public or private educational 
institution. These accounts also include nonschooling but education-related 
consumption, such as general public education, maintenance of museums 
and cultural centers, and costs of books not specific to a schooling level. 
There are notable patterns in per capita education consumption over 
the 25 years covered in Figure 1. First, per capita education consumption 
up to around age 16 hovers at around PHP 10,000 (in 2012 prices),                          
although there is a significant drop in 2005. Second, per capita education 
consumption begins to increase rapidly after age 16, peaking around age 20, 
reflecting the relative costs of higher education compared to basic education,                                            
and then tapers off as students exit formal education. The peak appears 
to be shifting to the right over the years. Third, public consumption                   
dominates at younger ages, particularly in primary and secondary school, 
while private consumption becomes predominant at later ages, especially 
in tertiary education and beyond. Fourth, public education consumption                
per capita has flattened, potentially indicating increased school attendance 
rates at higher grade levels in more recent years.

Focusing on basic education, Figure 2 presents total education 
consumption up to age 16 as a share of labor income. 8 While this measure 
may be unconventional, 9 it offers a particularly attractive interpretation. 
The values shown in Figure 2 can be viewed as the tax rate on labor 
income needed to finance basic education if it were to be fully funded by 
the workforce. 
Basic education consumption as a share of labor income varies 
widely among the 73 economies represented in Figure 2. It ranges from     
less than one percent in Guinea Bissau in 2010 to 15.7 percent in Mexico in                                      
the same year. In 2015, the Philippines spent 3.7 percent of labor income 
on basic education, slightly ahead of Vietnam’s 3.5 percent in 2012. Other 
countries in Asia and the Pacific region spent significantly larger shares: 
Singapore (4.7%), Japan (6.0%), Thailand (8.3%), South Korea (9.2%), 
Malaysia (10.1%), and Indonesia (10.5%). 
The division between public and private spending also varies 
substantially across countries. In Asia and the Pacific region, Vietnam 
and Cambodia stand out with less than 25 percent of their basic education 
consumption financed by the government. Other countries in the region rely 
more heavily on public funding. In Thailand and Singapore, in particular, 
more than 80 percent of basic education consumption is financed by the 
government. Meanwhile, financing for basic education in the Philippines is 
roughly equally shared between the public and private sectors.10 
The substantial share of the private sector in education financing 
is surprising given the dominant role of governments in providing basic 
education services, as evidenced by school enrollment rates. In East Asia 
and the Pacific, only about one-tenth of primary school pupils and one-fifth                   
of secondary school students are enrolled in private schools (UNESCO 2021).       
This may indicate that complementary spending by the private sector, 
primarily by households, is significant, even with a large public education 
sector, which is reflected as private consumption in the NTA. While 
educational instruction may be funded by the government, other important 
educational inputs, such as school supplies, may be financed privately.

The metric summarized in Figure 2 is based on the aggregate basic 
education consumption of countries at a specific time and depends not only 
on spending per capita but also on the distribution of school-age children.                 
Thus, the estimates are not directly comparable; an economy with a larger 
school-age population will appear to spend more as a share of labor income 
compared to a comparable economy with the same education spending              
and labor income per capita but with a smaller school-age population.
Figure 3 presents an alternative indicator based on a synthetic 
cohort. The figure shows the expected cumulative per capita public and 
private education consumption of an individual who would have lived up                                                                                                                                 
to age 16, assuming they encountered the particular schedule of per capita 
consumption for the year of the NTA estimate. A higher (or lower) value 
indicates greater (or lesser) spending on basic education consumption                                 
throughout the entire basic education cycle. The values are converted into 
2017 international dollars (in PPP dollars) to further facilitate comparison.11  
Over a span of 25 years, the Philippines’ cumulative basic education 
consumption almost trebled, although much of the growth happened more 
recently. Between 1990 and 2010, cumulative basic education consumption 
increased from PPP$ 3,390 to PPP$ 6,430, or about 3.3 percent per year.               
In the following five years, this measure grew by 6.3 percent annually to                  
PPP$ 8,720 by 2015. Notably, these growth rates are significantly higher than 
the annual increase in per capita GDP, which was 1.7 percent between 1990 
and 2010 and 4.3 percent between 2010 and 2015. Throughout this period, 
the Philippines’ private and public sectors contributed approximately 
equal shares to finance basic education.
Similar to the insights from Figure 2, Cambodia and Vietnam              
continue to rely more on the private sector compared to other countries in 
the region, as illustrated by the synthetic cohort measure of basic education 
consumption in Figure 3. Moreover, and more importantly, Figure 3 
indicates that public spending does not crowd out private spending on 
basic education; private spending increases by about 0.4 percent for every

The government’s share in cumulative basic education consumption 
varies considerably among lower-income countries, spanning almost the  
full range from 0 to 100 percent. However, the distribution narrows, 
and the average share increases with higher per capita GDP. Similar 
observations of this “financing transition” have been observed in health 
spending (e.g., Fan and Savedoff 2014), human capital investments                                                      
(e.g., Abrigo et al. 2018), and consumption in general (Peacock and 
Wiseman 1961).
What drives education spending?
Unless there is widespread child labor or capital markets allow loans to 
children, basic education consumption is primarily financed through 
government tax-transfer systems or through inter vivos transfers by 
older generations, especially family members. Motivations for such

=== PAGE 169 ===

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
intergenerational transfers have been well studied.13 Earlier theoretical work 
was based on parents deriving satisfaction from their children’s consumption 
(e.g., Barro 1974; Becker 1974), which was later expanded to allow for other 
possible motivations for intergenerational transfers, including parents 
benefiting from investments in their children (e.g., Becker and Murphy 1988; 
Becker 1991). 
Two important empirical studies in the Philippines exemplify these 
lines of thought. Estudillo et al. (2001a, 2001b) documented sex-differentiated 
giving norms in agrarian settings, with male children typically inheriting land 
while female children are compensated with greater education investments.            
A study by Yamauchi and Tiongco (2013), on the other hand, showed that              
the greater propensity of parents to receive future financial support from 
their female children may be an alternative driver of differential education 
investments in children. 
Although it may not be possible to disentangle these competing 
motivations using estimates of representative populations based on NTA, 
Figure 5 shows that cumulative basic education consumption increases with 
per capita income (Pearson’s correlation coefficient,    =0.96), and decreases 
with fertility rate (  =0.88). These observations align with the predictions 
of Becker’s (1960) classic child quality-quantity tradeoff theory and are 
supported by household-level empirical evidence, such as in the Philippines 
(e.g., Orbeta 2009; Yamauchi and Tiongco 2013). It is interesting to note 
that public and private cumulative basic education consumptions, separately, 
have lower correlation coefficients when compared to per capita income  
and fertility rate. 
Between 1990 and 2015, the Philippines appeared to be spending 
about the cumulative basic education consumption of the average 
country for the specified per capita income. However, for its fertility                                                         
rate, the Philippines is spending significantly below the average country, 
especially in more recent years. In 2015, for instance, the Philippines spent 
at a rate of only about half of the expected cumulative basic education 
consumption of PPP$ 18,350 (standard error = PPP$ 1,860) for a country 
with a total fertility rate of about 2.8 births per woman.
13 See Arrondel and Masson (2006) and Laferre and Wolff (2006) for a comprehensive discussion.

Does spending level matter?
Greater education spending does not automatically lead to better schooling 
outcomes. For instance, a series of papers by Hanushek (1981, 1986, 1989) 
found no strong relationship between expenditures and student achievements. 
However, more recent studies suggest some positive associations not only 
with schooling outcomes but also with certain later-life welfare measures 
(e.g., Card and Krueger 1992; Gupta et al. 2002; Baldacci et al. 2003;      
Huang et al. 2021). Other important factors in explaining schooling success 
include family background (e.g., Plug and Vijverberg 2003) and rent-seeking             
in government (e.g., Suryadarma 2012). 
Figure 6 presents scatter plots of cumulative basic education 
consumption up to age 15 and the corresponding country-average scores 
in the 2018 Program for International Student Assessment (PISA) by the 
Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD 2019). 
PISA is an international standardized test that evaluates the performance of 
15-year-old students in science, mathematics, and reading. The presented

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
cumulative basic education consumption values are modeled estimates 
using per capita GDP, total fertility rate, and government share in final 
consumption expenditure as predictors. This approach allows for the 
inclusion of all 77 economies in the 2018 PISA rather than just the 33 that 
overlap with NTA country estimates. The results are qualitatively similar 
whether using the 33 or the 77 data points.
In line with findings by Schleicher (2019), who used a different 
methodology, Figure 6 shows that average schooling quality increases 
with cumulative basic education consumption. The rate of change is 
much larger at lower levels of spending and gradually flattens with higher 
expenditures. 14 This observation applies to science, mathematics, and 
reading scores. While this association may not be interpreted as causal,                       
it suggests that greater resources might be required to improve schooling 
quality, especially in resource-poor settings. 
The figure also indicates that better schooling quality can be achieved 
with the same level of resources. For example, Belarus and Bosnia and 
Herzegovina both spend about PPP$ 50,000 in cumulative education 
consumption up to age 15, yet their average PISA scores differ by about                  
70 points. This suggests that some education systems may be more effective   
at converting educational inputs into outputs. 
This idea is formalized in Figure 7, which shows the estimated 
technical efficiency (TE) scores among different education systems in 
the 2018 PISA. The TE scores are estimated using a multi-output—in 
this case, PISA scores by subject—stochastic frontier model (Aigner et 
al. 1977; Meeusen and Van den Broeck 1977), with cumulative education 
consumption up to age 15 as the only input. 15 The TE scores compare                          
the distance of a country from a theoretical frontier based on a specified 
education production function. A TE score of 100 percent indicates that                       
a country is at the frontier, while lower TE scores imply some inefficiency                                   
in converting inputs to outputs relative to the production possibility                                                                                                                
frontier. Two sets of estimates were provided; in one set, China (represented                
by Beijing, Shanghai, Jiangsu, and Zhejiang) was excluded from the 
estimation sample to assess robustness against outliers.
14 Using the 33 countries with NTA data overlap, the fitted line is positively sloped until about                    
PPP$ 80,000, after which it flattens. The output elasticities are around 0.12 for each of the test subjects.
15 See Annex A for details.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
TE scores vary widely among the 77 PISA countries. With China 
included in the estimation sample, TE scores range from 56.6 percent to                       
93.9 percent. However, excluding China leads to upward revisions in TE 
scores across countries, with scores now ranging from 75.0 percent to             
99.4 percent. The Philippines scores lower than other East and Southeast 
Asian countries included in the 2018 PISA, although its TE score in the                    
no-China set is a more acceptable 86.5 percent. If the Philippines had fully 
maximized the use of its inputs, it should have scored closer to 400 points 
in each of the test subjects, instead of the observed range of 340 to 360.
 
Ways forward 
The analyses presented in this chapter highlight important trends in 
Philippine education financing. First, a synthetic measure of basic education 
consumption reveals that education spending per person in the country 
has grown robustly over the past 25 years, even outpacing recorded GDP 
per person during the same period. Second, the private sector contributes 
nearly as much as the government does to finance basic education. Third,               
while the Philippines spends at the average level for its per capita GDP, it 
allocates substantially less for its level of fertility. Fourth, despite the robust 
growth in spending per person, the country’s basic education spending                 
still lags behind its regional and aspirational peers, contributing to its poor 
performance in international standardized student assessments. Finally, 
while the Philippines falls short in education spending per capita, there 
may be opportunities to enhance schooling quality by optimizing the 
translation of inputs into outputs.
Although increasing available education resources may be desirable 
for improving schooling quality, this study’s cross-country comparison 
indicates that achieving this may be challenging, as per capita education 
spending is closely linked to a country’s economic development and fertility 
levels. Raising education spending per capita may therefore entail more 
than simply rallying resources for the education sector; it also requires 
ensuring that robust economic opportunities are available to improve   
average household incomes and assisting households achieve their desired 
fertility levels.
=== PAGE 176 ===
145
If You Pay Peanuts, You Get Monkeys? 
Poor schooling quality need not be the inevitable outcome of 
subpar education spending levels. A more critical and arguably more                                                  
urgent challenge for the government is to identify and scale cost-effective 
education interventions that better translate resource inputs into desired 
education outcomes.

Appendix A. Stochastic frontier estimation
Stochastic frontier models (Aigner et al. 1977; Meeusen and Van den 
Broeck 1977) are used to estimate and analyze technical (in)efficiency 
based on firm production theory. A firm, in these case countries, is assumed 
to produce a set of outputs, in this case, PISA test scores, using a common 
technology given some production inputs, in this case, cumulative basic 
education consumption. A firm is perfectly efficient when it produces 
the maximum possible output for the input it uses (i.e., when it is at the 
production possibility frontier).
The author estimated a multi-output, single-input stochastic frontier 
model using the following linearized production function:
ln(𝑦𝑦𝑦𝑦𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖) = ln(𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖) ⋅ 𝛽𝛽𝛽𝛽 + 𝛾𝛾𝛾𝛾 + (𝑣𝑣𝑣𝑣𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖 − 𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖)

where    is the column vector of PISA scores by test subject for country                                                                                                                                          
   =1,2,…,77, and     is its cumulative basic education consumption up to age 15,                 
a scalar. The conformable vector   refers to production elasticities that relate 
to a unit of input     to output    . The vector    is a subject-specific shifter that 
captures differences in production technology across test subjects. The model                                                                
residual is composed of two parts. The country- and test-specific vector     is 
assumed to follow a normal distribution with zero mean and variance    , 
and capture idiosyncratic shocks on production. In the general case, the 
country-specific vector    is assumed to follow some non-negative random 
distribution, which when exponentiated ranges between zero and one and 
provides an estimate of production efficiency, with a value of one indicating 
production at the frontier. In the model,    is assumed to be distributed as                           
half-normal           , and common across test subjects for a given country.
Two sets of models based on the above specification were estimated. 
All countries were included in the baseline model. In a second model, data 
for China was removed to assess the robustness of estimates to outliers.                        
As shown in Figure 7, the statistical ordering of technical efficiency scores 
across countries although magnitudes differ between specifications. Table A1 
provides a summary of the parameters of our estimated stochastic frontier models.
