=== PAGE 185 ===
154
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
In the previous decade, the Philippine basic educational system underwent a 
significant reform: transitioning from K-10 to K-12. This “new” educational 
system for Filipinos introduced senior high school (SHS), which includes 
Grades 11 and 12, as a stepping stone for both university education and 
immediate employment. The institutionalization of K-12 stemmed from 
empirical evidence that the 10-year basic education structure mainly 
contributed to the low mastery and academic performance among Filipino 
students (SEAMEO 2011). Extending basic education to 12 years was the 
result of nine decades of recommendations from studies 1 (dating back 
to the American colonial period) stating that 10 years of basic education 
inadequately prepares students for real life. Amid a polarized debate, 
Republic Act (RA) 10533 (Enhanced Basic Education Act) was enacted                                                                   
in 2013, mandating the implementation of the K-12 system starting 
Academic Year (AY) 2016–2017. The rollout of the SHS system led to a                                             
two-year transition period without university freshmen from AY 2016–2017                    
to AY 2017–2018. The early years of K-12 implementation were challenging                
for the basic and tertiary educational sectors. 
Graduates of the K-12 system can enter immediate employment                           
or pursue self-employment without needing to obtain bachelor’s degrees. 
This development aligns with the introduction of the Philippines 
Qualifications Framework (PQF), which was implemented simultaneously 
with the K-12 educational system. Whether K-12 graduates opt to enter the 
workforce immediately or pursue university education, the 12-year educational 
system aims to provide students with sufficient time to master concepts                 
and skills, become lifelong learners, and prepare for tertiary education, 
middle-level skills development, employment, and entrepreneurship. 
Studies suggest that completing 12 years of basic education can lead to higher 
incomes and increased wages for graduates (Card 1999; Psacharopoulos and 
Patrinos 2004; Heckman et al. 2006; ADB 2014). 
1 See Monroe (1925), Prosser survey (1930), UNESCO (1949), Swanson (1960), PCSPE (1970), ADB and 
World Bank (1999), and PCER (2000).
=== PAGE 186 ===
155
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
The K-12 approach may have enhanced students’ educational 
qualifications and employability. Thus, this chapter examines: 
• The initial years of the K-12 program’s implementation (in terms    
of program theory, service delivery, and utilization). 
• The alignment of the SHS curriculum and learning competencies 
with labor market needs. 
• The views of students and employers regarding K-12 graduates’ 
employability. 
• The employment outcomes of the initial cohort of K-12 graduates.
This chapter summarizes three studies by the Philippine Institute of 
Development Studies (PIDS): a process evaluation (Brillantes et al. 2019), 
a qualitative study (Orbeta et al. 2018), and an analysis of pertinent Labor 
Force Survey data (Orbeta and Potestad 2020). These studies, along with 
other research compiled in this compendium, highlight the importance of 
determining the processes, impacts, and outcomes of this major transition in 
the Philippine educational system. Combining these three specific studies 
showcases the education-to-employment nexus, particularly due to the 
K-12 program. Furthermore, these studies complement each other in 
assessing the earliest outcomes of the K-12 program.
Before discussing the specifics of the studies, it is important to  
provide context for the education reform. RA 10533 aims to improve the 
Philippine education system by strengthening the curriculum and extending 
the duration of basic education from 10 to 12 years. The desired outcome of                                                        
RA 10533 is to produce locally and globally competitive Filipinos. Based      
on the Implementing Rules and Regulations of RA 10533, the Enhanced                                                                                                  
Basic Education Program consists of the following elements:
• Strengthening early childhood education (universal kindergarten)
• Making the curriculum relevant to learners (contextualization 
and enhancement)
• Ensuring integrated and seamless learning (spiral progression)
• Building proficiency through mother tongue-based multilingual 
education
• Gearing up for the future (senior high school)
• Nurturing holistically developed Filipinos (college and livelihood 
readiness, 21st-century skills)
=== PAGE 187 ===
156
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
SHS covers the last two levels of the Enhanced Basic Education 
Program. The SHS curriculum aims to produce holistically developed 
students who are equipped with 21st-century skills and prepared for the 
future. This curriculum supports students whether they decide to pursue 
higher education, attain middle-level skills, seek employment, or enter 
entrepreneurship. It is divided into two main components: (1) core subjects 
and (2) track subjects that are grouped into contextualized tracks and 
specialized strands.
The following sections discuss the research questions, methods, 
major results, and findings of each of these studies. The chapter concludes 
with the major recommendations from the three studies, spanning the 
education-to-employment nexus.  
Status of senior high school implementation: 
A process evaluation
Research objectives and methods
Brillantes et al. (2019) assessed the implementation status of the SHS program 
through a process evaluation, focusing on the following components: 
(1) program theory, (2) service delivery and utilization, and (3) program 
organization. The program theory was assessed by determining the need 
that the program addresses, the critical assumptions in formulating the 
objectives, the logic and plausibility of attaining the program goals, and 
the sufficiency of preparations and resources for achieving these goals. 
Meanwhile, service delivery and utilization were assessed in terms of the 
program’s reach, stakeholder satisfaction, and implementation challenges. 
Lastly, program organization was examined based on the adequacy and 
use of resources (human, financial, physical), the quality of operational 
procedures, the external support received, and other organizational 
concerns that support program implementation. The goal of the process                                                   
evaluation is to unveil implementation issues, showcase best practices,  
and recommend appropriate interventions that can improve the execution                
of the SHS program.
The study was conducted from May to November 2018, using key 
informant interviews (KIIs) and focus group discussions (FGDs) with different 
=== PAGE 188 ===
157
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
stakeholders. A review of official program documents and analysis of 
available administrative data supplemented these methods.
The identified respondents for the KIIs and FGDs included program 
designers, policymakers, and implementers from different levels of the 
Department of Education (DepEd) such as (a) department officials (former 
and current secretaries and undersecretaries), (b) bureau representatives,                         
(c) field office representatives (SHS focal persons from regional and 
schools division offices), and school implementers (school heads, SHS focal                                         
persons, and teachers). On the program beneficiaries’ side, respondents 
included Grades 11 and 12 students and their parents. KII and FGD 
participants came from a roster of 25 schools across 13 provinces and 4 cities                    
i n  L u z o n ,  t h e  V i s a y a s ,  a n d  M i n d a n a o .  O v e r a l l ,  t h e  t e a m  c o n d u c t e d                                                                                                                              
93 KIIs—50 in schools and 43 with department representatives, regional 
offices, and division offices across the three island clusters. Additionally, 
106 FGDs were conducted with 248 SHS teachers and nonteaching 
staff, 261 Grade 11 students, 253 Grade 12 students, and 255 parents as 
participants (total N=1,017). 
Secondary data from the DepEd administrative reporting system 
were processed to provide a comprehensive quantitative background 
for the KII and FGD results. This analysis provided comprehensive 
information on the supply side, including the number and distribution of 
schools and their track offerings across various locations. This enabled a 
quantitative assessment of the extent of track offerings across regions.
Study findings 
The assessment revealed several notable gains in the program. Firstly, 
enrollment exceeded expectations. The DepEd anticipated only 1 million 
enrollees but received approximately 200,000 more, indicating a higher 
transition rate from Grade 10 to Grade 11 compared to the previous transition 
from fourth-year high school to college. Two other factors contributed to 
the high continuation rate among Grade 10 graduates: Many out-of-school 
youth returned to school, and graduates of the Alternative Learning System 
continued their studies. Preliminary data also indicated high graduation 
rates among the first batch of SHS students. 
Secondly, stakeholders provided anecdotal evidence that graduates, 
particularly from the technical-vocational-livelihood (TVL) track, found 
=== PAGE 189 ===
158
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
employment after graduation, which is one of the desired outcomes for SHS 
graduates. Thirdly, program implementers successfully forged partnerships 
with different sectors, including a local government unit (LGU) that 
established the only Arts and Design school in Region 8 and collaborated                
with the private sector to develop curricula for specialized strands. Fourthly,                  
the program has become a venue for public and private partnerships 
through initiatives like the SHS voucher program and the Joint Delivery 
Voucher Program for Technical-Vocational-Livelihood (JDVP-TVL). The 
SHS voucher program enables students who cannot be accommodated in 
public schools to study in private schools with tuition subsidies.  
The success of the SHS program can be traced to several factors. 
First, teachers compensated for program input with their resourcefulness, 
sometimes at their own expense, to deliver the curriculum. Second, the 
management skills of the school head are crucial for delivering the program, 
particularly in garnering external stakeholder support. Third, the timely 
technical assistance provided by schools division offices proved essential. 
Fourth, strong partnerships and linkages with various stakeholders 
facilitated and enhanced program implementation.
The highlights from the perspectives of students are also very 
instructive. SHS students experienced a “culture shock” due to the college-level 
subjects and workload. Many students felt they were largely teaching 
themselves, with Grade 11 being mostly lecture-based and Grade 12 
focused on reporting. Moreover, they found the topics and requirements 
overwhelming, with the quality of learning being compromised. On the other 
hand, being treated like college students allowed them to know themselves 
better and discover their potential. They found nonmajor subjects, 
particularly practical research, philosophy, and pre-calculus, difficult. Arts 
and design and sports students struggled to balance academic requirements 
with their practice and needed exposure through performances, exhibits, 
and competitions.
It is to the credit of the DepEd bureaucracy that the SHS program               
launched with a very good start, considering the enormity of the needs and 
challenges of implementing a new nationwide program. The bureaucracy 
was found to have prepared well to implement the program, and program 
support was conceptually well-organized. It has undertaken modeling 
efforts that began as early as 2012, four years before the rollout in 2016. 
=== PAGE 190 ===
159
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
Curriculum and learning materials were prepared starting in 2013, along with 
costing and budgeting. Extensive advocacy campaigns were launched to 
address skepticism from various sectors.
Despite these positive observations, the program faced many 
challenges, which hopefully are mostly mere birthing pains that can be 
resolved as implementation procedures stabilize and take root. These 
challenges can be summarized in three domains:
Program theory
• There was an overly optimistic assumption about the adequacy of 
resources at the school level.  The KIIs and FGDs revealed that 
building school facilities, hiring qualified teachers, and delivering 
teaching materials were incomplete at the start of the program for 
many reasons, primarily procurement issues and an enrollment 
turnout that exceeded expectations.
• Teachers and students have issues with the curriculum. While teachers 
view the curriculum as a step in the right direction, some find it 
too ambitious for the time allotted and for the students’ level of 
preparation. Students also complained about the heavy workload.
• Some stakeholders lack awareness and understanding of the program’s 
needs. The program was designed with the expectation of active 
participation from stakeholders beyond the school system, such as 
LGUs and employers. Yet, much of this collaboration depended 
on the individual initiatives of school heads, division and regional 
school officials, and their counterparts in the LGUs and industries.
Service delivery and utilization
• Many teachers find it difficult to deliver the curriculum due to insufficient 
guidelines, inadequate materials, and lack of preparation.  This issue 
arose from several factors, including a shortage of learning 
materials, teachers who are not adequately trained for specific 
subjects, and students who enter the program with varying levels 
of competency and preparation. 
=== PAGE 191 ===
160
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
• Students often lack choices in tracks and strands due to supply-side issues 
in program offerings. Although it has been claimed that program 
offerings considered both demand and supply, it appears that 
the decision to offer a strand has ultimately been determined by 
supply factors, specifically whether the school has the necessary 
resources and qualified teachers.
• Students from different schools in different areas have diverse program 
experiences. Despite extensive preparation activities before the 
program’s implementation, variations in experience still occurred. 
The difficulty in delivering the curriculum due to inadequate 
learning materials and differing interpretations of guidelines 
made the learning experience dependent on the teacher. 
• There is a clamor for support in taking National Certification (NC) 
exams. Often, students can only take the NC exams because their 
school arranged them. There is also a demand for subsidizing the 
fees for TVL graduates to take the NC exams.
Program organization and support
• The performance of program functions varies significantly. There 
are inconsistencies in executing these functions, even though 
they are well-defined in the SHS Manual of Operations. These 
functions include program advocacy, capacity building, monitoring 
and evaluation, technical assistance to schools, guidance and 
counseling, and partnership building at the school level.
• The interpretation of the SHS program guidelines also varies. This 
variation in implementing the SHS program is particularly 
evident in private schools, state universities and colleges, and 
local universities and colleges.
• There is a shortage of human resources. Most SHS focal persons 
expressed the need for additional workforce. Specializations are 
not appropriate, resulting in a lack of teachers for specific subjects. 
Additionally, adding tasks to the regular functions of teachers, 
such as monitoring, becomes overly burdensome, especially in 
large divisions.
=== PAGE 192 ===
161
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
• The long-term role of public-private partnerships, such as the SHS 
voucher and JDVP-TVL, remains unclear. These programs have 
enabled students who cannot be accommodated in public schools 
to attend private schools. However, there is no clear guidance on 
the longer-term roles of these programs in SHS implementation. 
Senior high school and the labor market: 
Perspectives of Grade 12 students and 
human resource officers*
Research objectives and methods
The first study (Brillantes et al. 2019) provided the impetus to determine 
the seamless placement of SHS graduates in the Philippine labor market. 
Meanwhile, the second study (Orbeta et al. 2018) assessed the likelihood of 
achieving this desired outcome by examining the experiences of Grade 12 
graduating students and the views of firms regarding the SHS graduates’   
labor market prospects. It aimed to describe the demand and supply aspects               
of the emerging labor market for SHS graduates. 
The primary data collection methods used were FGDs with Grade 12                                                                                                                                               
students and KIIs with firms—both ensured the confidentiality of all 
participants. The FGDs focused on four main topics: (1) enrollment in 
SHS, (2) experience in SHS, (3) employment prospects, and (4) plans for 
higher education. Meanwhile, the KIIs covered the (1) profile of the firms, 
(2) firms’ understanding of the K-12 program, (3) firms’ perspectives on 
t h e  q u a l i fi c a t i o n s  o f  t h e  g r a d u a t e s  a n d  t h e i r  w i l l i n g n e s s  t o  h i r e  t h e m ,                                                                                                                                             
(4) adjustments to firms’ hiring policies in light of the K-12 program, and         
(5) actual hiring of graduates.
Due to time constraints, only specific regions were targeted as study 
sites: the National Capital Region (NCR), Region IV-A (CALABARZON 
[Cavite, Laguna, Batangas, Rizal, and Quezon]), and Region III (Central 
Luzon). Schools offering Grade 12 were divided into three groups (small, 
medium, and large) based on enrollment size and whether they offered all or 
some of the academic strands. A total of 18 schools (12 public and 6 private) 
were visited for the FGDs with Grade 12 students, involving 3,618 students. 
* This section draws heavily from the PIDS Research Paper Series 2019-04, titled “Senior High School 
and the Labor Market: Perspectives of Grade 12 Students and Human Resource Officers”. The full study 
is available at https://doi.org/10.62986/rps2019.04.
=== PAGE 193 ===
162
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
For the KIIs with firms, NCR, CALABARZON, and the province 
of Cebu were selected as study sites because these areas are the hubs of 
Philippine business and industry. Based on the 2015 List of Establishments 
by the Philippine Statistics Authority (PSA 2017), NCR and CALABARZON 
have the highest number of business and industry establishments in the 
Philippines. CALABARZON has economic zones in Quezon, Batangas, 
Laguna, and Cavite. Similarly, Cebu province continues to lead the list of 
provinces outside NCR with the highest number of establishments. 
The team conducted a desk review of past studies, DepEd and 
Department of Labor and Employment guidelines on the implementation 
of K-12 and work immersion, Civil Service Commission (CSC) hiring 
policies, and online job sites prior to the actual fieldwork. The preferred 
respondents for the KIIs were human resource (HR) managers or designated     
representatives of selected private companies and LGUs, often referred to in 
this study as “firms”. 
The research team experienced difficulties in recruiting participants 
for face-to-face interviews. An HR officer from a large firm requested to 
accomplish the KII guide online instead. Some KIIs were postponed due to 
the geographic disparity of firms’ designated representatives. One region 
recorded the highest number of refusals to interview requests, and target 
companies in that region included supermarkets, organic farms, automotive 
companies, bakeshops, and water district offices. Despite sending request 
letters and repeated follow-ups, most firms seemed reluctant to discuss 
topics related to hiring manpower. The limited number of participants 
for these KIIs (33 HR managers/officers from 26 small, medium, and large 
firms in NCR, CALABARZON, and Central Visayas, spanning services, 
manufacturing, agribusiness sectors, and LGUs) presents a limitation of this 
study, especially compared to the 3,618 students who participated in the FGDs.
Study findings 
Based on the enrollment data for AY  2017–2018, approximately 11,087 schools 
offered SHS, with 58 percent being public schools. There were 2.7 million                   
SHS students, including 1.2 million Grade 12 students. This level of enrollment 
exceeded expectations. None of the schools offered all eight academic 
strands; most schools offered only one strand. The shortage was more in 
=== PAGE 194 ===
163
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
the number of strand offerings than in the number of schools. The most 
popular strand was the General Academic Strand, closely followed by                                                       
the TVL strand. There were more female than male SHS students, reflecting                     
the well-known phenomenon in the Philippines that male students drop out of 
school earlier than female students.
The FGDs with Grade 12 students revealed that most plan to attend 
college, even those in the TVL track. Most students shared that they 
would be applying or had already applied for college. The results revealed 
that school and track selection were based on convenience, personal 
preferences, affordability, or parental influence. 
The students expressed appreciation for the additional two years               
that allowed them to assess which college courses to take, helped improve 
their character and attitude, and equipped them with additional knowledge 
and skills in their chosen fields. Their appreciation depended largely on the 
quality of teaching, the relevance of the subjects taught, and the available                         
learning resources and facilities. However, the students revealed that they 
were not very confident that they would get a job after SHS. Even those 
who gained national certification for the TVL strand felt that companies 
would still prefer to hire college graduates. Most of them expect to earn a 
minimum wage salary if hired.
The KIIs highlighted that the firms interviewed lacked in-depth 
knowledge of the SHS program. They possessed only bits and pieces of 
information about the program and inadequate knowledge of the SHS 
curriculum and the profile/quality of its first batch of graduates. Firms 
articulated the need to be exposed to the graduates to understand their 
knowledge and skills and whether these skills are adequate and appropriate                
for the current needs of the industry.
There is reluctance among most respondent firms to hire SHS 
graduates. While many expressed a willingness to hire SHS graduates, they 
set preconditions such as required competencies and specialized skills, longer 
and more in-depth work immersion, and offering only low-level positions.                     
These preconditions are consistent with the reasons cited in the JobStreet (2018) 
survey report, which noted that firms believe that SHS graduates are not 
qualified and lack work experience. The reluctance to hire may also be 
=== PAGE 195 ===
164
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
linked to the firms’ lack of in-depth knowledge about the SHS program. 
Furthermore, most firms perceive SHS graduates as not yet “work ready” 
for a professional workplace, contributing to their hesitation to hire them.
The types of jobs available for SHS graduates are mostly entry-level 
positions requiring middle-level skills (e.g., clerical support staff, crafts 
and trade workers, machine operators, sales and services workers). Many 
employers, however, offer elementary occupations that only require 
primary education and skills below the middle level. The discussion on 
qualifications and competencies revealed some hiring policies of the 
companies or firms. Some firms prefer college graduates or college-level 
candidates for middle-skill jobs. Others do not differentiate SHS graduates 
from junior high school (JHS) completers and previous fourth-year 
high school graduates, putting SHS graduates on equal footing with no 
comparative advantage in terms of learning and behavioral skills. Some 
employers also expressed concerns about qualification standards, such 
as CSC employment regulations that LGUs follow, Maritime Industry 
Authority regulations for sea-based workers that shipping lines adhere to, 
and the length of internships or on-the-job placements that exceed the 
SHS work immersion requirements.    
It was noted that few companies offer SHS graduates positions as 
associate-level professionals or technicians, such as information technology (IT) 
technicians, nursing assistants, and operating room technicians. While 
these positions typically require degree holders, few companies are willing 
to adjust hiring policies to accommodate SHS graduates. Examples include 
requiring longer and more in-depth internship programs for IT technicians                 
and exploring the development of a course (with national certification                     
or NC) for nursing care that includes advanced training. 
Firms shared their experiences and insights on work immersion. 
Most firms believe that the minimum school requirement of 80 hours is 
not enough, as it only allows students to familiarize themselves with the 
work. Most respondents likened immersion to on-the-job training and 
college internships. 
Firms confirmed that very few companies are hiring SHS graduates. 
They explained that SHS graduates were not applying for jobs for several 
reasons: firms have no vacant positions, SHS graduates (regardless of 
their tracks) prefer to attend college first, SHS graduates feel they are not 
yet prepared to work, and graduates lack the confidence to compete with 
=== PAGE 196 ===
165
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
college graduates. The KII results further indicated that SHS graduates are 
mostly hired for elementary occupations (e.g., warehouse helpers, leisure 
and entertainment center attendants, ground cleaners, maintenance 
workers, park-based and guest service attendants, pickers). Some firms 
reasoned that they have no vacancies and that the only positions available 
for SHS graduates are elementary occupations. It could also be possible 
that only lower positions are offered because of reluctance to hire. It was 
also validated that SHS graduates are hired as call center agents by business 
process outsourcing companies.  
The issue of work retention emerged from firms’ experiences in 
hiring SHS graduates. Most firms that have hired SHS graduates observed 
their tendency to work only for a short period, either because they plan to 
attend college or because they want to gain experience before transferring 
to higher-paying companies. According to the respondents, this results in 
wasted time and resources spent on recruitment, orientation, and training. 
This is the reason why one large company is piloting the hiring of SHS 
graduates to gather data on their work performance and retention. The 
results will serve as a basis for adjustments in hiring policies.  
On the employability of senior high 
school graduates: Evidence from the 
Labor Force Survey*
Research objectives and methods 
The qualitative findings from the second study (Orbeta et al. 2018) warranted 
a follow-up study, this time utilizing data from the quarterly Labor 
Force Survey (LFS). Thus, Orbeta and Potestad (2020) gauged the actual 
performance of SHS graduates in the labor market, one of the potential 
exits for SHS graduates. With the first batch of SHS graduates completing 
their studies in April 2018, the study analyzed eight rounds of the LFS from                           
July 2018 to April 2020. The labor market outcomes of SHS graduates  
(Grade 12) were compared to those of their peers—Grade 10 and  second-year 
college completers.
* This section draws heavily from the PIDS Philippine Journal of Development Vol. 49 No. 1d, titled             
“On the Employability of Senior High School Graduates in the Philippines: Evidence from the Labor 
Force Survey”. The full study is available at https://pjd.pids.gov.ph/site/volume-issue-view?id=26185.
=== PAGE 197 ===
166
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Orbeta and Potestad (2020) used causal inference methods to 
measure the differences in labor market outcomes between SHS graduates 
(Grade 12) and the comparison cohorts (Grade 10 and second-year college 
completers). This comparison was framed as a multivalued treatment 
effect estimation, with each education level treated as a separate group. To 
improve the comparability of the different treatment cohorts, the study 
employed inverse probability weighting with regression adjustment (IPWRA) 
methods. Personal and household characteristics served as propensity score  
and regression adjustment variables.
Variables and data 
The labor market outcomes of interest for the study included (1) labor force 
participation, (2) employment or unemployment, (3) underemployment, 
(4) work hours, (5) basic pay, (6) proportion working as wage and salary 
workers, (7) proportion self-employed, and (8) proportion working in 
industry. These metrics provided a comprehensive description of potential 
labor market outcomes.
To examine the labor market outcomes of SHS graduates, this                     
study utilized data from the eight rounds of the LFS conducted in July 
and October 2018, and January and April 2020. This period followed the 
graduation of the first batch of SHS students in April 2018. The selection                 
of these periods was based on the availability of variables and the age 
cohort of interest.
Cohort of interest 
To ensure valid comparisons of labor market outcomes between SHS 
graduates and their peers, researchers first identified the age cohort most 
likely to constitute the SHS graduates. This approach minimized the influence 
of other variables, such as work experience, on labor market outcomes.                    
The treatment effect estimation was applied only within this cohort.
The cohorts of interest consisted of those who had completed the 
relevant education levels: Grade 10, Grade 12, and second-year college. 
T h e y  w e r e  i d e n t i fi e d  a s  t h o s e  c u r r e n t l y  e n r o l l e d ,  p r e s u m a b l y  a t  t h e                                                                                                                                           
next level, among those who had completed Grade 9, Grade 11, and first-year 
college  in the April 2018 round of the LFS. They were presumed to have

completed their next level of education, which were, respectively,                                          
Grade 10, Grade 12, and second-year college. The cohort was identified by 
their age. Table 1 shows the age distribution of these students according to 
the April 2018 LFS.
In treatment effect estimations, ideally, one would compare the 
labor market outcomes of the same individuals with different levels of 
educational attainment. However, this is not possible because different                         
educational attainments for the same individuals cannot be observed at                
the same time. This is known as the fundamental problem of causal                                                                                                                                           
inference (Holland 1986). The study instead compared virtually “similar” 
individuals, who differ only in their educational attainment (the treatment), 
using their personal and household characteristics. After generating 
comparable individuals, their labor market outcomes were compared. The 
similarity between the treatment and comparison groups is achieved by 
IPWRA, which combines inverse probability weighting and regression 
adjustment. Since IPWRA integrates two methods for estimating        
treatment effects, it is helpful to describe each method separately before 
discussing their combination.
=== PAGE 199 ===
168
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Assuming initially two outcomes and following the notation in 
Wooldridge (2010), regression adjustment (RA) estimates are obtained by 
ordinary least squares (OLS).

𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝̂(𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖) 
 
𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚�𝑔𝑔𝑔𝑔(𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑥) 
 
 
 as inverse 
probability weighted rather than by simple OLS. Then, similar to RA,                                                                                                                                              
it averages the difference in the predicted values of the inverse 
probability-weighted estimation results of the two regression adjustment 
equations. Wooldridge (2010) pointed out that IPWRA has doubly robust 
properties—only one of IPW or RA needs to be correctly specified to 
arrive at a consistent estimate. This is important because the analyst 
rarely knows whether the outcome equation or the propensity score is 
misspecified. This result is specific to binary treatment.
Up to this point, the discussion has focused on two outcomes 
corresponding to two treatments. However, this paper compares three 
treatments: Grade 10, Grade 12, and post-secondary and second-year college. 
=== PAGE 200 ===
169
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
IPWRA can handle multiple treatment values. The change is that regression 
adjustment will have three equations (one for each treatment) to estimate, 
and the propensity score estimation will use a multinomial logit rather 
than a simple logit equation to generate the generalized propensity               
scores. Using simulations, it has been argued that the properties of binary 
treatment, such as doubly robust, also extend to the case of multivalued 
treatment (Linden et al. 2015).
The eight rounds of the LFS were pooled in the estimation, with 
round-fixed effects included to account for any round-specific factors not 
captured by the independent variables. The estimation used the ipwra 
routine in Stata (StataCorp 2019). The multivalued version of ipwra  is the 
parametric implementation of the semiparametric estimators described in 
Cattaneo (2010).
Independent variables 
• Propensity score variables. The inverse probability aimed to make 
treatment and comparison units comparable, except for their 
completed education. The ideal propensity score variables were 
those not affected by the treatment. These variables included 
students’ demographic characteristics (e.g., sex), household 
characteristics (e.g., household size), parents’ capacity, and 
urbanity. Parents’ educational attainment and age were used as 
proxies for earning capacity. Since the characteristics of spouses 
were highly correlated, the maximum education and age of both 
father and mother were used. Following Linden et al. (2015), 
the final specification, which included interaction terms, was 
determined by the bfit routine in Stata, minimizing the Bayesian 
information criterion (BIC) (Cattaneo et al. 2013).
• Regression adjustment variables. Labor market outcomes were 
determined not only by educational attainment but also by other 
socioeconomic indicators. These included personal characteristics 
such as age and sex, as well as household characteristics like 
household size and parents’ education and age (e.g., Orbeta 2000).
=== PAGE 201 ===
170
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Study findings 
The LFS data revealed that just over 20 percent of SHS graduates entered                      
the labor force, while more than 70 percent continued their education. 
Attendance rates, presumably at the next higher level, increased over the 
quarters. This result indicates that the labor market is not a popular 
destination for SHS graduates, as they expressed before graduation                
(Orbeta et al. 2018). There were more Grade 10 completers and fewer 
second-year college completers entering the labor force compared to SHS 
graduates. One explanation for this trend is the socioeconomic status of 
the household. Generally, the higher a household’s socioeconomic status, 
the lower the probability of early entry into the labor market (Table 2). 
Table 2. Average LFP by education of parents, July 2018–April 2020 
Labor Force Survey
Education of Parents Grade 10 Grade 12 Second-Year College Total
Below elementary 0.253 0.294 0.182 0.260
High school 0.238 0.259 0.185 0.240
Above high school 0.142 0.145 0.142 0.143
Total 0.218 0.233 0.164 0.217
Source: Orbeta and Potestad (2020, p.19)
Parents’ education is a good proxy for household socioeconomic status,                 
as it is positively related to income generation. Using this as a proxy, the              
data showed that the average labor force participation rates for Grade 10,                                                                                                                                           
Grade 12, and second-year college completers decreased as parents’ 
education levels increased. For instance, 25 percent of Grade 10 completers 
enter the labor force when their parents’ education is elementary or    
below, compared to 14 percent when their parents’ education is above high 
school. A similar pattern was observed for SHS graduates and second-year 
college completers.
The estimation showed mixed results for SHS graduates in                              
the labor market, with no clear advantages or disadvantages compared                  
to their peers (Table 3). The comparisons indicate that Grade 12                                                          
graduates do not consistently show better labor market outcomes than                                                 
Grade 10 completers, contrary to the earlier findings of Manasan (2020).

=== PAGE 203 ===
172
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Similarly, second-year college completers do not consistently outperform 
SHS graduates in labor market outcomes. While using the same dataset 
(though this study utilized four additional quarters) and examining the 
same outcomes, Orbeta and Potestad (2020) sought to make the cohorts 
comparable by employing treatment effects estimation and utilizing                                              
personal and household characteristics that are not readily influenced by 
treatment. Compared to Grade 10 completers, SHS graduates have higher 
basic daily pay and a greater rate of self-employment. However, they have 
poorer outcomes in terms of labor force participation, employment and 
underemployment rates, hours worked, wage and salary workers, and 
working in industry. Relative to second-year college completers, more SHS 
graduates are in the labor force, have a lower visible underemployment                                                                                                       
rate, work more hours, are more likely to be wage and salary workers, and 
have a higher proportion working in industry. However, they have a lower 
employment rate and a smaller proportion of self-employment. These 
results do not entirely align with Manasan’s (2020) study, which concluded 
that SHS graduates have better labor market outcomes than Grade 10 
completers, and second-year college completers have better outcomes than 
Grade 12 graduates.
This study also confirmed earlier qualitative findings that employers 
have a wait-and-see attitude about hiring SHS graduates. Employers need 
more information about the capabilities of SHS graduates before making 
hiring decisions.
It should be emphasized that this study merely scratched the surface 
of understanding the labor market outcomes for SHS graduates.  
Conclusion and recommendations
This chapter integrated three PIDS-produced studies to examine the 
education-to-employment nexus following the rollout of the K-12 program. 
Specifically, it explored the program’s early years, students’ and employers’ 
views on K-12 graduates’ employability, and graduates’ actual employment 
outcomes two years after graduation.  
The process evaluation study (Brillantes et al. 2019) on SHS 
implementation revealed notable gains, particularly in high enrollment 
=== PAGE 204 ===
173
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
numbers. Credit goes to DepEd for launching the SHS program on a very 
good note, despite the many needs, anxieties, and challenges involved in 
implementing this new nationwide program. The DepEd bureaucracy was 
found to be well-prepared for program implementation; even program 
support was conceptually well-organized. Several implementers, teachers, 
parents, and students highlighted varying experiences and opportunities 
that are highly instructive for program implementers. While the program 
faced many challenges, these are hopefully mere birthing pains that can be 
addressed as implementation procedures stabilize and take root. 
With the first two years of K-12 implementation off to a positive start, 
questions arose about students’ and employers’ understanding of employment 
after completing 12 years of basic education. The second study (Orbeta et 
al. 2018) in this chapter revealed that despite the K-12 program’s focus on 
employment and entrepreneurship, three-quarters of Grade 12 students                                                                                                                                         
plan to pursue higher education, including those in the TVL track. 
Moreover, FGDs with students highlighted their lack of confidence in 
securing jobs after graduating from SHS. Even those who passed NC                                                                                                                               
assessments believe firms prefer to hire college graduates. Most employers 
admitted to lacking in-depth knowledge of the SHS program. Additionally, 
they expressed the need to interact with SHS graduates before they                       
can be confident in hiring them, given that the labor market has long 
been accustomed to hiring university graduates under the previous                                   
K-10 educational system. 
Given these viewpoints on K-12 graduates’ immediate employability, 
it proved crucial to determine their actual employability two years after 
the first K-12 graduates completed their studies in AY 2017–2018. The 
third paper (Orbeta and Potestad 2020) analyzed eight rounds of LFS                                       
from July 2018 to April 2020, comparing SHS graduates to their peers          
who completed Grade 10 and second-year college. It employed multivalued 
t r e a t m e n t  e ff e c t  e s t i m a t i o n  ( i . e . ,  I P W R A ) ,  w i t h  e d u c a t i o n a l  a t t a i n m e n t  a s                                                                                                                                                
the treatment and individual and household characteristics as independent 
variables. The results confirmed the findings of the earlier study, the  
second paper of this chapter, that only a small proportion (a little over 20%) 
of SHS graduates enter the labor force, while most (over 70%) continue to    
pursue university-level education. The estimations also show mixed results, 
with SHS graduates sometimes performing better and sometimes worse 
than their peers in terms of labor market outcomes. 
=== PAGE 205 ===
174
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
As the SHS component of K-12 continues, the process evaluation  
study has recommended several key actions. Under program theory, 
Brillantes et al. (2019) advised a continued curricular review that 
incorporates feedback from teachers and students. For service delivery and 
utilization, the recommendations included addressing teacher training, 
upgrading school facilities, clarifying curriculum implementation design 
and performance testing, streamlining the process for skills certification, 
and providing clearer guidance on technical-vocational offerings in                                                       
schools. Lastly, under program organization, the study underscored the 
importance of offering robust support to teachers to enhance the delivery 
of the K-12 curriculum and establishing mechanisms to ensure that                                                                                                                          
schools comply with K-12 curricular standards. 
These programmatic recommendations from the process evaluation 
study nicely dovetail with the qualitative study on students’ and employers’ 
views on the employability of K-12 graduates (Orbeta et al. 2018). These 
recommendations are largely anchored on work immersion as a learning 
activity (e.g., extending and making work immersion activities more 
competency-oriented, aligning work immersion venues with the strands of 
SHS students, integrating work immersion models into curricular updates);                   
on hiring policies that are welcoming to SHS graduates (e.g., adjusting 
government agencies’ hiring policies, posting SHS-friendly online job 
positions); and on improvements to assessments for NC acquisition by                
SHS graduates.
Since most SHS graduates continue their education rather than 
enter the labor market, the authors of the third study (Orbeta and                                                                                                    
Potestad 2020) recommended reexamining the SHS program’s employment   
and entrepreneurial objectives. A more nuanced view of these objectives 
is needed to reflect this reality. At this juncture of the economy, data 
indicate that most SHS graduates are not expected to join the labor 
force immediately. There is a need to determine the reasons for this 
revealing result, whether they stem from the demand side (employers),                                     
the supply side (students and their families), or both. Earlier interviews           
with prospective graduates and human resource officers, as detailed in the 
second study (Orbeta et al. 2018), suggest that both parties desire measures                                 
to facilitate students’ seamless entry into the labor market.
=== PAGE 206 ===
175
Senior High School’s Educational Delivery and Students’ Employability
This chapter discussed the K-12 program’s birth pains, from its 
implementation to the integration of graduates into the labor market. 
Further improvements to this young reform measure for basic education 
are anticipated as the MATATAG Curriculum has recently been piloted                             
in select schools (as of this book’s publication). Fresh assessments of the 
K-12 program’s curricular approaches, regardless of their forms in the 
succeeding years, need to proceed. The Philippines has gone beyond 
transitioning to a 12-year basic educational system that has been already 
commonplace in many parts of the world.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
Learning in the mother tongue (MT) is crucial to education. MT is the 
language a child has been exposed to from birth or during her/his critical 
growth period; it is the language that one has learned first, knows best, 
uses most, and identifies with (Kosonen and Young 2009). 
Children in the Philippines often struggle when they must switch 
from their home language to the language used in school. Historically, 
MTs have been used as auxiliary languages alongside dominant ones like 
Filipino and English throughout primary education. However, in 2009, the 
Department of Education (DepEd) institutionalized mother tongue-based 
multilingual education (MTB-MLE) through Order 74. The MTB-MLE 
program mandates the use of MT as the medium of instruction (MOI) for                                       
t h e  fi r s t  f o u r  y e a r s  o f  p r i m a r y  e d u c a t i o n ,  w i t h  F i l i p i n o  a n d  E n g l i s h  a s                                                                                                                         
subject areas and as MOIs beyond Grade 3. This program is expected to 
facilitate learning by reducing children’s difficulties in understanding  
basic concepts in a language they have yet to master fully. 
Studies have shown that MT education increases students’ success, 
encourages parents to become more invested in their children’s learning, 
and has more noticeable effects among girls and disadvantaged groups, such                           
as rural and indigenous children (Ball 2010). However, are these observations 
true in the Philippines? This chapter documents the implementation of 
the country’s MTB-MLE program through a process evaluation following 
its nationwide adoption under Republic Act (RA) 10533, otherwise known                 
as the Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2013. 
Language policy issues and challenges
Challenges of numerous languages in the 
institutionalization of language-in-education policies
Language-in-education policy refers to “legislation on and practices pertaining 
to languages or media of instruction and literacy used in basic education” 
(Kosonen and Young 2009, p.11). These policies provide guidelines for 
language use and acquisition within nation-states and institutions.  
The Philippines has made significant efforts in language planning and 
policymaking (Table 1). However, with over 180 languages spoken across 
the country, it faces challenges in institutionalizing language policies that 
accommodate its multilingual nature, especially considering its colonial past.
=== PAGE 212 ===
Table 1. Philippines’ language-in-education policies (1900–2009)
Years Policy
1901 Education Act 74 (Monolingual 
Education Policy)
US President William McKinley directed the Philippine Commission to make 
English "the basis of all public school instruction" (Dawe 2014, p.66).
1937 and 1940 Executive Orders 134 and 263 The 1935 Constitution adopted Tagalog as the national language in 1937 for all 
levels of education. Meanwhile, Executive Order 263 stipulated the teaching of 
Tagalog as a separate subject from primary to tertiary education.
1957 Revised Educational Act The Board of National Education provided for the use of vernacular languages 
as the medium of instruction (MOI) in Grades 1 and 2 and as auxiliary MOI in 
Grades 3 and 4. English was introduced as a separate subject and MOI from 
Grade 3 onwards. Tagalog or Pilipino (adopted in 1959) became an auxiliary MOI 
in Grades 5 and 6 (Bernardo 2004, p.19).
1973 DEC Department Order 9 
(Bilingual Education Policy)
The Department of Education and Culture (DEC) adopted the Bilingual Education 
Policy at all levels of schooling to develop bilingual competence in the use of both 
Pilipino and English as subject areas and as MOI. Regional languages are used as 
auxiliary languages in Grades 1 and 2.
1987 DECS Order  52 The Department of Education, Culture and Sports (DECS) reiterated the 
provisions in the Bilingual Education Policy: use English as the MOI for Science, 
English, and Mathematics while using Pilipino (spelled “Filipino” after 1987) as 
the MOI for Filipino, Social Studies, Character, Health, Work, and Physical 
Education at all educational levels. Regional languages remained as auxiliary 
MOI (Castillo 2000; Dekker and Young 2005).
2009 DepEd Order 74 (Multilingual 
Education Policy)
The Department of Education (DepEd) institutionalized the use of the mother 
tongue (MT) as the primary MOI in all subject areas from preschool until, at least, 
Grade 3. Filipino and English are introduced as separate subjects no earlier than 
Grade 2. The teaching of MT as a subject begins in Grade 1. Filipino and English 
become the MOI from the secondary level onward.
Source: Authors’ compilation
=== PAGE 213 ===
182
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Mixed use of Filipino and English is linked to students’ 
low scores in mathematics and science
Analysts believe the country’s Bilingual Education Policy (BEP), which 
employs both Filipino and English, is largely responsible for the weak 
performance of Filipino students in mathematics and science. Based on       
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) 
survey results, the performance of Filipino students in these subjects 
appears to have worsened. The Philippines’ rankings for Grade 4 and 
Grade 8 students dropped from 36th place in both subjects in 1999 to                                                                                                                                      
64th place in mathematics and 67th place in science in 2003. The overall 
performance in 1999 also pales in comparison to that of other countries 
(Tables 2 and 3).
These TIMSS results provide DepEd with reason to believe that 
Filipino students are not learning enough. Data show that Filipino learners  
are not mastering 60 percent of academic content (DepEd 2008), and 
language may be a contributing factor. A study by Tapang (2012) suggests 
teaching science and mathematics in the learner’s mother tongue, as 
countries with high scores in these assessments conduct basic instruction                                             
in the local language (except for Singapore).

‘Starting Where the Children Are’
Gains and pains observed in MTB-MLE implementation
Two impact assessments of the MTB-MLE and similar programs were 
conducted. One of these assessments was carried out by the United States 
Agency for International   Development (USAID) as part of the Basa Pilipinas 
program. This evaluation study compared the impact of Basa-supported 
early grade reading interventions with non-Basa interventions on elementary 
school students. The results showed that Basa students met or exceeded 
the midline benchmarks for oral reading fluency in Ilokano and reading 
comprehension targets, while the comparison group fell short in both 
areas. Both Basa and non-Basa students achieved the 40 words per minute 
benchmark in oral reading fluency but fell short of the 60 words per minute 
benchmark (Duthie et al. 2016). 
At the end of the five-year Basa Pilipinas project, reading 
comprehension skills among Grade 2 learners increased significantly                                                  
(by 14% comparing baseline to endline). In 2018, about 53 percent of the 
s a m e  g r o u p  o f  l e a r n e r s  m e t  t h e  r e a d i n g  fl u e n c y  b e n c h m a r k  o f  4 0  w o r d s                                                                                                                                             
per minute and the comprehension target of 60 percent correct answers, 
compared to only 45 percent in 2014 (EDC 2018). The project also 
improved reading instruction, delivery systems, and access to quality 
reading materials.

=== PAGE 215 ===
184
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The second study, conducted by the Assessment, Curriculum and 
Technology Research Centre, analyzed best practices in implementing the 
MTB-MLE in 50 linguistically diverse context (LDC) schools (Metila et 
al. 2016a). The study identified 17 unique program challenges in four 
categories: design, connection with the local community and culture, staff  
training, and staff selection. The biggest challenge was design, as schools 
often used the regional lingua franca as a fallback in diverse linguistic                                                       
communities. Teachers employed code-switching, translation, and bridging 
to reach as many students as possible. The study suggests that “adapting 
policy imperatives to local circumstances can be effective but can result                              
in unevenness in the extent to which localization is faithful to MTE                  
goals and principles” (Metila et al. 2016a, p.788).  
Methods
These realities and issues spurred the authors of this chapter to conduct                  
a systematic process evaluation of the MTB-MLE’s implementation under 
RA 10533. This process evaluation focused on MTB-MLE’s program logic 
and plausibility (how the program is expected to work in theory), service 
delivery and utilization (delivery mechanisms, initial successes or failures, 
and beneficiary responses), and program organization (the organizational 
setup for program implementation) (Rossi et al. 2004).
To enhance students’ learning, the Theory of Change (TOC) narrative 
(Figure 1) illustrates how the MTB-MLE program converts inputs,                
such as budget, teachers, classrooms, and textbooks, into outputs like 
MT textbooks, big books, learning materials, and trained teachers for 
instructing in the MT through corresponding activities. Intermediate 
outcomes include K-3 students reading, comprehending, and writing in MT. 
The program’s success depends on translating these intermediate outcomes 
into the final intended outcomes: improved reading comprehension, higher 
completion rates, reduced school dropouts, and decreased educational costs 
from repetitions.
Key informant interviews (KIIs) and focus group discussions (FGDs) 
were conducted with a total of 405 respondents—parents (N=176), 
teachers (N=171), and DepEd administrators (N=58)—from Luzon (144), 
the Visayas (127), and Mindanao (134). These respondents were selected 
from 14 public and 4 private schools chosen from a sample population.

These 18 schools vary in terms of linguistic diversity (see Appendix A). 
The MTB-MLE team requested 10 parents and 10 teachers from randomly 
selected schools to participate in field visits. Large schools had 10–12 parents, 
while small schools had fewer participants. FGDs were conducted with 
teachers, with large schools fielding up to 12 teachers and small schools 
fielding only 4. 
Researchers also rolled out a quick online survey (QOS) to schools 
to gauge the implementation of the program across the entire K-3 school 
system, especially since not all elementary schools are implementing the 
MTB-MLE program. This survey determined the extent of implementation 
in public and private schools, the MOIs being used in schools, the reasons 
for not implementing the program, and the accomplishments in terms of 
the four basic activities required for effective MTB-MLE implementation                                         
(i.e., writing big books, documenting orthography, grammar, and the 
dictionary of the language). Out of the 51,140 schools targeted for this QOS, 
16,479 schools (32.2%) responded (see Appendix B). Note that the MTB-MLE 
program is designed for K-3 pupils. Given the target of 51,140 schools for                                    
the QOS, DepEd is serving 15.8 million K-3 students, 91 percent of whom 
are in public schools and the rest in private schools.
=== PAGE 217 ===
186
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
What was found?
Ingredients to MTB-MLE’s success: Educational system, 
regional policy, and stakeholder support 
A strong MTB-MLE program requires presenting the program in two modes:               
as the MOI (MT) and as a subject (MTS). It also needs gradual pacing 
for first (L1), second (L2), and third languages (L3); continuous teacher 
training; a working orthography; contextualized reading resources; and 
community support. 
When the MTB-MLE program was implemented nationwide in                                                                                                                                               
School Year (SY) 2012–2013, it initially focused on eight major languages 
(Tagalog, Kapampangan, Pangasinense, Iloko, Bikol, Cebuano, Hiligaynon, 
Waray) and four languages from southern Philippines (Tausug, Maguindanaoan, 
Maranao, and Chavacano). In July 2013, seven local languages were added 
(Ybanag, Ivatan, Sambal, Akeanon, Kinaray-a, Yakan, and Surigaonon), 
bringing the total number of MOIs to 19.   
The success of MTB-MLE implementation depends on the education 
system’s structure and the region’s ability to make policy decisions regarding 
which language to use as MOI based on their needs and contexts (Stone 2012). 
At the same time, research suggests that addressing teachers’, parents’, and 
communities’ attitudes and beliefs about MTB-MLE can support rather than 
stall implementation (Stone 2012; Burton 2013). Cultural activities involving 
the MT (e.g., poetry reading, writing) and raising awareness among parents 
can also help improve community perceptions. However, Stone (2012) 
asserted that while teachers do not intentionally act as barriers to MTB-MLE 
implementation, their limited understanding of the program’s rationale may 
compromise policy results (especially if not guided by superiors).
Some 99 percent of schools have implemented MTB-MLE
The online survey revealed that about 16,287 schools (99%) have                 
implemented MTB-MLE, while only 192 schools have not. Nonetheless, the 
extent of implementation varies. Among the schools that have implemented 
MTB-MLE, as many as 6,102 schools (32%) identify Tagalog as MT,  
followed by Cebuano in 4,556 schools (24%) and Iloko in 1,996 schools (11%). 
Interestingly, while a few schools teach up to five MOIs, most (82%) teach 
only one (Table 4).

Nine in 10 schools have not implemented all four activities 
of MTB-MLE
The implementation of the MTB-MLE program in schools can be assessed 
based on their performance in four required activities for the MOI they 
are using: a) writing big books on language, literature, and culture;                                                                                                                                         
b) documenting the orthography of the language; c) documenting the 
grammar of the language; and d) writing a dictionary of the language. 
The online survey revealed that only 9 percent of schools claimed to 
have completed all four activities, while 6 percent have not done any 
of the activities (see Table 5). This result indicates a significant gap in 
implementation for the 19 languages currently officially recognized as MOI. 
As to the nature of these activities, nearly half (45%) of schools have 
written big books, 21 percent have documented orthograph, 18 percent                
have documented grammar, and 13 percent have documented a dictionary                  
of the language. Data indicate that these schools need assistance in                  
all four areas of MTB-MLE (Table 5).

‘Starting Where the Children Are’
The lack of textbooks and learning materials is the top 
reason for not implementing MTB-MLE
For the 192 schools that have not implemented MTB-MLE, the leading 
reasons (Table 6) are resource-related: teachers lack relevant teaching 
materials (17%), schools do not have a dictionary of the language (16%), 
and students lack textbooks (16%). The next set of reasons is related to the 
chosen MOI of the school: teachers lack expertise in the MOI (12%),                                                     
students do not speak the MOI of the school (9%), and parents do not speak 
and support the chosen MOI (9%).

A one-size-fits-all linguistic policy confronts the country’s 
diverse linguistic makeup
The Philippines has 185 spoken languages (Ethnologue 2019). This linguistic 
diversity complicates educational outcomes since millions of children 
enter school without proficiency in the chosen MOI and are described as 
“deficient”. Framed within the language-as-problem orientation (Ruiz 1984), 
the issue of linguistic diversity has been addressed through monolingualism                                         
=== PAGE 221 ===
190
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
to facilitate the delivery of academic content (i.e., English-only policy) and 
token accommodations for linguistic diversity. However, a “one-size-fits-all” 
approach to the unique linguistic makeup of Philippine classrooms proves 
inadequate, often resulting in suboptimal outcomes. Further complicating 
matters, it has been shown that as many as 15 other languages can coexist                
in a classroom alongside the dominant MT (see Box 1). A consolation 
is that this issue is not widespread, as 82 percent of schools have only                        
one MOI (refer to Table 4).
Yet, catering to all distinct languages in classrooms 
is not possible
The MTB-MLE policy aims to bridge the gap between proficiency in                 
the MT and literacy in the L2/L3. However, DepEd has acknowledged 
that it is impossible to cater to all distinct languages in the classroom.                                                                                                        
To address this issue, DepEd has encouraged schools and divisions to 
localize the MTB-MLE implementation. Nonetheless, localization requires 
appropriate support since teachers often have to improvise, making the 
results dependent on the teacher’s ability.  
Teachers play crucial roles but can also become barriers 
to MTB-MLE implementation
Recent assessments of the MTB-MLE program have highlighted the crucial 
role of teachers as program implementers, but they can also act as barriers 
to implementation (Stone 2012). Some teachers hold negative attitudes 
toward teaching in their mother tongue due to their beliefs about the greater 
value of learning English (Burton 2013). These attitudes stem from a lack 
of confidence in teaching literacy in their MTs (Stone 2010), leading to 
outward support but subtle resistance in practice. Additionally, there are 
claims that the economic benefits of English competence outweigh those               
of learning the MT (Parba 2018).  
MT usage leads to more student-teacher interactions 
and engaged learning
Visits to public and private schools showed that classrooms using the MT 
have more interactions between children and teachers. Most classrooms 
do not use only one MT, allowing pupils to use any available language. 
=== PAGE 222 ===
191
‘Starting Where the Children Are’
Box 1. Linguistically diverse classroom scenarios: Taguig 
school and Cagayan de Oro division
Source of basic data: Unpublished data for the School Year 2016–2017 from the 
Education Management Information System Division, DepEd, received via email on 
April 27, 2018
The National Capital Region (NCR) is considered a less linguistically 
diverse context (L-LDC), where Tagalog, the predominant language 
spoken in the region, may be everyone’s mother tongue (MT). 
However, this is not the case in a classroom in Taguig, which 
illustrates the complex linguistic nature of Philippine  education. 
In a Grade 1 class of 40 pupils, as many as 16 languages are 
identified as MTs by the learners themselves. Tagalog is spoken by 
13 pupils (not a clear majority), followed closely by Sinugbuanong                                                    
Binisaya with 10 speakers, and other Cebuano dialects with 5 speakers. 
Each of the following languages has one speaker: Bikol (west Albay), 
Bikol (Miraya), Bikol (Libon), Hiligaynon, Tagbanwa, Kinaray-a, 
Capiznon, Chavacano, Kalinga, Davawenyo, Bantoanon, and  Tausug.
In another L-LDC in the Cagayan de Oro division in Mindanao, where the 
clear majority of students (N=48,568) speak Cebuano/Sinugbuanong 
Binisaya/Kana and its variants, some 1,546 K-3 pupils speak many 
other languages as their MTs. These include Maranao,   Higaonon, 
Tagalog, Hiligaynon, English, Bol-anon, Arabic, Bikol, Tausug, 
Surigaonon, Chabacano, Maguindanaoan, Binukid, Capiznon, Waray, 
Aklanon, Iloko, Kapampangan, Manobo, Ivatan, Minasbate, Butuanon, 
and Kinaray-a.
Translanguaging—switching between MT, Tagalog, and English—occurs 
daily. Public schools use textbooks, visual materials, television, and desktops                  
to engage learners, while private schools rely on handouts, worksheets, 
and online resources.
Children generally seem well invested in their lessons. They appear 
motivated, engaged, highly responsive, and participative in their classes.                  
A pattern for language use was noticed: (1) The MT is used for 
delivering and managing academic content and often for greeting people;                                                      
(2) Tagalog/Filipino usage in LDCs is minimal; when used, it is often 
during unguarded moments (e.g., exclamations of surprise like “Galing!”);                
(3) English is used in everyday expressions (e.g., “Right!”, “OK”, “Good job”); 
and (4) translanguaging always happens (e.g., the Chabacano phrase                                                                              
“singko cupcakes na plato” mixes Spanish, Tagalog, and English).
=== PAGE 223 ===
192
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Program logic: Contradictions, unsmooth transitions, 
wrong assumptions
Contradictions in MOI usage with levels of learning 
and a DepEd order
The primary rationale for the MTB-MLE program is to provide K-3 students 
with academic content in L1 to help them gain competence in oral and 
written L1 before learning L2 and L3. It is observed that readiness and 
eventual competence in L2 depend on the competence already developed 
in L1 (Malone 2012). 
However, the MOI matrix provided in DepEd Order 31 (s. 2012)  
shows that three languages are used simultaneously in Grade 1 (Figure 2).  
Filipino and English are taught as subjects using these two languages,                                           
which should only be used as MOI from Grade 4 onward (since MT is                                                                       
used as MOI from K-3). This means that seven-year-old pupils are exposed                                                                                                            
to three languages at once, making it challenging for them to learn and for 
teachers to assess their fluency and understanding. These modifications 
contradict departmental, legal, and national provisions and will significantly 
affect the children’s academic content learning.
Figure 2. MOI across grade levels
MOI = medium of instruction; AP = Araling Panlipunan; EPP/TLE = Edukasyong 
Pantahanan at Pangkabuhayan/Technology and Livelihood Education; MAPEH = Music, 
Arts, Physical Education, and Health; EsP = Edukasyon sa Pagpapakatao; G = Grade
Source: Lifted in full from DepEd Order 31 (s. 2012, p.8)
=== PAGE 224 ===
193
‘Starting Where the Children Are’
Unsmooth transitions from MT to national and 
international languages
A key element of the MTB-MLE program is the transition to the national 
language, Filipino, and an international language, English. Completing 
the transition from L1 to L2 and L2 to L3 before a child is ready can have  
adverse effects on language learning, potentially resulting in subtractive 
bilingualism (Baker 2001). There is an optimal design that considers 
the number of years children need to master their MT before being bridged 
to additional languages (Table 7). According to Malone (2012), four- or 
five-year-old pupils begin learning oral and written L1 in school, then 
they only start learning oral L3 in Grade 3 and begin writing in L3 
by Grade 5. L2 is used alongside L1 as the MOI until the child exits                                                                                                                              
primary grade.
In Figure 3, the team modeled what they observed as MTB-MLE 
implementation realities. The MTB-MLE language outcomes can be 
represented as stacks, with the MT as the bedrock, followed by L2                                
and L3. The proposed model is provided in column A, where MT is used 
as the MOI and as a subject before L2 and L3 are introduced. However, 
children are exposed to MT and other regional languages at home, which                                              
is more realistically represented in column B. This situation is particularly                  
true in regions where L1 speakers of Sinugbuanong Binisaya may also                                                                                                                                           
speak Cebuano or Kana. Factors such as parents and grandparents                                 
from different linguistic backgrounds often speaking different languages 
at home expose children to multiple languages simultaneously with the 
school’s MOI. Other models, like those illustrated in columns C and D,                                        
are also observed. 
MT for all subjects (MOI) versus MT-as-subject: 
Limited training and resources prevail
Private schools in Metro Manila and other regions implementing their                 
own MTB-MLE programs have instead introduced the MTS component, 
with English as the students’ L1. This means all subjects are taught in 
English, except for Filipino and Araling Panlipunan, which are taught in 
Filipino or as MT subjects. 
It should be noted that English is not included in the 19 languages 
under the MTB-MLE program. Hence, no teacher training, learning 
materials, or other resources have been designed for these schools, leading                 
to uncertainty among private school administrators about a transition 
program for additional languages.

* Filipino/Tagalog is identified as the mother tongue in public schools in NCR, based on interviews with parents. However, observations on 
the ground suggest that the single MT identified is typically learned alongside another predominant language. For instance, Filipino/Tagalog 
MT speakers often learn English simultaneously, while English MT speakers learn Filipino/Tagalog.
