196
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Sequential or simultaneous bilingualism?
This study also found that the program’s design assumes a single language               
is used as MT in Filipino households, which then serves as the foundation                
for learning an L2 when children go to school. This type of learning is 
known as sequential bilingualism (Baker 2001). In reality, language 
acquisition in the Philippines occurs through simultaneous bilingualism  
(De Houwer 2017), where two or more languages are learned concurrently 
(also known as bilingual first language acquisition).
Service delivery: Resistance, confusion, 
and policy ambiguity
For service delivery, key elements of the program were not in place prior            
to the program rollout in 2012. The following observations explain why 
less-than-desirable outcomes were found in the field:
• A lack of accurate information among key stakeholders on the rationale              
for MT implementation resulted in resistance to using MTs as MOIs.  
Teachers and parents did not fully understand the rationale behind 
MTB-MLE. Policy guidelines took time to reach the rank and 
file, and the absence of enough lead time before implementation 
resulted in teachers resenting the policy.  
• Teacher competence to teach in the school’s MOI was not fully assured 
before the program’s rollout. A review of program documents and 
qualitative interviews/discussions revealed that a mass training 
of teachers was conducted merely a month before the nationwide 
program implementation in 2012. There was no specialized 
training for classroom pedagogy in MTB-MLE. 
• Lack of adequate preparation resulted in the school’s inability to respond 
to challenges.  Due to insufficient program rollout mechanisms, 
teachers used individualized instruction for children who did not 
speak the school’s MOI or sought help from community speakers.  
• Mismatched MOIs and MTs caused confusion. Field research showed 
that a school’s MOI is determined at either regional or national 
levels, leading to a mismatch between the community’s language 
and the school’s MOI. Mismatched MOIs and MTs also emerged 
as a problem in some areas. 
=== PAGE 228 ===
197
‘Starting Where the Children Are’
• Dearth (even the absence) of textbooks and learning materials caused 
frustration. Provisions in the law prohibiting the writing of 
textbooks by DepEd employees inadvertently delayed the delivery 
of quality learning materials. Additionally, the mismatch between 
the language used in textbooks and the school’s MOI necessitated 
schools to contextualize materials for regional use, which could 
be an arduous process. 
• Languages of assessment that do not match the MOI contravene                
MTB-MLE’s policy rationale. Many public and private schools test 
students in three languages or sometimes translate questions for 
better comprehension. This approach contravenes the MTB-MLE 
policy. Although Order 74 specifies that pupils be tested in the 
MT until at least Grade 3, ground-level realities show that all 
assessments are conducted in three languages: English in English 
subjects, Filipino for Araling Panlipunan and Filipino subjects, 
and MT in MTS.
Service utilization: MTB-MLE bearing burdens?
• Some teachers view MTB-MLE use as ‘backward’. Most teachers 
acknowledged that MTs are crucial for learning academic 
content, developing children’s self-esteem, instilling pride, and 
preserving linguistic heritage. However, some teachers perceive 
the implementation of MTB-MLE as a “backward” step since job 
interviews are conducted in English, not in the MT.  
• Addressing parents’ linguistic biases about English and MT is equally 
important. Many parent respondents correctly understood the 
program’s objectives and cited faster comprehension, better 
understanding, and early reading skills as strengths of the                  
MTB-MLE program. However, some believe that MT should 
not be taught beyond Grade 3 and should remain a subject,                                                                                                 
not as MOI.  
• Teachers find using MT in mathematics and science too cumbersome.  
Teacher respondents revealed that translating curriculum guides 
and teachers’ guides from English to MT is a perennial headache. 
They find using MT as the MOI for mathematics and science     
cumbersome because some key terms can only be expressed in 
English and not in MT.
=== PAGE 229 ===
198
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Program organization: Limited competencies, 
capacities, and resources
• Limited linguistic competence.  Teachers are adequate in number, 
but interviewees think some teachers lack the necessary linguistic 
competence to teach in the MT. Principals respond to this 
challenge through mentoring and peer teaching.
• Few MTB-MLE personnel in DepEd and schools. For example,                           
one region has only one regional MTB-MLE focal person 
overseeing 12 divisions. Each division has eight districts 
overseeing a total of 51 elementary schools. Additionally, only a 
few schools have dedicated focal persons who provide guidance 
through echoing seminars on MTB-MLE.  
• No dedicated funds for MTB-MLE activities. There are no dedicated 
funds for developing big books, indigenizing teaching materials, 
and staging poetry reading and indigenous dance activities at 
the school level. Thus, principals use their schools’ maintenance                  
and operating expenses to print MTB-MLE-related curriculum 
guides and handouts. DepEd’s district or regional offices also do 
not have dedicated funds for the tasks needed to be carried out by 
MTB-MLE focal persons. 
How do we move forward?
The primary rationale of the MTB-MLE is “starting where the children 
are”—implementing learner-centered education from the beginning, using 
a child’s MT as MOI as the school’s foundation. This approach facilitates 
academic content and language learning in later grades.
Although MTB-MLE was implemented nationwide only recently 
through RA 10533, MT education has a long history. In theory, the program 
teaches children from K-3 in their MT (or L1), with L2 and L3 introduced 
in Grade 4. Initially limited to 12 languages, MLE expanded to 19 languages                                        
in SY 2012–2013. While there are discussions about officially adopting 
more languages, 8 LFs and 11 LLs are officially recognized. Schools are 
encouraged to determine the feasibility of localization in their areas, but 
establishing different pathways for localization is challenging.
=== PAGE 230 ===
199
‘Starting Where the Children Are’
 The decision to use a smaller set of languages in implementing                
MTB-MLE was aimed at feasibility, but this approach created issues for 
children and teachers. Teachers were asked to teach in a regional language 
that may not be identical to their home language. Dialectal differences are 
evident among local languages (L1s) and the 19 officially adopted languages, 
even within a geographic area (e.g., a province). These variances give rise 
to both conceptual and operational challenges in implementing MTB-MLE. 
Additionally, they lead to teacher resentment and highlight limitations                         
in teacher capabilities. Parents complained that their children were being 
taught an archaic version of their own language.
 Linguistic diversity in classrooms is a significant issue (Metila et 
al. 2016b). Children are exposed to multiple languages in their homes 
and communities, often in less linguistically diverse contexts where they 
encounter at least three languages from birth. Schools often adopt a model 
assuming only one L1 for implementation, but there is no clear guidance 
on managing linguistic diversity beyond finding feasible localization of                 
learning materials. Given the extent of language diversity in classrooms, it is 
challenging to provide general guidance to address numerous possibilities.
 In addition to conceptual issues, there are procurement 
problems and a lack of specific funding support for MTB-MLE-related 
operational activities. Both issues have hindered the delivery of MTB-MLE                                                
learning materials. 
Notwithstanding its numerous problems, the MTB-MLE program                 
has a solid pedagogical foundation. Thus, there is a need to find more 
effective, efficient, and acceptable ways of implementing MTB-MLE.
The first set of recommendations covers MTB-MLE’s program logic. 
DepEd should enhance information dissemination of empirical research 
highlighting the efficacy of learning in MT. When parents are convinced 
of the program’s logic, they could be enlisted to provide moral support 
for the use of MT at home. DepEd may need to review the MTB-MLE 
program’s design and encourage knowledge generation about how children 
learn multiple languages at once (simultaneous bilingualism), which should 
inform and refine program theory and delivery. These recommendations                                                       
consider the reality that more than one L1 is often spoken at home, forming 
the foundation for learning L2 and L3 languages through sequential 
=== PAGE 231 ===
200
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
bilingualism (with the Philippines’ diverse linguistic communities as 
context). Understanding how to teach languages in these unique contexts 
can refine implementation logic and modify service delivery.
It may also be beneficial for DepEd to study the effects of exposure 
to various languages on educational outcomes. Moreover, DepEd can 
examine the impact of social media on language acquisition and learning                                       
and then determine how best to harness these technological affordances.  
 For MTB-MLE’s service delivery and utilization, creating localized            
or indigenized learning materials (prepared with quality, reviewed, and 
updated regularly), training teachers, and monitoring and evaluating 
schools’ MTB-MLE implementation all help improve MT usage in student 
learning. The DepEd central office may also need to ensure precision in 
using common conceptual vocabulary within the program. Words and 
concepts such as “lingua franca”, “regional language”, “dialect”, and other 
technical terms used in the MTB-MLE program should be clearly defined 
to ensure that teachers access and employ accurate terms for teaching and 
learning. The program’s focal persons at DepEd regional offices may need                
to continually advocate for MTB-MLE to convince school administrators              
and teachers of the program’s soundness.
Given the observed prevalence of linguistic diversity in schools, it is 
recommended that schools instill pride and value in languages by making               
them visible in the landscape. English should be used in classrooms from    
Grade 4 onwards, while MT should be strictly used in K-3 classrooms. 
Children unconsciously imbibe the sense that their local languages are 
valued when they see them on classroom walls, around, and outside of                                                                                  
t h e  s c h o o l .  I n  a d d i t i o n ,  t h e  D e p E d  c e n t r a l  o ffi c e  m u s t  e n s u r e  t h e  u s e  o f  M T                                                                  
as the language of assessments in all content areas in K-3.  
 Finally, for MTB-MLE’s program organization, the DepEd central 
office must designate funds for the program’s operational activities. 
Complementing this approach, the DepEd regional offices should deputize             
and strengthen dedicated MTB-MLE focal persons at the division levels to 
enable regional and division offices to synergize and share best practices 
on MTB-MLE learning. Such complementary and synergistic efforts from 
DepEd’s different tiers can help schools systematize and institutionalize 
annual language mapping to determine their MOI.

=== PAGE 239 ===
208
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction 
The Philippines values the role of education in lifting Filipinos out of 
poverty. This is why ensuring equitable and inclusive access to quality 
higher education becomes crucial for economic development and poverty 
reduction. On this score, the Philippine government enacted the Universal 
Access to Quality Tertiary Education (UAQTE) Act in 2017. The law 
mandates public higher education institutions (HEIs) and government-run 
technical-vocational institutions (TVIs) to provide free quality tertiary 
education to eligible Filipino students. The government envisions 
significantly increasing university enrollments regardless of students’ 
socioeconomic status. 
The UAQTE is one of the three laws enacted by the national 
government to provide extensive educational support—subsidies, free 
tuition, and loans—to Filipino students, especially considering the K-12 
educational system rollout in the Academic Year (AY) 2016–2017. However, 
these free tuition schemes, tuition subsidies, and student loans have raised 
concerns about their effectiveness and sustainability based on the experiences 
of other countries. 
This chapter examines the implementation of Republic Act (RA) 10931 
during its pilot year and identifies areas for improvement to better                
achieve its objectives. It focuses on the higher education component of              
the law, examining the policy’s logic and plausibility, its delivery to and 
uptake by intended beneficiaries, and the overall program organization 
required for its implementation.  
Developments in higher education
Three laws mandate financial support for tertiary 
education students
Three national laws govern the provision of financial support to students 
seeking tertiary education: RA 10687, known as the Unified Financial 
Assistance System for Tertiary Education (UniFAST) Law of 2015; the Free 
Tuition Program in 2017, enabled by a special provision in the General 
Appropriations Act (GAA); and RA 10931, known as the UAQTE or                  
Free Tuition Law (FTL). Throughout this chapter, “UAQTE” and “FTL” 
will be used interchangeably.
=== PAGE 240 ===
209
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
UniFAST aims to promote equitable and rationalized access to 
quality tertiary education for academically able and highly motivated 
students from low-income backgrounds. UniFAST mandates the efficient 
targeting of beneficiaries, coordination of Student Financial Assistance 
Programs (StuFAPs), equitable allocation of scholarships across regions, 
provision of grants-in-aid (GIA) to students from marginalized sectors,               
and offering student loans. The law established the UniFAST Board as the 
implementing body.
The law placed the existing StuFAPs (summarized in Table 1) under 
the UniFAST Board. These programs are mostly merit-based, requiring               
s t u d e n t s  t o  m a i n t a i n  s p e c i fi c  g r a d e s  o r  e q u i v a l e n t s  w h i l e  a l l o w i n g                                                                                                                                
them to enroll in their preferred HEIs or TVIs. In addition, these HEIs 
or TVIs must be part of the UniFAST Registry and certified by the 
Commission on Higher Education (CHED). In this way, StuFAPs help 
public and private HEIs and TVIs play complementary roles in providing 
access to tertiary education. 
The next relevant education-related intervention is the Free Tuition 2017                                                                                                        
program, stemming from a GAA special provision. The beneficiaries 
consist of students enrolled in undergraduate programs at state colleges 
and universities (SUCs) during AY 2017–2018. Priority is given to existing 
StuFAP beneficiaries, while nonbeneficiaries may also qualify, subject to 
available funds and prioritization based on year level and economic status 
(CHED 2018). 
Following the Free Tuition 2017 program, the government enacted 
RA 10931 or the UAQTE. This law comprises four components: free higher 
education (FHE), free technical-vocational education and training (FTVET), 
a tertiary education subsidy (TES), and a national student loan program 
(SLP). It applies to all Filipino students who are currently enrolled or will 
enroll at any time after the law’s effectivity in any SUC, state-run TVI, local 
university or college (LUC), or private HEI listed in the UniFAST Registry. 
The FHE provides free tuition and other school fees (covering at 
least 13 types of fees) in all SUCs and CHED-recognized LUCs. Meanwhile, 
the FTVET offers similar benefits for TVIs recognized by the Technical 
Education and Skills Development Authority (TESDA). On the other 
hand, the TES supports students enrolled in post-secondary undergraduate 
programs at eligible HEIs and TVET programs registered under TESDA.

=== PAGE 243 ===
212
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Depending on the classification of the beneficiary, the TES provides 
three categories of benefits: (1) tuition and other school fees for those 
enrolled in private HEIs (TES1), (2) allowances for room and board, 
books, transportation, and other miscellaneous expenses (TES2), and 
(3) allowances for disability-related expenses and a one-time cost for    
obtaining the first professional credentials or qualifications. The priority                
beneficiaries for TES are those in the Listahanan of the Pantawid Pamilyang   
Pilipino Program by the Department of Social Welfare and Development 
(DSWD). Finally, the SLP, which began in 2019, provides short- and                                                                                     
long-term loans to tertiary HEI or TVI students. These loans are coursed 
through partner banks and financial institutions under arrangements 
developed by the UniFAST Secretariat.
Note, however, that implementing these educational support 
programs is not without challenges. For example, an evaluation of the 
UniFAST program found that it lacks a separate budget and organizational 
support for implementation and does not have a comprehensive set of 
implementing rules and regulations (Asuncion and Tullao 2018). Similarly,                                               
the Free Tuition 2017 program faced issues, with the Commission on Audit   
(COA 2018) reporting a low disbursement rate of only about 18.1 percent 
(equivalent to PHP 1.45 billion) for the calendar year 2017. CHED (2018) 
responded that second-semester disbursements were not considered in 
the report and that delays in HEIs’ submission of billing claims snarled up                 
the disbursement process.  
Expansive number of HEIs and enrollments in public HEIs…
Based on CHED data for AY 2019–2020, there were 1,975 operational HEIs                
in the country (including SUCs’ satellite campuses, the total number of HEIs 
rose to 2,396). Private HEIs (N=1,729, or 87.5%) comprised the majority, 
followed by SUCs (N=112, 5.7%) and LUCs (N=121, or 6.1%) (CHED n.d.).
In terms of enrollment share by type of institution, private HEIs had 
a slightly higher enrollment rate than public HEIs in AY 2018–2019, with                               
54 percent compared to 46 percent.  For private HEIs, this result represents a 
steady decline from 79 percent in AY 1994–1995 (CHED 2019) (Figure 1). 
=== PAGE 244 ===

Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act

by the Philippine Statistics Authority (PSA) showed that public HEI 
enrollment remained steady at over 55 percent in 2011, 2013, 2014, 2016, 
2017, and 2019. However, with the introduction of the K-12 program 
in AY 2017–2018, public HEI enrollment reached three-fourths of total

Methods
If expanding access to university education was the goal of the UAQTE 
law, how was the program implemented, especially during the crucial                            
first year of its rollout? This chapter seeks to answer this question                   
through a process evaluation of the law. It examines the theoretical                                                                                                                               
policy logic and feasibility, service delivery, and utilization by assessing                             
early outcomes—including successes, failures, and reactions from 
beneficiaries—and the organizational structure for implementing the program 
(Rossi et al. 2004).
The Theory of Change (TOC) for this process evaluation (Figure 
3) illustrates that the law aims to increase graduation rates in HEIs and TVIs     
while also boosting graduates’ employment rates. The policy envisions 
achieving these goals through increased participation in higher education 
and the provision of quality education. An increase in enrollment rates is 
facilitated by offering (1) FHE and FTVET and (2) TES and the national                                 
SLP to students at risk of dropping out of tertiary education due to financial 
constraints. The TOC assumes that the UAQTE’s resources are adequate, 
sufficient, and provided early; that these resources are transformed into 
adequate facilities, tools, and technical know-how; that an increasing 
=== PAGE 247 ===
216
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
 
 Figure 3. Theory of Change in the Free Tuition Law
HEIs = higher education institutions; TES = tertiary education subsidy; TVIs = technical-
vocational institutions; LUCs= local universities and colleges
Source: Authors’ illustration
number of secondary school graduates will pursue higher education; and  
that HEIs will be encouraged to participate in the program. Failure to meet 
these assumptions will diminish the likelihood of achieving the desired 
outcomes through the pathways identified in the TOC.
A desk review, an online survey among various HEIs across the 
country, in-person key informant interviews (N=18, including 13 HEIs) 
with relevant stakeholders, and administrative data (documentary data) 
collection were conducted. 5 The survey targeted HEIs in the National                                             
Capital Region, Luzon, Visayas, and Mindanao, selected based on publicly 
available directories and existing research. Thirteen out of the 22 targeted 
universities were interviewed in person or via video conference.
5 Since the FTL was implemented during the transition period at the time of the interviews, changes and 
optimizations in the procedural guidelines and issuances of the law were likely. Hence, there is a real 
probability that the information and insights collected during the data collection and while this report is 
being written may not be applicable at the current time.

=== PAGE 248 ===
217
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
What the study found
On the policy’s logic and plausibility
Confusion in the program’s design and limited resources
For the FTL program’s logic and plausibility, the process evaluation found 
that respondents varied in their interpretation of the term “access” to higher 
education. Many perceive “access” as increasing opportunities for students, 
particularly those from low-income families seeking university/college 
entry. These variations in interpretation persist despite the clarity of the 
law’s overall objective, the FTL’s desire to see the complementarity of public                            
and private HEIs and TVIs, and the mechanics of the FHE, TES, and the 
national SLP components.
However, most respondents regard the law’s implementation design 
and its available resources with apprehension. There is skepticism regarding 
the implementation of the components (mainly the TES) and the design 
of the law itself. While the FTL expands educational access to financially 
challenged students, there are concerns about its ability to maintain the 
quality of education provided by HEIs to the beneficiaries. 
Amid FTL implementation, better-off students form the 
bulk of admission passers and enrollees 
Some respondents wondered why only enrollees in government-recognized 
HEIs are targeted and how this arrangement provides “equal” opportunities 
to students. Based on their observations, economically better-off students 
have an advantage in meeting HEI admission policies, even if some                         
private HEIs target certain income classes, such as students from lower- to 
middle-income households. Students from higher-income households are 
typically better equipped and have a higher chance of transitioning from 
senior high school (SHS) to college. 
APIS data support this observation, showing that the percentage 
of poor students enrolled in HEIs remains significantly smaller compared 
to those from middle- to upper-income levels (Figure 4). This trend is 
consistent for public and private HEI enrollments (Figures 5 and 6). From 
2011 to 2017, there was only a slight change in the socioeconomic mix of 
students enrolled in HEIs. Students from families belonging to the poorest 
and second-lowest income quintiles made up less than 28 percent overall 
(Figure 4), below 38 percent in public HEIs (Figure 5), and below 14 percent                                                                   
in private HEIs (Figure 6).

=== PAGE 250 ===
219
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
Doubts about the government’s capacity to bolster 
public HEI quality
Some stakeholders emphasized that a sufficient budget and balanced 
allocation across the law’s four components are essential to achieve the 
intended outcomes. Meanwhile, regarding the objective of providing quality 
tertiary education, some respondents doubt the government’s capacity to 
ensure that public HEIs conform to CHED’s standards, especially with the 
expected influx of students into these institutions. 
Challenges in increasing FTL budgets
CHED stated it is more committed to ensuring HEIs acquire Certificates of 
Program Compliance (COPCs), which requires substantial funding for public 
HEIs to meet standards. However, drastic budget increases for a specific 
program like the FTL would compete with other essential government 
social programs. Some private HEIs noted that the law seems to favor public 
HEIs in terms of funding allocation for implementing FHE. Figure 7 shows 
the allocation of the FTL’s program components.

=== PAGE 251 ===
220
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
On service delivery and utilization
How poor should qualified students be? 
Regarding the FTL program’s service delivery and utilization, most 
respondents noted the tight schedule for meeting documentary compliance. 
In addition, they found the law’s guidelines to be vague, incomplete, 
and confusing. Some respondents encountered difficulties categorizing 
students into “poorest of the poor”, “poor”, and “near poor” households. 6 In 
a follow-up call, CHED attempted to match students’ names with families 
listed in the DSWD’s 4Ps program Listahanan database.  
Needed documents slow down billing and 
reimbursement processes
Some HEIs experienced difficulties in the billing and reimbursement process 
because of changes in the documentary requirements. Others also noted that 
requirements were voluminous and sometimes redundant. Despite these 
lapses in the guidelines, almost all respondents considered these issues and 
challenges as part of the “birthing pains” of policy implementation.
6 Based on a UniFAST memorandum dated September 14, 2018, to all eligible HEIs (i.e., those in the registry).

=== PAGE 252 ===
221
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
Implementation issues include limited capacity and 
delays in guideline issuance
The UniFAST Secretariat confirmed the lack of technical capacity,  
manpower, and other guideposts (e.g., templates for sorting school billings)                                            
as they had to start from scratch. Since the UniFAST budget is lodged under 
CHED, assistance from CHED’s regional staff was crucial. The Secretariat 
noted that technical working groups for program management and 
administration were formed to address management concerns.
However, respondents pointed to the delayed release of guidelines as 
a major issue. Although the implementing rules and regulations (IRR) of             
RA 10931 was released within the prescribed period, the guidelines for 
the TES were issued later than expected: October 2018, after first-semester 
enrollment in most HEIs had already ended. The delayed release of the 
guidelines also confused some HEIs about what to prepare for and how to                                 
implement the TES. Given the constrained timelines, the HEIs stated there                                                                                                                                         
was no opportunity to clarify the guidelines and their potential implications. 
Frequent changes in the documentary requirements (e.g., billing forms) 
added to the confusion.  
Challenges in budget disbursement and reconciliation
Budget disbursement and reconciliation hampered the initial rollout of 
the FHE. The FHE component received a budget of PHP 16 billion, with a                                                                                                                                              
PHP 8 billion cap for each semester of AY 2018–2019 (see Figure 7). 
However, delays occurred in fully disbursing the budget and reconciling 
per-semester budgets to ensure the PHP 8 billion cap was not exceeded.                                              
For the first semester, not all SUCs (59 of 113) and LUCs (50 of 76)                          
received payments for FHE billings—and the total billing even exceeded 
the PHP 8 billion cap for that semester by PHP 3.63 billion (PHP 3.17 billion 
for SUCs and PHP 0.457 billion for LUCs). CHED charged the excess 
disbursements to the second-semester budget of FHE (another PHP 8 billion).
During the second semester, UniFAST required eligible public 
HEIs to submit their billings by February 15, 2019. This deadline provided 
UniFAST enough time to reconcile the sums for the first and second 
semesters for reimbursement. As of December 3, 2019, the total payments for 
the FHE component of the Free Tuition Law amounted to PHP 14.9 billion, 
benefiting 1.2 million students from SUCs and LUCs (Table 3). The excess 
budget of PHP 1.2 billion was allotted to mid-year or “summer” semester 
billings of public HEIs.

The FTL missed out on “other school fees”
Another recurring issue among SUCs is the billing of other school-related 
fees, as these fees were not identified in RA 10931 or its IRR. These fees 7 
form part of the SUC’s income and were used for their operations prior to 
the FHE program. Failure to reimburse these fees would either result in 
schools absorbing the deficiency or passing the cost to students. Eventually, 
CHED’s UniFAST Secretariat reimbursed some of the fees excluded from 
the law, but not student insurance.
Ambiguity in guidelines, requirements, and 
beneficiary selection
Under service delivery and utilization, respondents from private HEIs felt 
that the execution of the FTL program was unclear and unpredictable. 
They cited issues with the guidelines, documentary requirements, processes 
for selecting beneficiaries, and communication with HEIs regarding the 
implementation of the program’s components.  
• Beneficiary selection. UniFAST provided guidelines for selecting 
beneficiaries, explicitly instructing HEIs to use the Listahanan 2.0 
list of poor households and match those with their own individual 
lists of “poorest of the poor, poor, and near-poor” students. 
However, the instructions for submitting these lists varied, 
7 There is also debate regarding subsidizing certain miscellaneous fees (e.g., entrance exams) identified                   
in the law.
=== PAGE 254 ===
223
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
causing confusion among HEIs, especially since UniFAST did not 
provide clear standards of poverty for HEIs to base their student 
beneficiary selection on. Specific issues regarding beneficiary 
selection arose from this guideline. In AY 2018–2019, with the 
TES component receiving PHP 16 billion, only 300,000 of one 
million applicants could receive the subsidy. Consequently, some 
students from Pantawid families were deemed ineligible.
• TES portal and Listahanan-related issues.  Glitches surfaced in the 
TES portal of UniFAST, the platform for HEIs to submit their 
recommended student names. Issues included incorrect postal 
codes for beneficiaries’ locations, technical problems with large 
files, delays in ensuring available facilities and tools for DSWD 
to provide its Listahanan to UniFAST, missing or unmatched 
names, and data discrepancies between the first Listahanan  
round (2010–2011) and the second Listahanan (the basis used 
by UniFAST). Given these issues, UniFAST could not fill all 
300,000 slots for TES 1 and 2; approximately 70,000 TES slots 
remained unfilled in 2019 (Table 4). 
• Gainers and losers among HEIs in terms of enrollment and classroom 
capacities. Using data from the PSA APIS, enrollment data was 
collected from 59 public and private HEIs nationwide. A sharp 
but expected drop in enrollment was observed in 2016 due to the 
introduction of the SHS program (see Figure 8). Subsequently, 
AY 2018–2019 marked the first opportunity for HEIs to recover 
from the enrollment losses in 2016 and 2017. 
On average, the enrollment levels of SUCs have already 
surpassed their pre-K-12 levels, while private HEIs have not yet 
reached theirs. Comparing the two sectors, SUCs appear to have 
recovered more from the enrollment drop than private HEIs. 
Although inconclusive, this situation may support the suspicion 
of a surge in public HEI enrollment due to the FHE. Since 2017,                
the gap in first-year enrollment between public and private HEIs 
has been widening.

=== PAGE 256 ===
225
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
The rise in enrollments in AY 2019–2020, as some 
respondents indicated, can be attributed to the observation 
that the FHE can be accessed without prejudice to the school’s 
admission and retention policies. However, some private HEIs 
experienced declines in enrollment. Certain degree programs at 
private HEIs were significantly impacted by the surge in public 
HEI enrollments facilitated by the FHE. Even students who failed 
these HEIs’ admission examinations were accommodated, with 
bridging programs provided for these new entrants.
Given these measures, some believe that private HEIs 
can become catchment areas for students who did not gain                
admission to SUCs/LUCs, and some teachers are considering 
transferring to these public institutions. For SUCs, the FHE has 
helped increase enrollment levels, but class sizes are growing 
larger (e.g., 60 students per class). This situation raises questions 
about CHED’s prevailing guidelines on teacher-student ratios 
(i.e., classroom capacity) and on maintaining quality education.

=== PAGE 257 ===
226
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
On program organization
Administrative burdens surfaced
On the FT’s program organization, SUC respondents admitted to providing 
additional organizational law to support the rollout of the FTL. They 
relied on volunteer work for administrative tasks for new entrants                                     
(e.g., collection of promissory notes, enrollment processing). Some SUCs 
even had to reconfigure their enrollment systems to integrate processes, 
comply with the law, and harmonize the FTL with other financial 
assistance programs.
However, respondents from private HEIs stated they would not 
compromise the quality of education they provide, including maintaining 
their admission policies. For SUCs experiencing an influx of students, the 
next step is to ensure quality education if the higher demand for student entry 
continues. For its part, CHED is monitoring SUCs and LUCs in consideration               
of establishing satellite campuses to accommodate FTL beneficiaries.
The HEI sector’s numerous associations, 8 all of which CHED 
consulted before and during the FTL’s implementation, had to adjust so that 
member HEIs could accommodate FTL beneficiaries (e.g., communication 
mechanisms). These stakeholders made the inclusion of TES and SLP in 
RA 10931 possible.
Ways forward
The Free Tuition Law is a bold measure aimed at significantly expanding 
Filipino students’ access to university education. This process evaluation 
studied the FTL program’s birth pains, revealing the difficulties of 
implementing a national program designed to enable underprivileged 
but deserving students to access tertiary education. The issues observed 
during the FTL program’s early years included program dynamics, the 
readiness of program implementers and HEIs, the resources allocated to 
target beneficiaries, and the measures to target, search for, and match 
program beneficiaries.  
8 These HEI associations include the Philippine Association of Colleges and Universities, the Coordinating 
Council for Private Educational Associations, the Association of Local Colleges and Universities, and the 
Catholic Educational Association of the Philippines.
=== PAGE 258 ===
227
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
Respondents generally displayed a good understanding of the law’s 
objectives, such as providing universal access to quality tertiary education, 
prioritizing underprivileged students, and ensuring the complementarity of 
public and private education.
Despite this understanding, views varied on whether these objectives              
are realistic and achievable. Some respondents raised concerns about the 
design of the law and its IRR, the implementation of program components, 
and the adequacy of resources to sustain these programs.
The main issue regarding service delivery and utilization was the 
lack of timely and clear guidelines, which led to challenges in processing 
billing requirements and resulted in reimbursement delays. Despite these 
deficiencies, respondents generally described these challenges as “birthing 
pains” of the program and acknowledged the tremendous task that                             
UniFAST faced in implementing the law. These birth pains included limited 
personnel, the scrounging of support for information technology tools, and 
the establishment of collaboration measures (e.g., technical working groups)                        
to ensure smooth program implementation.
On the other hand, most HEIs reported that they did not require 
major adjustments to their enrollment and registration systems to process 
the documentary requirements mandated by UniFAST. However, this was 
not the case for all schools, as school size and information system capacities 
were identified as potential mediating factors. HEIs, particularly university 
associations, noted that UniFAST provided mechanisms for consultation 
and information regarding the law.
Analysis of enrollment data collected from HEIs highlighted the impact                
of the K-12 transition on HEI enrollments, especially as HEI enrollment  
levels recovered by AY 2018–2019. Notably, public HEIs in the sample were 
able to recover more effectively than private HEIs in terms of freshman 
enrollment. This development resulted in low enrollment turnouts for 
private HEIs. While the FTL strove to see complementarity between public 
and private HEIs, there were indications of a widening gap in the share of 
first-year student enrollment between them.  
These findings from the process evaluation study of the UAQTE 
suggest the need for reform measures in the HEI sector and improved 
implementation of RA 10931. 
=== PAGE 259 ===
228
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
First, CHED should strengthen the monitoring of the quality of                     
public and private HEIs and the quality standards it advocates. Meanwhile, 
UniFAST should strictly enforce these rules (including those for program 
beneficiaries) and regularly review school quality and performance to 
ensure the optimal utilization of funds allocated to the programs under 
the law. HEIs should also seek ways to promote compliance with quality 
standards, including institutional or program-based accreditation for  
LUCs and private HEIs and applications for COPCs by SUCs. A positive 
outcome of the FTL for LUCs is that these HEIs are now pursuing 
accreditation with higher education accrediting bodies, a goal they had 
vigorously pursued before these programs began.
Second, SUCs and LUCs should be monitored to ensure they do not 
exceed their respective carrying capacities. UniFAST should continue to 
oversee this issue in the coming years, as there are clear incentives to enroll 
more students in SUCs and LUCs.
Third, government stakeholders should leverage the FTL program 
subsidies to enhance the quality of HEIs. Some HEIs recommended that 
the government explore providing subsidies directly to schools for quality 
improvements and research programs and facilities.
Fourth, CHED may need to reassess the FTL program design. Some 
studies show that free tuition schemes in various countries have fallen 
short of their objectives, particularly regarding equity. Disadvantages faced 
by students from lower socioeconomic classes in primary and secondary 
education remain barriers to entry into university education, despite tuition 
subsidies. Several interviewees raised similar issues to those found in the                                                                                          
literature (e.g., Buckner 2013; Bucarey 2018; Habibi and El-Hamidi 2016);                                                                                                                                     
some interviewees even suggested alternative schemes, such as the 
socialization of tuition, as potentially more efficient ways to leverage 
limited government higher education funds. 
Orbeta and Paqueo (2017) noted that untargeted tuition subsidies 
may worsen income inequalities. Program implementers and national 
legislators should remain open and flexible to facilitating redesign and 
improvements to the program to better democratize access to tertiary 
education, particularly for the poor.
Fifth, programmatic implementation reforms are necessary. These 
reforms include (a) strengthening the targeting of beneficiaries for the 
TES and improving the use of the Listahanan to identify beneficiaries,                                         
=== PAGE 260 ===
229
Process Evaluation of the Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act
(b) providing clearer and more definite guidelines that HEIs can follow 
within a reasonable period, (c) calibrating calls for applications and 
deadlines for submitting requirements, (d) reviewing the inclusion of 
miscellaneous and other school fees in the subsidies provided so that 
SUC and LUC operations are not affected, (e) conducting more efficient 
consultations and orientations for HEIs, and (f) expanding organizational 
support for UniFAST—at national and regional levels—to better 
implement the FTL.

=== PAGE 263 ===
232
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
The Philippine government has long been firmly effecting programs to 
provide quality higher education and make it accessible to more Filipino 
students from poor households. Ensuring that students have access to 
quality higher education is a key government objective. Until now, funding 
public higher education institutions (HEIs) has been the main policy tool 
to achieve this objective. However, despite the expansion of the number of 
publicly HEIs in the country in recent years, studies show that the number 
of state universities and colleges (SUCs) has no significant correlation with 
the enrollment of poor students in HEIs (Orbeta et al. 2016). Moreover, 
the returns to higher education continue to be high (Paqueo et al. 2012), 
indicating that completing higher education remains a good investment, and 
supporting poor students in completing their higher education is a proven 
blueprint for breaking the cycle of poverty.
In 2012, the Philippine government implemented the Students 
Grants-in-Aid Program for Poverty Alleviation (SGP-PA) to provide poor 
but capable learners access to higher or college education. The program 
aims to increase the number of higher education graduates among poor 
households by directly providing grants for their education in selected 
SUCs. While there are other grants-in-aid programs, the SGP-PA has                                                                                                                                       
two unique features: (1) it is well-targeted to identified poor households, 
especially beneficiaries of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program (Pantawid 
Pamilya); and (2) it provides a substantial grant amount sufficient to cover                 
all normal education expenses, including a living allowance.
This chapter reviews the academic performance of the grantees 
of the SGP-PA and the expanded SGP-PA (ESGP-PA) and compares it 
with that of their nonbeneficiary peers. 1 Leveraging data from selected 
SUCs, this study analyzes factors affecting academic achievements, 
such as admission test scores and semestral grades, especially in major 
subjects. It also documents the challenges the program faces and provides 
recommendations on how to address these challenges and refine existing 
guidelines to effectively achieve the program’s key objectives.
1 See Silverberg and Orbeta (2018) for the main report.
=== PAGE 264 ===
233
Improving Access of the Poor to College Education
SGP-PA (ESGP-PA) and its implementation 
The SGP-PA was implemented in the Academic Year (AY) 2012–2013 
through the Commission on Higher Education (CHED) Memorandum 
Order 09 (s.2012). The program’s implementers include selected SUCs, 
the Department of Social Welfare and Development (DSWD), and the 
Department of Labor and Employment. It started with 4,041 beneficiaries 
from identified poor households in 609 focus municipalities covered by the 
DSWD’s Pantawid Pamilya program, a conditional cash transfer (CCT) 
initiative. In AY 2014–2015, the ESGP-PA was rolled out, covering                                                    
36,412 beneficiaries and bringing the total number of beneficiaries to 40,453. 
The number of implementing SUCs also grew from 35 to 112 nationwide.
The SGP-PA provides a maximum total grant amount of PHP 60,000              
per academic year per student beneficiary, covering tuition and other 
fees (PHP 10,000 per semester), textbooks and other learning materials                       
(PHP 2,500 per semester), and stipend (PHP 3,500 per month for                                                                                                                               
10 school months). 
Selection of grantees
The grantees had to meet general requirements for eligibility and comply 
with documentary requirements to be listed in the program. They must 
be identified as Pantawid Pamilya beneficiaries, be no older than 30 years 
at the time of selection, be physically and mentally fit, have completed 
high school or its equivalent, not be covered by other higher education 
scholarships or public institution grants, pass the entrance exam and 
academic requirements set by the leading SUC, pass the requirements 
set by the National SGP-PA committee, and enroll, shift, or transfer to                            
CHED priority programs, preferably in the leading identified SUCs most 
accessible from their residence. 
The grantees were required to submit documentary requirements, 
including an accomplished SGP-PA application form, a certification from 
DSWD confirming Pantawid Pamilya beneficiary status, a birth certificate 
certified by the National Statistics Office 2 or local civil registrar, a health 
certificate issued by a government physician, Form 138 or proof of passing 
2 Now Philippine Statistics Authority
=== PAGE 265 ===
234
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
the Philippine Educational Placement Test, and a signed commitment of 
participation. The participation commitment includes clauses requiring 
grantees to take a full course load each term, complete the program within              
the prescribed four-year period, and maintain satisfactory academic 
performance in accordance with the policies and standards of the SUC. 
The program intends to award grants to those expected to be the 
first college graduates in their households. 3 The selection process has 
also been made very open to accommodate even those who have been 
out of school for a long time and those who are married. Proportional 
regional allocation of slots was also considered. However, the ESGP-PA 
has changed the eligibility requirements for inclusion in the program:    
potential grantees can only have been out of school for a maximum of               
five years, a cap not in place for the first batch. Additionally, the age ceiling                
of 30 was strictly enforced for the second batch of grantees. 
Data collection
Data on the grantees and their peers 4 was obtained in collaboration                    
with selected SUCs (8 SGP-PA-implementing SUCs and another                                   
8 ESGP-PA-implementing SUCs). 5 The participating SGP-PA SUCs 
received a PHP 300,000 grant to collect data over four semesters for selected 
subjects, while the ESGP-PA SUCs received a PHP 225,000 grant to 
collect data for two semesters for selected subjects. The SUCs were                                                            
asked to submit data for both the grantees and their peers, along with 
a report on implementation, program issues, and other SGP-PA and                                                                                                     
ESGP-PA-related experiences.6 The data reports were reviewed, and those 
with encoding errors, duplicate entries, or incomplete information were 
returned to the SUCs for correction.  
3 To achieve this, the DSWD had to sift through millions of eligible Pantawid Pamilya households 
listed in the Listahan, a roster of approximately 10 million households that serves as the source of 
Pantawid Pamilya beneficiaries. 
4 “Peers” refer to the batchmates of the grantees in the same course.
5 SUCs were selected based on the total number of grantees, their expression of interest to participate, 
and the nomination of a collaborating faculty-researcher or department as certified by the school head. 
6 Due to delays in processing the Memorandum of Agreement between the Philippine Institute for 
Development Studies and the SUCs, only four SGP-PA SUCs and five ESGP-PA SUCs have been 
included in the analysis presented in this chapter.
=== PAGE 266 ===
235
Improving Access of the Poor to College Education
Methods of analysis
The following variables were analyzed and compared with those of the 
grantees’ peers. 
• Profile of students. This includes demographic and socioeconomic 
variables, such as gender, civil status, age of entry, parents’ 
educational attainment, type of high school attended, average 
annual household income, and household size. Since socioeconomic 
profiles are expected to affect academic performance, a test on 
the difference in means was conducted to ascertain whether there 
are statistically significant differences between the two groups.  
• Entrance exam scores and semestral grades of students. The means and 
standard deviations of entrance exam scores and semestral grades 
(including scores in Science, Mathematics, and English) were 
obtained for both groups. A test on the difference in means 
was also performed to determine any statistically significant 
differences between the two groups. The entrance exam scores 
are expected to indicate the grantees’ readiness for higher 
education at the point of entry, while the semestral grades reflect 
their performance in banner subjects. 
• Relative academic performance controlling for entrance exam scores 
and other socioeconomic characteristics.  To provide evidence on 
the relative academic performance of grantees and their peers, 
regression analyses of semestral grades in banner courses were 
performed. These analyses controlled for entrance exam scores, 
which are expected to provide a standard or baseline measure 
of academic preparation at entry and other socioeconomic 
characteristics. In addition, these analyses offer a more 
comprehensive understanding of relative academic performance 
by controlling for known important determinants, which cannot 
be done with bivariate analyses.7 
7 Bivariate analysis is a type of statistical analysis where two variables are observed against each other.                
One variable is dependent, while the other is independent. These variables are denoted by X and Y.
=== PAGE 267 ===
236
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Results and analysis
SGP-PA results
The profile analysis showed that SGP-PA grantees are mostly female, have 
an average age of 19.7 years, and have been out of school for two years.                             
The program’s lenient selection process accommodates those who have 
been out of school longer and those who are married, resulting in a higher 
proportion of married students among the grantees compared to their 
peers. The grantees are also older, with almost 20 percent being over                                                                                                                                             
22 years old, compared to only 2.5 percent of their peers. Consequently, on 
average, the grantees have been out of school longer—twice the time their  
peers have been out of school. Fourteen percent of the grantees graduated                           
from high school before 2008, indicating they had been out of school for 
five years or more. Unsurprisingly, most grantees graduated from public                       
high schools.
The peers’ parents have higher educational attainment than the 
grantees’ parents. Eighty-seven percent of the peers’ fathers have at least  
some high school education, as opposed to only 47 percent of the grantees’ 
fathers. In comparison, 91 percent of the peers’ mothers have at least some  
high school education, while only 56 percent of the grantees’ mothers have 
similar qualifications. Although the program intended to cater to poor 
households without college graduates, 1.9 percent of the grantees’ fathers 
and 3.7 percent of their mothers have completed college. 
The profile also reflects the economic disadvantage of the grantees 
compared to their peers. The grantees are from substantially larger 
households, and on average, the annual income of a peer’s household is 
three times that of a grantee’s household. 
Academically, the first wave of grantees lagged behind their peers 
upon entering university or college. The entrance exam scores of grantees 
were 6.61 percentage points lower than those of their peers. This result is 
significant at the 1 percent level. Figure 1 shows the mean scores of both 
groups and their standard deviations.
Despite initially lagging behind their peers, the grantees appeared  
to have caught up by the first semester of the second year, except in the 
English subject, where a 4-point difference remained by the second semester                                  
of the second year (see Figure 2). The semestral grades of SGP-PA grantees   
and their peers can be found in Appendix A.

238
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
ESGP-PA results
Most ESGP-PA grantees are female and entered university at an average 
age of 18. ESGP-PA grantees are, on average, half a year older than their 
peers. However, the average time gap between high school and college is 
approximately the same for both groups. Further analysis showed that a 
higher percentage of peers, compared to grantees, graduated high school 
five or more years before entering university. For the second wave of the 
program, the guidelines were updated to allow only potential grantees who 
are at most 30 years of age. 
As expected, the grantees’ economic disadvantage vis-à-vis their peers 
persists. The average annual household income of the peers is 1.6 times 
higher than that of the grantees, with the per capita income of the grantees’ 
households being only half that of the peers’. The grantees typically come 
from larger households, with an average of 7.2 members compared to an 
average of 6 members in their peers’ households.
The peers’ parents are more highly educated than the grantees’ parents. 
Specifically, 73 percent of the peers’ fathers (versus 53% of the grantees’ 
fathers) and 80 percent of the peers’ mothers (versus 68% of the grantees’ 
mothers) have at least some high school education. Like in the first wave,                  
a small percentage of the grantees’ parents have completed college. 
The grantees’ disadvantage compared to their peers is primarily 
economic. Not only do they enter university at around the same age, but                 
they also do not have a disadvantage in the length of time they may have 
been out of school between high school and college. Academically, the 
grantees are competitive with their peers upon entry into the university, 
even scoring higher on average in the entrance exams (Figure 3). The 
grantees scored, on average, 4.3 percentage points higher than their peers. 
This difference is statistically significant at the 1 percent level.  
The academic advantage of the grantees over their peers remained 
during the first year of studies except in some subjects, such as Mathematics, 
where the grantees fell behind their peers in the second semester. Graphs                      
on means and standard deviations are in Appendix B.
=== PAGE 270 ===
239
Improving Access of the Poor to College Education
Program dropouts
Data on dropouts are available for only three SGP-PA SUCs. Grantees who 
drop out are referred to the DSWD for case management before their grants 
are terminated. The program’s failure rate is determined by the number of 
grantees who fail their degrees. 
The most often cited reasons for dropping out include academic 
difficulties, pregnancy, and disinterest in further education. Financial 
difficulties were also commonly mentioned. Due to some birthing pains 
of the program, there was a delay in the release of funds to the SUCs, 
causing grantees to wait for their stipends and spend out of pocket in the 
meantime. For the second wave of the program, this delay in fund release 
has been addressed, and the grant is intended to cover the full cost of 
education, so this issue should no longer affect the second batch of grantees.
Other reasons for dropping out include health issues, familial 
obligations, and difficulty adapting to the cultural change of relocating to a 
more urbanized setting.

=== PAGE 271 ===
240
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Programmatic and implementation issues 
The SUCs brought up some recurring bottlenecks, as well as programmatic  
and implementation issues, including: 
• Compromising university regulations to accommodate the grantees. 
The CHED Memorandum Order 9 (s.2012), which contains the 
guidelines used for the pilot, states that SUCs must “administer 
admission examinations or other appropriate admission 
requirements in line with the objectives of the Disbursement 
Acceleration Program”. However, SUCs have had to waive 
entrance exams or lower the passing scores to allow more 
potential grantees to qualify. The guidelines for the ESGP-PA 
have been relaxed, with Section 8.4.3 of the Joint Memorandum 
Circular No. 2014-1 stating that SUCs must “administer flexible 
admission policies at no cost to students” (CHED et al. 2014). 
For the second batch, the late provision of the list of grantees 
made it difficult for SUCs to adhere to their admission policies. 
• Lack of staff for program management and capacity to handle 
additional problems. Some SUCs have maintained that the 3 percent                                          
allocation of the total grant for administrative expenses is 
often insufficient to hire additional or specialized staff to deal                         
with the special needs of the grantees. These needs range                    
from academic support to individualized counseling. Some 
SUCs have encountered behavioral issues among grantees that 
require special attention from trained counselors, but they lack 
access to such professionals.
• Academic difficulties experienced by grantees. Most SUCs signified 
the need for a bridging program for the grantees, both before 
the school year starts and between terms, to help them cope 
academically. This need was particularly true for the first batch 
of grantees, most of whom had been out of school for years and 
required refresher courses, even for basic learning techniques. 
• Health concerns of the grantees. Medical and laboratory tests are 
typically part of the admission requirements for SUCs, but some 
schools had to waive these to avoid imposing out-of-pocket                                                                                                                                
expenses on grantees. Some grantees were found to have 
undiagnosed illnesses that manifested later in the term.                      
=== PAGE 272 ===
241
Improving Access of the Poor to College Education
Those who required medical attention and hospitalization often 
sought help from the SUC to settle their medical bills. Some 
grantees were found to be suffering from tuberculosis (TB) 
and worms. Those with TB were allowed to go home to avail 
themselves of the free TB-DOTS program at their rural health 
units, resulting in them being excused from classes for at least 
a month. 
• Budget allocation for out-of-term scholastic activities.  The SUCs 
remark that there is no budget allocation for summer courses, 
on-the-job training programs, national competency exams, 
field trips, and thesis completion. All these activities, some of 
which are conducted outside regular term time, are essential 
for students to complete their degrees. Summer courses are 
often required between the third and fourth years of study. 
Furthermore, the grant allocation only covers four years of 
education. Students who take longer to complete their degrees or 
are enrolled in five-year courses (such as engineering, which is 
among the priority courses the grantees can choose from) must                                                                                                                               
pay out-of-pocket.
Another issue occasionally arises when a grantee drops 
out and must be replaced. The replacement can only use the 
remaining allocation left by the one who dropped out. This rule 
requires the SUC to find a replacement at the same year level; 
otherwise, the replacement will have to pay the fees for any 
years beyond the remaining allocation.
Implemented interventions 
• Academic services. SUCs provided additional academic services 
for grantees to help them cope with their lessons, especially 
in Sciences and Mathematics. Although some SUCs already 
implemented this in the first wave, remedial and mentoring 
programs were included in the SUCs’ list of responsibilities                 
for the second wave. 
• Social services. Counseling is offered to address recurring 
behavioral issues such as tardiness, absenteeism, and violations 
of dorm and school policies. Counseling and monitoring are also 
=== PAGE 273 ===
242
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
provided for bullying-related problems. In collaboration with 
DSWD field offices, the SUCs conduct personality development 
and other social activities to help the grantees adjust to university 
life. Some SUCs also conduct financial literacy seminars to assist 
the grantees in managing their stipends more effectively.
• Basic necessities.  Most grantees come from areas far from the                      
SUC where they enroll, often coming from other provinces. 
SUCs assist in finding accommodation, either through dormitories 
or accredited boarding houses. For SUCs with dormitories, there 
are often not enough slots to accommodate all the grantees. SUCs 
frequently act as guarantors for advance payments or deposits 
required to secure boarding house rooms. Some SUCs have also 
arranged for food services through the school canteen, providing 
the grantees with three meals daily, 30 days a month during the 
school term, with the cost deducted from their stipends. 
• Health services. The provision of financial assistance for grantees               
who fall ill or have accidents varies among SUCs. Some SUCs 
resort to “passing the hat”, with faculty and other students 
sometimes contributing. Other SUCs offer medical coverage 
through school insurance. 
Ways forward
While the SGP-PA and ESGP-PA have been critical in providing quality 
education to the poor, there is still much room for improvement.                                  
It is recommended that the following be given attention for future                    
policy directions:
• Enforcement of entrance exams. The relationship between entrance 
exam scores and academic performance has been established in 
the literature and from the SGP-PA and ESGP-PA data. Given 
the program’s thrust, it is important that the selected grantees 
have a relatively high likelihood of completing their degrees.  
Enforcing admission exams is one way to achieve this objective. 
Conducting the admission exams will also serve as a useful 
baseline for monitoring the grantees’ progress over time. 
=== PAGE 274 ===
243
Improving Access of the Poor to College Education
• Importance of data keeping. Monitoring and comparing the 
grantees’ progress to their peers over time is essential for 
assessing the program’s efficacy. Good data is needed for monitoring 
and evaluation. Creating and regularly updating a database will 
warrant faster and more effective evaluation process. It will also 
allow the SUCs to track their grantees and regular students and 
design necessary interventions. 
• Designing the selection process and interventions for the program.               
The program’s failure rate is determined by the number 
of grantees who did not complete their degree or dropped 
out. There is a need to probe deeper into the reasons behind                           
these dropouts. Data collected from three SUCs indicate that at 
least 39 percent of the dropouts were unprepared for tertiary 
education, and 11 percent were uninterested or preferred to 
work. This finding has important implications for the selection 
process, reiterating the need to identify those willing to pursue 
tertiary education. Other reasons for dropping out include 
pregnancy, health issues, and familial obligations. There are 
aspects of the program that extend beyond the academic realm. 
The cultural change experienced by the grantees from being 
relocated to a more urbanized setting than what they have been 
accustomed to is a legitimate issue raised by the SUCs. These 
issues should be considered when designing interventions for 
the program.
• Cooperation with other government agencies.  Health concerns of 
the grantees during term time, especially hospitalization, are 
a recurring problem brought up by SUCs. As members of 
Pantawid Pamilya households, the grantees should be covered 
by the Philippine Health Insurance Corporation (PhilHealth). 
Still, some grantees are unaware of the program and the 
coverage they are entitled to. It is important to inform them 
about their coverage and ensure they have membership cards to 
access benefits without additional documentation. PhilHealth’s 
membership guidelines need to be clarified, especially for 
grantees over the age of 21 who no longer qualify as dependents. 
=== PAGE 275 ===
244
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
The implementing agencies also need to partner with the 
Commission on Audit (COA) to set guidelines for the grantees 
and advise them on how to utilize the grant more efficiently.                                   
For most SUCs, tuition is below the allocated PHP 10,000.     
SUCs typically have to return the remaining balance instead 
of utilizing it for other necessary school-related expenses. As 
mentioned in the previous section, students incur additional 
expenses that are not covered by the grant. The savings from 
tuition can be used for other expenses if permitted by the 
program’s rules and authorized by COA.  
• Grantees need additional academic support. SUCs have noted that 
grantees need academic support outside of regularly scheduled 
classes, especially those from the first batch. Universities have 
observed that the grantees would benefit from a bridging program 
before the school year starts to reacclimate to a learning 
environment and catch up on basic theory and learning techniques. 
Tutorials are also needed, which can be devised through a “buddy 
system” between senior students and grantees within the same 
degree program. These academic support systems must be 
integrated into the program design to ensure all SUCs involved 
can and will provide them. 
The observations, analyses, and recommendations in this chapter 
provide guidelines that will increase the likelihood that the program will 
attain its purpose and better serve the nation’s learning public.

Chapter 11
Demand Intervention: 
Assessing the Impact 
of the Pantawid 
Pamilyang Pilipino 
Program on Education*1
Aniceto C. Orbeta Jr.
Kris Ann M. Melad
Nina Victoria V. Araos
* This chapter is a condensed version of PIDS Research Paper Series 2023-06, titled “Reassessing 
the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino  Program: Results of the Third Wave Impact 
Evaluation”. The full study is available at https://doi.org/10.62986/rps2023.06.
=== PAGE 281 ===
250
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
The Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program (Pantawid Pamilya) is the 
Philippines’ version of a conditional cash transfer (CCT) program. It aims               
to break the intergenerational transmission of poverty in the country                                                                                      
by investing in the human capital of children from poor households. 
Officially launched in 2008 by the Department of Social Welfare and 
Development (DSWD), Pantawid Pamilya registered approximately 
300,000 household beneficiaries in its first year. It has since expanded to 
cover more than 4 million poor households across nearly all municipalities 
and provinces nationwide. 
Global evaluations of CCT programs have shown positive impacts 
such as smoothing the consumption of beneficiaries, increasing investment                 
in human capital, and alleviating poverty (Fiszbein et al. 2009). CCT programs 
have been found to result in increased school enrollment and utilization of 
healthcare services, with several studies indicating longer-term impacts 
(Gertler et al. 2012). In the Philippines,  evaluations indicate that the 
Pantawid Pamilya program is on track to achieve its goals of improving child 
education and health outcomes through human capital investments and 
other support mechanisms (DSWD 2015). However, mixed results have 
been observed for some outcomes, and no impact was noted for critical 
indicators like total household consumption and infant immunization in the 
last two impact evaluations.
Three impact evaluations of the Pantawid Pamilya program have 
been conducted. The first evaluation (IE Wave 1) in 2011 showed that the 
program had reached most of its key objectives in improving education                                                                                                                            
and health outcomes. The second assessment (IE Wave 2) in 2013 
also yielded positive results, confirming that the program is still on 
track to keeping children healthy and in school even after five years of 
implementation. The third-wave impact evaluation (IE Wave 3), conducted 
in the 10th year of program implementation, reassessed its impact on 
short-term and intermediate outcomes and reexamined the mixed results 
from previous evaluations. 
=== PAGE 282 ===
251
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
This chapter presents the findings of the main study from IE Wave 3,                                            
which employed regression discontinuity design (RDD), focusing on the 
education results. 1 Based on these findings, lessons from over a decade 
of government innovations are discussed, and recommendations are put 
forward to address issues of access and quality in education. 
Summary of previous impact 
evaluation studies
In 2011, the first impact evaluation of the Pantawid Pamilya program was 
conducted by the DSWD, with support from Australia’s Department of 
Foreign Affairs and Trade, the World Bank, and the Asian Development 
Bank. The evaluation covered a sample of 3,742 households from 4 provinces 
and 8 municipalities across Luzon, the Visayas, and Mindanao. A phased-in 
randomized control trial (RCT) evaluation design was used for the main 
analysis involving 1,418 poor households from 130 barangays (villages), 
half of which were randomly assigned as “control” and the other half as 
“treatment” areas. The treatment areas were exposed to the program for                                           
two and a half years, while the control areas were withheld from receiving 
program benefits to serve as a source of counterfactual information and to 
observe what would have happened without CCT implementation. The 
remainder of the sample was used to measure the unexpected or spillover 
effects of the program among the nontarget population living in the program 
areas. The program’s impact was then estimated by comparing outcomes 
between eligible households in the treatment areas and those in the control 
areas. Results showed increased school enrollment among younger      
children (3–11 years old) and increased school attendance among children 
6–17 years old. Additionally, beneficiary households invested more in 
the education and health of their children and spent less on vice goods 
compared with nonbeneficiaries (DSWD and World Bank 2013).
1 For more information, including health outcomes, refer to the main paper (Orbeta et al. 2021, 2023).
=== PAGE 283 ===
252
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
When the IE Wave 2 was conducted in 2013, the program had 
expanded its coverage to almost all areas of the country, making it difficult 
to find representative areas not yet exposed to the CCT program for an 
experimental study. Thus, RDD2  was employed, using ineligible households 
near the cutoff (poverty threshold) as the comparison group. The survey 
covered 5,041 households from 30 randomly selected municipalities                                                       
(10 per island group) across 26 provinces, including households just below 
(poor and eligible) and just above (near-poor, not eligible) the poverty 
threshold.3 Findings showed that after five years of implementation, 
Pantawid Pamilya was still on track to keeping children in school and healthy. 
Among the notable results that impact education are: the program keeps 
older children in school, with gross enrollment among high school 
children ages 12–15 years being higher for beneficiary children; the 
program increases beneficiary households’ investment in education, 
with these households spending PHP 206 more per school-aged child per 
year compared to nonbeneficiary households; and the program helps 
r e d u c e  c h i l d  l a b o r  h o u r s  a m o n g  p o o r  c h i l d r e n ,  w i t h  b e n e fi c i a r y  c h i l d r e n                                                                                                                                  
ages 10–14 years working seven days less per month compared to 
nonbeneficiary children. As child labor is one of the reasons children cannot 
attend school regularly, this result helps explain the increased school 
attendance (DSWD 2014).
In summary, findings from the first two waves of impact evaluation 
(DSWD and World Bank 2013; DSWD 2014) showed that Pantawid 
Pamilya has successfully achieved its short-term objectives of increasing 
school enrollment, school attendance, and access to key maternal and child    
healthcare services. However, some findings and observations require                                                    
closer investigation, such as the lack of impact on mean per capita 
consumption and childhood immunization coverage, as well as the 
persistent incidence of child labor among beneficiary households. 
2 RDD is a quasi-experimental method that creates a valid counterfactual by comparing households                 
near a predetermined cutoff. In this case, households are ranked according to their PMT scores, and 
their assignment to treatment and comparison groups depends on whether their PMT scores are                                
below or above the respective provincial poverty threshold and whether the household has children             
ages 0–18 years or pregnant members.
