program for at least two years before data collection.
=== PAGE 284 ===
253
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
About the Pantawid Pamilya
Pantawid Pamilya aims to break the cycle of intergenerational poverty by 
encouraging poor households to invest in their health and education. It seeks                 
to improve the health of young children and mothers through increased              
use of preventive healthcare services and better health-seeking behavior, 
increase children’s enrollment and attendance rates in school, and promote  
family development and community participation among beneficiaries. 
Program conditions
The Pantawid Pamilya program provides grants to beneficiaries who meet 
conditionalities related to the education and health of children and pregnant 
women. In addition, a unique third conditionality focused on family 
development is introduced and tied to the continuous provision of health 
grants. To receive the cash grants, beneficiaries must comply with three sets 
of conditions: (1) time-specific take-up of basic maternal and child health 
services, (2) enrollment and regular attendance in schools, 4 and (3) regular 
attendance at family development sessions (FDS). 5 The health conditions 
apply to children from birth up to 14 years of age and pregnant women in the 
household, while the education conditions apply to children ages 3–18 years. 
The specific conditions for health and education are detailed in the Pantawid 
Pamilya operations manual (DSWD 2015).  
Eligibility
To be eligible for the Pantawid Pamilya program, households must be 
identified as poor by the Listahanan, 6 have a pregnant member or at 
least one child aged 0‒18 years at the time of assessment, and willing to 
commit to comply with the program conditionalities. The Listahanan 
targets beneficiaries through a household assessment survey and uses a 
4 Children ages 3‒5 years must enroll in daycare or kindergarten, while children ages 6‒18 years should 
enroll in elementary or high school. They should attend at least 85 percent of school days monthly.
5 The objective of the FDS is to educate beneficiaries, enabling them to fulfill their roles in the human 
capital development of their children and participate as active community members. The FDS also serves               
as a venue to remind beneficiaries to comply with program conditions and encourage peer support 
among them (DSWD 2015). 
6 Formerly known as the National Household Targeting System for Poverty Reduction Program (NHTS-PR)
=== PAGE 285 ===
254
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
proxy means test (PMT) methodology to predict household income based 
on household characteristics (e.g., household composition, education of  
members, housing conditions, assets, tenure status, access to basic services) 
and regional control variables. 
Transfer package and payment system
Currently, the program provides three types of cash grants to beneficiary 
households: education grants, health grants, and rice subsidies. Cash grants  
are delivered to beneficiaries through two modes of payment: bank cash 
cards, where grants are withdrawn via automated teller machines (ATMs);   
and over-the-counter transactions, where grants are provided directly to 
beneficiaries in cash. The Land Bank of the Philippines (LBP) manages 
the payment delivery system as the program’s authorized government 
depository bank. Therefore, in areas with available ATMs, beneficiaries 
are enrolled to have LBP cash card accounts, and their cash grants are 
transferred to these accounts during payout schedules every  two months.
Program implementation
The beneficiaries’ compliance with the program conditions is monitored 
every two months through a process conducted jointly by the DSWD, 
the Department of Health through the local government units, and the 
Department of Education. The average compliance rates reported by the 
program are high. For example, based on DSWD administrative data  
from 2010 to 2018, the average compliance rate was 95 percent for health                         
and education conditions. These high compliance rates suggest that 
program beneficiaries consistently utilize health services and send their 
children to school.
Program modifications
Since its launch, the Pantawid Pamilya program has undergone several 
design modifications, including changes in age coverage, exit policy, open 
selection of children to be monitored, and the addition of a rice subsidy.
Following a key policy recommendation based on the results of the 
first impact evaluation by the DSWD and World Bank (2013) and analyses 
by Paqueo et al. (2013) and Reyes et al. (2013) to expand age coverage, the 
DSWD extended the education grants to children up to 18 years old in  
=== PAGE 286 ===
255
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
2014. Along with this, the education grant was increased from PHP 300 
to PHP 500, considering the higher expenses of high school education and 
the greater opportunity costs for older children. In addition to extending 
the coverage to older children ages 15–18 and differentiating grants for 
elementary and high school, the DSWD lifted the five-year limit on 
program participation. Furthermore, there is a new program exit policy 
w h e r e  b e n e fi c i a r y  h o u s e h o l d s  c e a s e  r e c e i v i n g  p r o g r a m  b e n e fi t s  w h e n                                                                                     
the last of their (three) children beneficiaries in education either graduates 
f r o m  h i g h  s c h o o l  o r  t u r n s  1 9  y e a r s  o l d ,  w h i c h e v e r  c o m e s  fi r s t .  I n  2 0 1 5 ,                                                                                                                                             
parent beneficiaries were also allowed to reselect children within the 
household to be monitored under the education conditionality in an activity 
called “open selection”. 
In January 2017,  the program added a rice subsidy as a third type                      
of cash grant on top of the education and health grants. This subsidy aims                
to increase the food consumption of beneficiaries, provided they comply 
with the program’s conditionalities.
Analytical framework
The Pantawid Pamilya program aims to break the intergenerational cycle 
of poverty 7 by promoting investment in the human capital of children 
from poor households. The strategy encourages these households to 
invest in their children’s education and health, thereby increasing their 
chances of higher productivity and income in the future (see Chapter 3 
for more information about intergenerational education mobility). The                     
program aims to achieve these goals through four key pathways, helping 
beneficiaries accumulate human capital for better access to opportunities 
and improved welfare. 
7 In his book Under-Rewarded Efforts: The Elusive Quest for Prosperity in Mexico , Levy (2018) notes that 
although CCTs—such as Mexico’s former Oportunidades, now Prospera—generate improvements 
in health, education, and other socioeconomic outcomes, they are not sufficient to address 
intergenerational poverty. This insufficiency stems from the influence of the larger socioeconomic 
environment composed of labor or entrepreneur-worker relations, taxation, and market conditions                
that Levy collectively terms E(L, T, M). These factors hinder poor workers from obtaining better    
and more stable jobs (Levy 2018). Given this, Levy argues that rather than relying primarily on 
CCTs and their complementary programs as the primary poverty alleviation strategy, there should                                                             
be a shift in policy focus toward addressing stagnant growth and productivity to raise worker welfare 
and break the intergenerational transmission of poverty.
=== PAGE 287 ===
256
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Income augmentation pathway
The additional income is expected to smoothen the consumption of poor 
households, especially for food and basic needs. In the short term, it 
is expected to help lower the incidence of income poverty and hunger                                                                                                                    
and lessen the effects of economic shocks. In the long term, it is expected 
to contribute to higher savings and improve the investment behavior                                  
of households. 
Education pathway
By providing the education grant and requiring children to enroll in 
and attend school at least 85 percent of the time, education outcomes 
are expected to improve. Short-term outcomes include higher school 
enrollment and attendance rates. Meanwhile, medium-term outcomes 
relate to better school performance, such as increased promotion, 
completion, and transition rates and reduced repetition and dropout rates. 
The long-term outcome is for child beneficiaries to finish high school                   
or attain higher education.
 
Health pathway
In terms of health outcomes, the program is expected to increase the 
utilization of preventive health care and improve the overall health-seeking 
behavior of beneficiaries. Short-term intermediate outcomes include 
increased immunization, regular growth monitoring, and preventive 
healthcare visits for children. In addition, with the expected increase in 
households’ food consumption and regular growth monitoring, children are                      
also expected to have better nutrition outcomes in the short and long term.
FDS or social pathway
Through the FDS, beneficiaries are expected to gain increased knowledge 
of parenting and awareness of family and social issues; improved parenting 
attitudes and practices on child-rearing, finances, gender, and marital 
relations; and greater appreciation for education and health. In the 
medium term, beneficiaries are expected to actively engage in community 
development affairs and demand better services. The long-term outcome 
is for beneficiaries to experience improved subjective welfare, aspirations,  
and social integration.
=== PAGE 288 ===
257
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
The achievement of program outcomes relies on several assumptions:                  
the grant amount is sufficient to incentivize households to comply with 
program conditions and invest in their children’s human capital; the               
supply conditions support the realization of this goal (i.e., the availability 
and accessibility of health and school facilities); and the quality of services                    
is acceptable. 
The third wave of evaluation focused on hypotheses related to 
health, education, household welfare, access to government services, 
and other behavioral outcomes. These hypotheses 8 address the short-, 
medium-, and long-term outcomes outlined in the program’s Theory of 
Change. The hypotheses tested regarding education and child labor pertain 
to the program’s impact on the school participation of children and other 
education outcomes, the incidence and time spent on child labor, and 
household investments (i.e., expenditures) in children’s education.
Results
This chapter presents the results of the third-wave evaluation, focusing 
solely on the program’s impact on education. For details on the program’s 
impact on other indicators, refer to Orbeta et al. (2021, 2023). 
The tables in this section present the estimated impact of the program 
using three types of bandwidths: (1) coverage error rate (CER)-optimal 
bandwidth, (2) mean square error (MSE)-optimal bandwidth, and (3) full 
sample bandwidth. The first two bandwidths were data-derived based on 
the procedure and software developed by Calonico et al. (2014a, 2014b), 
while the third used all sample observations in the analysis. Cattaneo et                                                                                                                                            
al. (2019) recommend using the MSE-optimal bandwidth for point 
estimates and either the MSE-optimal or CER-optimal bandwidth for 
confidence intervals. Using the MSE bandwidth for confidence intervals 
is valid, but using the CER bandwidth also minimizes coverage error.            
Hence, the discussion of the results is based on point estimates using the 
MSE-optimal bandwidth, and significance will be based on both MSE 
and CER bandwidths. The sample bandwidth estimates are reported to 
demonstrate robustness.
8 For full details of the Analytical Framework and Methodology of the Pantawid Pamilya, refer to                 
Orbeta et al. (2021, 2023). 
=== PAGE 289 ===
258
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Additionally, the tables show two sets of estimates for the three 
bandwidths. The analysis employed sharp and fuzzy RDD to estimate 
program impacts. In sharp RDD, there is perfect or near-perfect compliance 
with the treatment assignment, so the program impact is considered an 
intent-to-treat (ITT) effect. On the other hand, fuzzy RDD reports the 
treatment effects on the treated (TOT) or the effect of the program, taking 
compliance with the treatment assignment into account.
Impact on education
This section presents the estimates of program impact on education outcomes, 
including school enrollment and attendance and household investments in 
children’s education. Also discussed in this section is the program’s impact 
on child labor, as changes in school attendance are expected to influence                
the incidence and time spent on child labor.
The discussion of the program’s impact on education outcomes     
includes subgroup analyses for urban and rural areas and by sex of the child.           
In addition, the results include subgroup analysis based on the monitoring 
status of the child. In this study, there were enough number of children                              
ages 6–20 years who were monitored and not monitored, enabling a 
meaningful comparison of the program’s impact between these groups 
(Table 1). However, the table of estimates for the subgroup analyses is not 
presented here but can be found in the main report (Orbeta et al. 2021, 2023).

Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
Hypothesis: Pantawid Pamilya increases the school 
participation among children
Since school enrollment and attendance rates are among the program’s 
direct conditionalities, they are expected to be higher among beneficiary 
children. Testing this hypothesis will determine whether Pantawid Pamilya 
beneficiaries achieve better educational outcomes than their counterparts 
due to a higher valuation of education and increased school participation. 
Table 2 shows the estimated program impact on school enrollment 
by age group. The results indicate that the program increased enrollment 
rates for older children but not for younger children. A positive impact 
on enrollment rates was observed for children ages 12‒17 years (CER and 
sampling bandwidths). For the preferred MSE bandwidth, the results are                                     
not statistically significant, but the predicted proportions are comparable                                             
t o  t h e  C E R  e s t i m a t e s  ( 8 8 %  f o r  n o n b e n e fi c i a r i e s  v e r s u s  9 3 %  f o r  P a n t a w i d                                                                                                       
Pamilya beneficiaries). There is no significant difference in enrollment 
rates for elementary-age children, where the enrollment rate is relatively high

and 6‒14 years. In the fuzzy RD model, the enrollment rate among   
children ages 6‒14 years was statistically significant across all bandwidths,                                    
but the impact was very small (1 to 2 percentage points higher) because 
enrollment in this age group is almost universal, leaving little room for the 
program to marginally increase it. These results indicate that expanding 
the program to cover older children has led to increased school participation 
in this age group. 
Regarding attendance rates, no significant difference was observed 
between Pantawid and non-Pantawid children across all age groups, 
including preschool age, for both sharp and fuzzy RD models (Table 3). 
On average, the attendance rate is around 88 percent for children                                                                      
ages 3‒5 years, 97 percent for children ages 6‒11 years, 98 percent for 
children ages 12‒15 years, and 98 percent for children ages 16‒17 years. 
Likewise, no statistically significant difference was observed in the 
proportion of Pantawid and non-Pantawid children attending class at 
least 85 percent of the school days (Table 4). Despite the lack of impact                                                                                                     
on attendance rates, it is important to note that attendance rates for all age 
groups are already very high, even for older children who are expected 
to have a higher risk of dropping out of school. In the second impact 
evaluation, a positive impact on attendance for children ages 3‒5 years was 
identified; however, this result was not observed in this study.

When analyzing the results by urban and rural location, a statistically 
significant impact was observed on the attendance rate of children                         
ages 3‒5 years in rural areas (92% for Pantawid and 85% for non-Pantawid             
based on MSE bandwidth). In addition, a positive but smaller impact was 
observed for older children ages 16‒17 years in rural areas, where the 
Pantawid impact is 2 percentage points higher based on the sharp RD                                                                         
estimation. However, no statistically significant impact was observed using 
fuzzy RD for this outcome. For children ages 15‒20 years, Pantawid’s impact 
is a 2-percentage point increase in attendance rate (sharp and fuzzy RD 
estimations). In terms of the proportion of children who have at least                                                                                                            
85 percent attendance, an increase of 20 percentage points for sharp RD                                                
and 22 percentage points using fuzzy RD (MSE bandwidth) was noted for 
children ages 3‒5 years in rural areas. 
Meanwhile, the subgroup analysis by sex showed a disparity in the 
program’s impact on attendance rates between male and female children in 
the age groups 12‒15 and 6‒14 years. For both outcomes, using both sharp 
and fuzzy RD, a small positive impact was observed for female beneficiary 
children, while no impact was observed for male beneficiary children.
No statistically significant impact on attendance rates was observed  
for children ages 3‒5 and 6‒11 years, regardless of their program monitoring 
status, based on sharp and fuzzy RD estimations. For the 6‒14-year-old age 
group, attendance was higher for non-monitored children by 1‒2 percentage                         
points based on sharp and fuzzy RD estimates, respectively. However, the 
results for the 12‒15 and 16‒17 age groups are contradictory. The attendance 
rate of non-monitored children ages 12‒15 years is significantly higher 
statistically for Pantawid compared to non-Pantawid, while for children 
16‒17 years old, attendance rates are significantly lower statistically for 
non-monitored children, and no impact was observed among monitored  
children. This pattern is consistent across sharp and fuzzy models, although                 
a higher magnitude of impact is observed for fuzzy RD.
Hypothesis: Pantawid Pamilya participation results in 
improved education outcomes of children.
Testing this hypothesis determines whether Pantawid Pamilya beneficiaries 
have better educational outcomes than their counterparts due to a higher 
valuation of education and increased school participation (i.e., increased 
enrollment and attendance rates). In addition, this hypothesis explores

w h e n  t h e  f u l l  s a m p l e  i s  u s e d  f o r  b o t h  s h a r p  a n d  f u z z y  R D .  T h e s e  r e s u l t s                                                              
are somewhat consistent with earlier findings on increased enrollment of 
older children. In contrast, no program impact was found on dropout rates                
in the second wave of evaluation.
Subgroup analyses by urban and rural location show a statistically 
significant reduction in dropout rates for children ages 6‒14 and 12‒15 in 
rural areas. In contrast, no statistically significant impact on dropout rates 
was observed in urban areas. However, for older children ages 16‒17, a 
statistically significant reduction in dropout rates was observed in both 
urban and rural areas. The estimated impact is consistent between sharp 
and fuzzy models. 
In terms of the sex of the child, a 4-percentage point reduction in 
dropout rates was observed among female children ages 12‒17 using 
both sharp and fuzzy models. No statistically significant program impact 
on dropout rates was observed for male children in these age groups. 
Meanwhile, for younger children ages 6‒14, a reduction in dropout rates                
was observed among male children, while no statistically significant impact 
was observed among female children in the two models. These results 
suggest that the program keeps younger boys in school, but its impact 
dwindles as the children age. Conversely, the program has a stronger                                                                                              
effect on keeping girls in school as they grow older. This finding captures 
a missed opportunity for the program because boys are at greater risk of 
dropping out, especially as they age. For all age groups, male children 
consistently have higher dropout rates compared to female children. 
As with other educational indicators, the program’s impact on dropout 
rates was compared between monitored and non-monitored beneficiary 
children. The results showed a larger decrease in dropout rates among 
monitored beneficiary children across all age groups for both sharp and 
fuzzy RD estimations. In addition, the reduction in dropout rates becomes 
larger as the age group increases, possibly due to the higher baseline 
proportions of dropouts among older children.
The program’s impact on children’s participation in extracurricular 
activities was also analyzed. These activities include participation in 
academic, artistic, and/or athletic competitions and memberships in      
school clubs and organizations. This composite indicator reflects the                                              
=== PAGE 304 ===
273
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
child’s academic and athletic capabilities, willingness to participate and 
immerse in learning activities outside the classroom, and self-esteem. The 
results showed that a higher proportion of Pantawid Pamilya children 
participate in extracurricular activities. The participation rates of beneficiary 
children are significantly higher statistically by 5 percentage points based  
on sharp RD within the MSE bandwidth, and by 8 percentage points                                                                                                                                     
using fuzzy RD within the MSE bandwidth. There was no statistically 
significant impact on the number of activities participated in for both models. 
Subgroup analyses showed a positive program impact on 
extracurricular participation for beneficiary children who are in rural 
areas, male, and monitored in the program, using sharp and fuzzy RD models.                       
Based on the results, beneficiaries in rural areas are 6 percentage points 
more likely to participate in extracurricular activities compared to                     
non-Pantawid children in urban areas. A larger impact was observed using 
fuzzy RD, with a positive impact of 8 percentage points. By sex, program 
impact is only observed among male children for both participation in 
any extracurricular activities and the number of extracurricular activities 
participated in, with a larger impact using fuzzy RD. This result is likely 
due to lower baseline proportions among male children compared to                              
female children. Among non-Pantawid children, only 42 percent of males                                                                           
participate in extracurricular activities compared to 53 percent of females. 
Likewise, both sharp and fuzzy results observed an 8-percentage point 
increase in participation among child beneficiaries who are monitored                              
in school (MSE bandwidth). No impact was observed when comparing 
non-monitored Pantawid and non-Pantawid children.
Hypothesis: Pantawid Pamilya reduces the incidence and 
time spent on child labor
No statistically significant difference was observed for child labor, both                    
in terms of incidence and time spent on child labor (Table 7). This result              
was consistently observed in both the sharp and fuzzy RD models. 
The proportion of Pantawid and non-Pantawid children ages 10‒14 
who worked at least an hour in the past month does not significantly differ 
statistically within the MSE bandwidth, at 5.9 percent and 5.0 percent, 
respectively. The second wave of evaluation also found no statistically 
significant impact on work incidence for this age group but noted a higher 
percentage (12%) of both beneficiary and non-beneficiary children engaged 
in labor.

Despite the lack of program impact on child labor indicators, the 
survey data showed that children engaged in economic activities also 
attended school, as reported by the respondents. Of the children who worked 
at least one day in the past 12 months, 9 out of 10 were also enrolled in school 
at the same time. This result suggests that children are not dropping out 
of school despite concerns. This behavior may be motivated by the desire 
to generate additional income to supplement the cash grants covering 
education expenses.
The results of the subgroup analysis for urban and rural areas 
using both sharp and fuzzy RD models are interesting. In urban areas, a 
statistically significant increase in the proportion of children ages 10‒14 years   
engaged in paid and unpaid work was observed among beneficiaries,                                                  
with a 7-percentage point increase in sharp RD and a 6-percentage point 
increase in fuzzy RD MSE. In contrast, a statistically significant reduction 
in the proportion of child labor was observed in rural areas, with a 
6-percentage point decrease in sharp and fuzzy RD MSE. This finding 
supports the earlier hypothesis that children engage in child labor to 
supplement the cash grants, as the cost of education and living is generally

on the duration of child labor was observed in urban and rural areas.

Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
For comparison, the reported program impact on education expenditure                      
per school-aged child in the second wave of the evaluation was only                                                     
PHP 200. The mean spending on individual education components, such as 
school materials, allowances, and uniforms, was also higher for beneficiary 
children. However, these differences were not statistically significant, 
except for indications of a small impact on expenditures for uniforms.
The fuzzy RD estimation also found a statistically significant increase 
in the monthly education expenditures for Pantawid children compared                        
to non-Pantawid children, but this was observed only in the model using                      
the full sample of observations. Monthly expenditures on school materials                                  
and supplies are 6 percent higher for beneficiaries, while monthly 
expenditures on school uniforms and total average monthly expenditures are 
8 percent and 7 percent higher, respectively, compared to non-beneficiaries.
Results on monthly education expenditures in rural and urban areas 
are comparable for sharp and fuzzy RD models. Monthly expenditures on 
school materials and supplies are higher among Pantawid children compared  
to non-Pantawid children in rural areas. Expenditures on school uniforms                                 
are also higher for rural beneficiaries, but no statistically significant difference 
was observed in urban areas. Additionally, no statistically significant impact 
was observed for other education expenditure items, such as tuition and 
uniforms, in both urban and rural areas. Likewise, no statistically significant 
impact was observed on education expenditures by sex.
For monitored children, monthly expenditures on school materials 
and supplies were 12 percent higher for Pantawid children compared to 
non-beneficiaries. In addition, monthly expenditures on school uniforms 
were 11 percent higher, and average monthly total school expenditures 
were 13 percent higher for monitored Pantawid children. No statistically 
significant impact was observed for these indicators when comparing  
non-monitored Pantawid children with non-Pantawid children. These 
results are consistent with the findings in other education indicators, 
showing that monitored children benefit more from the program than 
other children in Pantawid Pamilya households.
=== PAGE 309 ===
278
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Ways forward
The evaluation findings generally indicate that the Pantawid Pamilya program  
still achieves most of its short- and medium-term objectives of encouraging 
children to enter and stay in school. However, despite the positive program 
impacts observed, some gaps need to be addressed, especially the minimal 
impact on education among children ages 3‒5 years and the persistent 
incidence of working children. Thus, the following recommendations                   
are proposed:
• Consider more effective and efficient ways of using the education grant.  
One option is to refocus education interventions on older  
children, where benefits are larger and the risk of dropping out of 
school is higher. There are several ways to do this, and they should 
be implemented gradually. One way is to remove the elementary 
education grant and the conditionality of school attendance,                                                                                         
replacing it with a reasonably attractive amount for grade-level 
completion and enrollment in the next level. The savings from not 
providing grants conditional on elementary school attendance 
could be reallocated to increase the education grants for high 
school children, still conditional on their enrollment and regular 
school attendance. A study by Barrera-Osorio et al. (2008) in 
the CSAE program in Colombia found that schemes providing 
bulk cash transfer benefits at the end of each grade level and 
cash transfer benefits conditional on the student’s graduation 
and subsequent enrollment in the next level have even stronger 
impacts on education outcomes than schemes that depend on 
monthly attendance. Corollary to this is the call for concentrating 
efforts on monitoring older children. 
• Pursue studies that analyze the program’s impact on learning.  It has 
been established in this and previous evaluation rounds that the 
program increases school attendance, except in the elementary 
grades where attendance rates are already near universal. The 
next important education outcome to consider is learning. 
This requires having the achievement test scores of beneficiary 
children and their counterparts. The program should endeavor 
to acquire these test scores from DepEd to understand the 
=== PAGE 310 ===
279
Assessing the Impact of the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program on Education
program’s impact on learning. This is important because the 
literature reveals that school attendance does not always translate 
into learning (World Bank 2018).  
• Find solutions to reduce child labor incidence and duration. Findings  
show that children continue to attend school despite being 
employed. This result indicates that the issue is not primarily 
driven by the beneficiaries’ behavior (such as lack of interest) but 
rather by the costs they incur to pursue schooling. The program 
needs to reassess the grant amount and examine the opportunity 
costs of forgoing child labor among Pantawid Pamilya children. 
With the declining real value of the grants, children might 
resort to work to support their schooling and/or contribute to 
household income. An interesting intervention to explore is 
whether increasing the education grants for high school children 
would produce a desirable impact on the incidence and duration of 
child labor. A notable result by Edmonds and Theoharides (2018) 
showed that asset transfers, while improving household welfare, 
actually increased—rather than reduced—child labor and boosted 
economic activity in the household.

Chapter 12
Nutrition Effects of 
DepEd’s School-Based 
Feeding Program
Jose Ramon G. Albert
=== PAGE 313 ===
282
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Introduction
The government, through the Department of Education (DepEd), has 
implemented school-based feeding programs (SBFPs) since 1997. The 
DepEd’s first SBFP, initially called the Breakfast Feeding Program (BFP), 
aimed to address short-term hunger. Over the years, the SBFP shifted its 
focus from addressing short-term hunger to addressing undernutrition 
among public primary pupils. The SBFP also underwent changes in 
target beneficiaries, coverage, and service delivery mode. This chapter 
summarizes the results of an impact evaluation (IE) study of the SBFP and its 
complementary activities in School Year (SY) 2013–2014 (Tabunda et al. 2016). 
In SY 2013–2014, the SBFP fed 40,361 severely wasted pupils enrolled                
in kindergarten to Grade 6 across 814 public elementary schools in the 
country. The feeding program was conducted for 100–120 feeding days, 
using a 20-day cycle of standardized recipes with malunggay. Each meal             
provided at least 300 calories, which is lower than the 876 calories per meal 
found in feeding programs elsewhere (Adelman et al. 2008). Furthermore,                                                                 
the SBFP offered fewer feeding days compared to the 180 feeding-day 
average in developing countries (Bundy et al. 2009).
The SBFP aims to (1) rehabilitate at least 70 percent of severely 
wasted1 beneficiaries to normal nutritional status by the end of the 
feeding days; (2) ensure 85 percent to 100 percent classroom attendance                                     
of beneficiaries; and (3) improve children’s health and nutrition values and 
behavior. According to the Health and Nutrition Center of DepEd, the 
primary goal of the SBFP is nutrition, while the other goals are secondary. 
To implement the SBFP, each school head establishes an SBFP core 
group (CG) and involves volunteer parents in meal preparation and feeding 
the children. The SBFP also devolves procurement and financial reporting 
responsibilities and is complemented by other DepEd programs such as 
deworming, the Gulayan sa Paaralan  Program (GPP), and the Essential 
Health Care Program (EHCP).
1 If the weight is below three standard deviations from the median weight-for-height, then a child is 
considered severely wasted. Meanwhile, if the weight-for-height is lower than two standard deviations 
from the growth standard but higher than three standard deviations, then a child is moderately wasted. 
The DepEd uses the World Health Organization (WHO) weight-for-age tables for preprimary school 
children ages 5 years and below and the WHO Body Mass Index–for-age tables for preprimary, primary,                
and secondary students ages 6–19 years in determining nutritional status.
=== PAGE 314 ===
283
Nutrition Effects of DepEd’s School-Based Feeding Program
In SY 2013–2014, the daily feeding budget for the SBFP was 
PHP 16 per beneficiary, covering both food (PHP 15 per beneficiary) and 
administration and monitoring expenses (PHP 1 per beneficiary). DepEd 
had a total budget of PHP 77.5 million for the SBFP in SY 2013–2014                                                             
to feed a fraction of the severely wasted pupils in public primary schools.                 
The number of SBFP beneficiaries (40,361) represented 7.2 percent of the 
more than half a million (562,262) severely wasted primary school students 
across the Philippines.
In SY 2014–2015, the SBFP had a budget of over a billion pesos, 
allowing DepEd to feed all severely wasted children in public primary 
schools. For SY 2015–2016, the SBFP budget more than doubled from 
the previous school year, targeting 532,752 severely wasted pupils and                                  
about half (627,403) of the number (1,312,935) of wasted pupils in public 
primary schools. With the expanded scale of the SBFP since SY 2014–2015,                                                                                          
an IE is essential to provide insights on how to implement the program                                                             
more effectively and efficiently. The IE focused on SBFP implementation                                   
in SY 2013–2014 rather than later school years since a counterfactual 
analysis—which asks what would have happened without the                                                            
program—becomes challenging in subsequent school years due to the 
absence of nonbeneficiary severely wasted pupils for comparison.
Allocations of the nearly 40,000 targeted beneficiaries in SY 2014–2015 
were determined from regions to divisions based on the prevalence of 
severe wasting, and then to schools based on prevalence and the capability 
of school heads. Furthermore, beneficiary schools were not to have existing 
feeding programs. The number of beneficiary pupils in a school varied                            
from 10 to 718. The allocation of beneficiaries was also disproportionate to 
population size across major islands, especially since several international 
and nongovernmental organizations have undertaken feeding programs 
in Mindanao, effectively restricting the participation of schools in                                    
that region. 
Study design and limitations
The study employed mixed methods research, undertaking quantitative 
surveys of students who were SBFP participants and matched nonparticipants 
(to generate a counterfactual analysis), as well as their parents, teachers, 
=== PAGE 315 ===
284
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
and school heads. Leaders or members of SBFP CGs in sampled beneficiary 
schools were also interviewed, along with one teacher from each sampled 
beneficiary school. Focus group discussions (FGDs) were conducted in five 
schools to probe program processes.
The study aimed to obtain a two-stage stratified random sample 
of 1,151 SY 2013–2014 beneficiary pupils. The first stage involved 
randomly selecting 44 beneficiary schools using information from DepED 
Memorandum 74 (s. 2013) as the sampling frame. The second stage 
c o n s i s t e d  o f  s a m p l i n g  b e n e fi c i a r y  p u p i l s  f r o m  t h e  l i s t  o f  c h i l d r e n  f e d ,                                                              
based on SY 2013–2014 SBFP forms, copies of which were obtained 
from school heads. For the first-stage sampling, beneficiary schools were        
proportionally stratified from a cross-classification of five region clusters 
and grouped by the number of beneficiary children in each school. 
Once a beneficiary school was selected, field teams were to interview 
school heads and request (1) copies of SY 2013–2014 nutrition status reports, 
SBFP forms, and addresses of beneficiary children, which served as the 
sampling frame for selecting beneficiary pupils; (2) the list of SBFP CG 
members or feeding coordinator(s); (3) the list of teachers of beneficiary 
pupils; and (4) the list of SBFP volunteer parents. 
The study sought to match each randomly selected severely wasted 
beneficiary with a severely wasted pupil of the same sex and age (in years) 
from a public primary school, preferably within the same municipality,               
who was not a beneficiary of the SBFP in SY 2013–2014. Both beneficiary                 
and nonbeneficiary pupils were randomly chosen using systematic sampling 
from each school’s list of severely wasted pupils, ordered by grade level. 
The study faced several limitations due to fieldwork challenges, 
including difficulties in obtaining complete documentation from schools and 
finding matched nonbeneficiaries. The expanded coverage in SY 2014–2015 
added complexity, as about a third of the matched nonbeneficiary pupils 
from SY 2013–2014 became beneficiaries in SY 2014–2015. Furthermore, 
the analyses could not include survey weights for the sampled students. 
Several schools could not provide the study team with nutrition status             
reports or SBFP forms, or they provided incompletely filled-out documents. 
Thus, the actual number of severely wasted beneficiary pupils fed in                                                
SY 2013–2014 could not be obtained and/or verified. Consequently,                                    
survey weights could not be accurately computed. Tabunda et al. (2016) 
detail the challenges and limitations of the study.
=== PAGE 316 ===
285
Nutrition Effects of DepEd’s School-Based Feeding Program
Profile of sampled beneficiary schools, beneficiary 
families, and beneficiary students
Of the 44 sampled beneficiary schools, 30 are located in rural barangays, 
while nearly half are located in first-class-income cities or municipalities. 
Twelve school heads reported that in SY 2013–2014, they fed wasted pupils, 
not just severely wasted pupils. Forty schools conducted feeding for                                                                      
120 days, while the other four schools did so for 100 days. All SBFP CG 
leaders/members or feeding coordinators said that they used DepEd’s 
malunggay recipes, with most reporting that the children liked the meals.  
All but five schools had a vegetable garden, and all but two implemented 
EHCP. Two-thirds of the schools implemented SBFP for the first time                           
in SY 2013–2014, while a quarter (11 schools) had also implemented SBFP             
in the previous school year. However, DepEd Order 87 (s. 2012) and 
DepED Memorandum 74 (s. 2013) suggest that only seven schools were 
repeat beneficiaries. 
Of the 14 schools that implemented SBFP prior to SY 2013–2014,                   
11 had repeat beneficiary pupils. For nine of these schools, the number 
of repeat beneficiary pupils ranged from 1 to 28,  while two schools had 
100 and 134 repeat beneficiaries. When asked about the reasons for repeat 
beneficiaries, four school heads said that the children did not attain normal 
nutrition status at the end of the previous SBFP. Nine other school heads 
reported that their repeat beneficiary pupils had attained normal nutrition 
status at the end of the previous SBFP but reverted to severely wasted or 
wasted status at the start of SY 2013–2014. 
The majority (67%) of the 1,081 families of the sampled beneficiary 
children reside in rural barangays. The family sizes ranged from 2 to 16 members. 
Most families (96%) eat at least three meals a day. A majority (65%) have 
access to a safe water supply, while the rest source their water from dug              
wells (10%), natural bodies of water (11%), and peddlers (15%). Nearly half 
(48%) were CCT beneficiaries both in SY 2013–2014 and at the time of the 
interview. About 15 percent reported having OFW relatives who send them 
money. About three-quarters of the parents/guardians said their families 
planted or had their own vegetable gardens. Furthermore, nearly 9 in 10 
said that the child beneficiaries had been eating malunggay even before the 
school feeding program, while about 1 in 10 reported otherwise. 
=== PAGE 317 ===
286
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Regarding the beneficiary children, male pupils (56%) slightly 
outnumber female pupils. At the time of the survey period, the children 
ranged in age from four to 19 years, with both the mean and median age 
being about 10 years. About a third of the children were at most eight years 
old. Their parents reported that more than a third (38%) of the children 
were repeat beneficiaries by SY 2013–2014 implementation, and about the 
same percentage (36%) were beneficiaries in SY 2014–2015. Meanwhile, 
1 4  p e r c e n t  w e r e  S B F P  b e n e fi c i a r i e s  f o r  t h r e e  c o n s e c u t i v e  s c h o o l  y e a r s :                                                                   
SY 2012–2013, SY 2013–2014, and SY 2014–2015. 
A study by Tabunda et al. (2016) explains the repeat-beneficiary 
phenomenon with a logistic regression based on the specific identities of 
the beneficiary schools, and an interaction variable for rural residence and 
family size as explanatory variables. The study found that 12 schools are more 
likely to have thrice-repeating beneficiaries than other schools. Further, in 
a school with a huge percentage of repeat severely wasted beneficiaries, 
severely wasted children from large rural families are more likely to be 
repeat beneficiaries. 
Main findings
Similar to the findings of the process evaluation study on the SBFP by 
Albert et al. (2015), the study observed inconsistencies between the verbal               
descriptors for nutrition status recorded in SBFP forms and nutrition 
status reports, and in the verified prefeeding and postfeeding nutrition 
status computed from birthdates and weight and height measurements also 
recorded therein. To determine the postfeeding nutrition status of more 
children, missing postfeeding dates were imputed using either the latest 
postfeeding date recorded for children in the same school or the date of                
the last feeding day mentioned by the school head.
Assessment of SBFP nutrition goal
In SY 2013–2014, about 62 percent of severely wasted beneficiaries 
attained at least normal nutrition status at the end of the feeding program 
(Table 1), compared to the SBFP nutrition goal target of 70 percent.  
=== PAGE 318 ===
287
Nutrition Effects of DepEd’s School-Based Feeding Program
Further, about 70 percent of the children who were verified as wasted 
before the start of the feeding program attained normal status by the end. 2 
However, 10 percent of the children who were verified as having normal 
nutrition status before the start of the feeding program regressed to wasted 
or severely wasted status by the end.
Interestingly, 62 percent of parents interviewed said that their child 
attained normal weight for height at the end of the SBFP; 19 percent said 
that their child did not; and the remaining 19 percent could not recall if 
their child attained normal weight or body mass index at the end of the 
feeding program.
The attainment of the nutrition goal depends not only on how 
well the SBFP was implemented but also on factors beyond the control 
of program implementers, such as the characteristics and practices of 
beneficiary families, parents/guardians, and the children themselves. A 
logistic regression to explain improvement from severely wasted to normal

Attainment of SBFP attendance goal
Of the approximately 200 SBFP pupil beneficiaries verified to be severely 
wasted before the start of the feeding program and who had school attendance 
data, only about 3 percent attended school for less than 85 percent of the 
total school days. The median percentage attendance for severely wasted 
children is 97.5 percent. The school attendance of nonbeneficiary pupils                     
is comparable, with 95 percent of them attending 85 percent of the total 
school days.
Assessment of complementary programs
Nearly all beneficiary schools implemented the GPP and EHCP. Some 
parents mentioned that when their child’s school sometimes lacked food,  
the feeding implementers supplemented meals with vegetables from the 
school garden.

Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
Children were taught the importance of good grooming, washing 
hands before and after meals, brushing their teeth, and maintaining good 
nutrition. Nearly all children said that they continue to wash their hands 
before and after eating, both at school and at home. However, only                                  
69 percent of field interviewers found the children to be well-groomed at 
the time of the interview.
Sustaining nutrition gains
The nutritional gains of the program are not sustained in the case of 
many severely wasted beneficiaries a year or more after the feeding. Of 
the 179 severely wasted beneficiaries whose nutrition status improved to 
normal by the end of the feeding program and who had consistent height 
measurements for the prefeeding and survey periods, about half (48%)                                                                                                                                            
remained normal by the time of the survey. However, some regressed                                  
to wasted or severely wasted a year or more later. This result suggests                     
the need to continue feeding most severely wasted beneficiaries beyond 
the 100–120-day feeding cycle while also introducing other government 
interventions (not necessarily administered by DepEd) to help disadvantaged 
families meet their nutritional needs.
Other results
Field interviews and FGDs suggest that the SY 2013–2014 SBFP program 
was generally well-implemented, with most school heads, teachers, and                 
parents expressing appreciation for the SBFP and with sizable percentages               
of heads and teachers wanting the SBFP to continue and expand. The program 
also promoted a culture of care and active participation among stakeholders.
The SBFP was found to be helping improve the attentiveness and 
sociability of beneficiary pupils. Teachers reported that most beneficiaries 
enrolled in their classes showed improvement in attentiveness during     
feeding (96%) as well as after (95%). The children also became more sociable 
during the feeding (97%), and this development was sustained after the    
feeding (96%). Additionally, teachers reported improved class attendance for 
94 percent of beneficiaries, with 92 percent maintaining good attendance.
=== PAGE 322 ===
291
Nutrition Effects of DepEd’s School-Based Feeding Program
Ways forward
Policy implications
For improved program implementation, DepEd should provide all schools 
with recommended weighing and height-measurement scales, rather 
than leaving the procurement of such instruments to the resourcefulness 
of school heads. All schools need standardized equipment, as nonbeneficiary                         
schools also need to submit accurate nutrition status reports, which serve as  
the basis for determining beneficiary schools.
School heads, school nurses, and class advisers, if not all teachers, 
should be trained on the proper use of these scales and the importance of 
proper documentation of the prefeeding, feeding, and postfeeding phases                                
of the program. This training will help in the proper selection of beneficiary 
schools and beneficiary pupils, as well as in monitoring and evaluating 
program outcomes. 
The basis for the 70 percent figure in the SBFP nutrition goal must                 
be reviewed regularly, as 10 percent of normal and 30 percent of wasted 
children can regress to wasted or severely wasted status, possibly due to 
severe illness or growth spurts. About 18 percent of sampled beneficiaries 
were reported to have suffered severe illness lasting at least three days in 
SY 2013–2014. The SBFP nutrition target has since been increased to                                                             
80 percent in SY 2015–2016, possibly because wasted children were 
included in the program’s coverage. However, available data indicates                          
that only about 70 percent of wasted beneficiaries attain normal nutrition 
status by the end of the program.
Given that the administrative component of the budget has 
increased since SY 2013–2014, it is also important to increase the food 
budget allocation and consider inflation-adjusted increases.
While malnutrition is largely an economic issue, poor nutrition 
persists across socioeconomic classes. Malnutrition often starts among 
children under five and continues into early childhood, affecting learning 
achievements. While the IE shows positive impacts of the SBFP on children 
fed, there are concerns about sustained nutrition effects. The government 
should provide sufficient budgets to DepEd for the program to feed 
beneficiaries for the entire school year to sustain nutrition gains. The SBFP 
is currently implemented for 60 days, just a quarter of the entire school 
days. The government will also need to complement the SBFP with other                
=== PAGE 323 ===
292
Making Philippine Education Reforms and Interventions More Transformative
programs to address malnutrition in children under 5, especially in light of  
the country’s commitment to the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development                       
and the Sustainable Development Goals, to ensure that no child is left 
behind in both schooling and nutrition.

293
The Authors
Dr. Aniceto C. Orbeta Jr. served as President of the Philippine Institute  
for Development Studies (PIDS) from 2021 to 2025, following his 
tenure as a Senior Research Fellow from 1992 to 2021. He holds a PhD 
in Economics from the University of the Philippines (UP) School of 
Economics and completed his postdoctoral studies at Harvard University. 
His areas of expertise include applied economic modeling, impact evaluation, 
education and labor markets, social protection, demographic economics, 
and information and communication technologies.
Dr. Michael R.M. Abrigo  is a senior research fellow at PIDS. Prior to 
this, he was a Postdoctoral Research Fellow at the East-West Center in 
Honolulu, Hawai’i. He holds a PhD in Economics from the University of 
Hawai’i at Mānoa and earned his Master of Statistics and BA in Development 
Studies (magna cum laude) from the University of the Philippines. His areas 
of specialization include population, education, health, and nutrition policy.
Dr. Vicente B. Paqueo is a distinguished visiting research fellow at PIDS.           
He previously served as lead economist at the World Bank in Washington, 
DC, and as a professor at the UP School of Economics. He holds a PhD 
in Economics from the University of the Philippines and completed his 
postdoctoral studies at Princeton University. His areas of specialization 
include education, social protection, and human development.
Dr. Jose Ramon G. Albert  is a senior research fellow at PIDS. He   
previously served as the chief statistician of the Philippines and as 
secretary-general of the former National Statistical Coordination Board. 
He holds a PhD in Statistics from the State University of New York at 
Stony Brook. His areas of expertise include poverty measurement and 
diagnostics, agricultural statistics, survey design, data mining, and the 
statistical analysis of missing data.
Ms. Nina V. Araos is a former research analyst at PIDS and currently 
works as a monitoring and evaluation specialist for Lunas Collective. She 
holds a master’s degree in international relations from Waseda University. 
Her research specializations include gender and development, education 
and labor market issues, maternal and child health, and social protection.
Dr. Connie G. Bayudan-Dacuycuy  is a senior research fellow at PIDS. 
She earned her bachelor’s and master’s degrees in economics from the UP 
and completed her PhD in Economics at Kyoto University in Japan. Her 
=== PAGE 325 ===
294
areas of specialization include household, family, and gender issues; poverty 
analysis; and applied economic modeling. Prior to joining PIDS, she was 
an assistant professor in the Economics Department at Ateneo de Manila 
University and worked as a consultant for the Asian Development Bank. 
In 2019, she also served as the national expert for the Philippines in a 
research project sponsored by the Asian Productivity Organization, which 
focused on developing human resources for future industries.
Ms. Karen Dominique Brillantes is a development professional with 
over a decade of experience in research and project management across 
various sectors, including education, financial inclusion and microfinance, 
governance, and youth development. She recently served with the Global 
Anti-Corruption team of the United Nations Development Programme 
(UNDP) in Norway and currently manages a joint program for UNDP 
Philippines. Karen holds a master’s degree in development economics from 
the University of the Philippines.
Ms. Erlinda M. Capones is a former Director at the National Economic 
and Development Authority. She earned her master’s degree in economics 
from Thammasat University in Bangkok, Thailand. Her professional 
experience centers on various aspects of social development planning               
and policy.
Dr. Lawrence B. Dacuycuy  is a full professor and university fellow at 
De La Salle University. He earned his bachelor’s and master’s degrees in 
economics from the University of the Philippines and completed his PhD 
in Economics at Kyoto University in Japan. His areas of expertise include 
econometrics, macroeconomic modeling, labor economics, and social 
mobility. As a recipient of the 2009 Outstanding Young Scientist Award 
from the National Academy of Science and Technology, he served as chair 
of the Social Sciences Division of the National Research Council of the 
Philippines from 2022 to 2024. He remains an active contributor to national 
policy discussions on labor markets and economic development.
Dr. Kris A. Francisco is a senior research fellow at PIDS. She holds a PhD 
in Public Economics from the National Graduate Institute for Policy Studies 
(GRIPS) in Tokyo, Japan. Her areas of specialization include development 
economics, applied econometric analysis, and transportation economics.
Ms. Justine Beatrice B. Jovellanos was a research assistant for education 
at PIDS. She holds a bachelor’s degree from Ateneo de Manila University and 
has professional experience in the fields of education and transportation.
=== PAGE 326 ===
295
Ms. Marites B. Lagarto previously served as an independent 
consultant at PIDS. She holds a master’s degree in community nutrition                                 
from the University of Queensland in Australia and specializes in process 
evaluation, local governance, and social development issues related to 
public health and nutrition.
Ms. Kris Ann M. Melad is a supervising research specialist at PIDS. 
She holds a master’s degree in development economics from the UP School 
of Economics. Her current research focuses on social protection, human 
capital development, and program evaluation.
Ms. Jennifer D. Monje  is an education consultant at PIDS and an 
associate professor at Pamantasan ng Lungsod ng Maynila (University of 
the City of Manila). She holds master’s degrees in comparative literature 
from the University of the Philippines Diliman and in second language 
studies from the University of Hawai’i at Mānoa. Her research focuses on 
linguistic landscapes, language policy, and second language learning, with 
several publications in these areas.
Dr. Adoracion M. Navarro  is a senior research fellow at PIDS. She 
holds a PhD and MA in Economics from the University of the Philippines 
Diliman and an MPA in Economic Policy Management from Columbia 
University in New York. Her research interests include energy and 
other infrastructure sectors, public-private partnerships, and regional 
(subnational) development.
Ms. Danica Aisa P. Ortiz  is a former supervising research specialist 
at PIDS. She earned her master’s degree in public policy from GRIPS in 
Tokyo, Japan. Her research work has focused on health, education, and 
local governance.
Ms. Ma. Kristina P. Ortiz is a supervising legislative staff officer at the 
Congressional Policy and Budget Research Department (CPBRD) of the 
House of Representatives. Prior to joining CPBRD, she spent a decade as 
a policy researcher at PIDS. She holds a bachelor’s degree in economics 
from the UP School of Economics, a master’s degree in Statistics from the 
UP School of Statistics, and a second master’s degree in public policy from 
GRIPS in Tokyo, Japan. Her research portfolio covers regulatory reform, 
logistics and infrastructure, ASEAN economic integration, and education 
policy, with her current work focusing on labor and employment issues, 
including minimum wage and productivity analysis.
=== PAGE 327 ===
296
Ms. Maropsil V. Potestad was a research analyst at PIDS. She obtained             
her bachelor’s degree in economics from UP Los Baños. Her research                    
focused on education, labor markets, and geographic information systems.
Dr. Celia M. Reyes served as PIDS president from 2018 to 2021. 
An expert in econometrics, she has conducted and published extensive 
research on poverty assessment and the evaluation of social protection                    
programs. She also served as the network leader of the Community-Based 
Monitoring System. Dr. Reyes holds a PhD in Economics from the 
University of Pennsylvania.
Ms. Denise Silfverberg was a consultant at PIDS. She holds an MA in 
Economics from the University of Edinburgh and an MSc in Development 
Economics from the University of Amsterdam. As a development 
economics researcher, she has provided consulting services to various 
government agencies and development banks, with a focus on human 
development, particularly in the areas of health and education.
